# Optimal Quantum de Finetti Theorems via Argmax Rounding

**arXiv ID**: 2608.02590v1
**Authors**: Fernando Granha Jeronimo, Pei Wu, Haochen Xu
**Published**: 2026-08-03
**Categories**: quant-ph, cs.CC
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.02590v1

## Abstract

We prove optimal finite quantum de Finetti upper bounds. Given a bosonic state $ρ_N\in D(\mathrm{Sym}^N(\mathbb C^d))$, there is a probability measure $ν$ on the unit sphere such that \[   \left\|   ρ_N^{(2)}-\int |u\rangle\langle u|^{\otimes 2}\,dν(u)   \right\|_1   \le \frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{N-1}. \] By purification, the bosonic theorem also gives the optimal $O(d/N)$ upper bound for arbitrary exchangeable states. These results settle the dimension dependence left open by Christandl, König, Mitchison, and Renner (CMP 2007). The proof casts de Finetti approximation as sum-of-squares rounding and applies the argmax method of Jeronimo, Wu, and Xu (manuscript 2026).   More generally, $t$-site marginals satisfy $O(t\sqrt d/N)$ bosonic and $O(td/N)$ permutation-invariant bounds. Our proof formulates de Finetti approximation as the integrality gap of a symmetric-extension semidefinite program and rounds an optimum by the argmax principle. The sharp bounds have several consequences. For every fixed $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, we construct a channel with input dimension $D=\exp(O_\varepsilon(\sqrt d\log d))=\exp(o(d))$ whose outputs are $\varepsilon$-close to separable states of local dimension $d$ and whose image contains every such separable state, thereby refuting Watrous's disentangler conjecture. We also obtain deterministic $\exp(\widetilde O(\sqrt d/\varepsilon))$-time algorithms for explicit Best Separable State without perfect completeness and for trace-distance separability testing.   Finally, spectral truncation gives the first dimension-free bosonic de Finetti theorem in Hilbert--Schmidt distance, with the optimal rate $Θ(N^{-1/2})$ when the dimension may grow.

## Optimal Quantum de Finetti Theorems via Argmax RoundingFernando Granha JeronimoUniversity of Illinois Urbana–Champaign.
Email:granha@illinois.eduPei WuThe Pennsylvania State University.
Email:pei.wu@psu.eduHaochen XuThe Pennsylvania State University.
Email:hpx5065@psu.edu

## Abstract

We prove optimal finite quantum de Finetti upper bounds. Given a bosonic stateρN∈D​(SymN⁡(ℂd))\rho_{N}\in\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathbb{C}^{d})), there is a probability measureν\nuon
the unit sphere such that∥ρN(2)−∫‖u‖=1|u⟩⟨u|⊗2dν(u)∥1≤d−1N−1.\left\|\rho_{N}^{(2)}-\int_{\left\lVert u\right\rVert=1}\lvert u\rangle\langle u\rvert^{\otimes 2}\,d\nu(u)\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{N-1}.

By purification, the bosonic theorem also gives the optimalO​(d/N)O(d/N)upper bound for arbitrary exchangeable states. These results settle
the dimension dependence left open by Christandl, König,
Mitchison, and Renner (CMP 2007). The proof casts de Finetti approximation
as sum-of-squares rounding and applies the argmax method of Jeronimo, Wu,
and Xu (manuscript 2026).

Among the consequences of the optimal bounds, we record three advances
on long-standing problems in quantum complexity that have resisted
progress for decades.
- 1.

We refute Watrous’s disentangler conjecture. A disentangler is a
quantum channel whose outputs are all close to separable states and
whose image approximates every separable state. For every fixedε∈(0,1)\varepsilon\in(0,1), we construct such a channel with exact coverage,
that is, an(ε,0)(\varepsilon,0)-disentanglerD​(ℂD)→D​(ℂd⊗ℂd)\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{D})\to\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d})withD=exp⁡(Oε​(d​log⁡d))=exp⁡(o​(d)).D=\exp\bigl(O_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{d}\log d)\bigr)=\exp(o(d)).

The conjecture, recorded by Aaronson, Beigi, Drucker, Fefferman, and Shor
(CCC 2008), predicted instead that every constant-error disentangler must
haveD=exp⁡(Ω​(d))D=\exp(\Omega(d)).
- 2.

We give the first algorithm for general explicit Best Separable
State with arbitrary completeness and soundness parameters whose running
time is subexponential in the local dimension: at every fixed accuracy,
it runs in timeexp⁡(O~​(d))\exp(\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{d})). This removes the
perfect-completeness assumption from the earlier algorithm of Barak,
Kothari, and Steurer (STOC 2017), which achieved such a running time
only in that case.
- 3.

We give the first algorithm for trace-norm separability testing of
an explicitly given state whose running time is subexponential in the
local dimension: at every fixed additive accuracy, it runs in timeexp⁡(O~​(d))\exp(\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{d})).
Computational separability has been studied since at least the work of
Gurvits (STOC 2003), yet no such algorithm was previously known for
trace-norm testing.

We also prove the first dimension-free bosonic quantum de Finetti theorem
in Hilbert–Schmidt norm, with upper boundO​(N−1/2)O(N^{-1/2}).
This dimension-free result follows from our
optimal trace-norm theorem by spectral truncation and recovers the
dimension-free classical phenomenon of Diaconis and
Freedman (The Annals of Probability1980), which is impossible in
quantum trace norm.

## 1Introduction and main results

## 1.1Background

The quantum de Finetti theorem is one of the fundamental structural
results in quantum information. It gives a quantitative expression of
the monogamy of entanglement: highly symmetric quantum systems cannot
share much entanglement among many subsystems. The theorem captures
this principle by showing that the marginal on any fixed number of
sites approaches a mixture of tensor-power states as the total number
of sites grows.

The standard finite bounds make this statement quantitative. LetρN∈D​((ℂd)⊗N)\rho_{N}\in\mathrm{D}((\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes N})be permutation invariant, and writeρN(t)\rho_{N}^{(t)}for itstt-site marginal. The quantum de Finetti
theorem of Christandl, König, Mitchison, and
Renner[CKMR07, Theorems II.7 and II.8]states that, for every1≤t≤N1\leq t\leq N, there is a
probability measureμ\muonD​(ℂd)\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{d})such that12​‖ρN(t)−∫D​(ℂd)σ⊗t​𝑑μ​(σ)‖1≤2​d2​tN.\frac{1}{2}\left\|\rho_{N}^{(t)}-\int_{\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{d})}\sigma^{\otimes t}\,d\mu(\sigma)\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{2d^{2}t}{N}.

IfρN\rho_{N}is supported onSymN⁡(ℂd)\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathbb{C}^{d}), there is instead a
probability measureν\nuon the unit sphere ofℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}such that12∥ρN(t)−∫‖u‖=1|u⟩⟨u|⊗tdν(u)∥1≤2​d​tN.\frac{1}{2}\left\|\rho_{N}^{(t)}-\int_{\left\lVert u\right\rVert=1}\lvert u\rangle\langle u\rvert^{\otimes t}\,d\nu(u)\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{2dt}{N}.

The infinite quantum de Finetti theorem goes back to Størmer and to
Hudson and Moody[Stø69,HM76]; Caves, Fuchs, and Schack later
recast it in quantum-information language[CFS02]. Finite
quantitative versions developed in the early 2000s, with the work of
Christandl, König, Mitchison, and Renner forming a culmination of the
theory of approximation by mixtures of exact tensor
powers[KR05,CKMR07]. In a complementary direction, Renner’s
exponential de Finetti theorem obtained exponentially small error by
allowing almost-product states, with applications to quantum
cryptography[Ren05,Ren07].

In quantum complexity, finite de Finetti bounds are a basic tool for
analyzing SDP hierarchies based on symmetric extensions: they certify
convergence to separable or product states and thereby yield algorithms
for separability membership and product-state
optimization[DPS04,NOP09,BCY11b,BH13,FF21]. They also
enter the study of QMA with multiple unentangled proofs and of
disentangler constructions[KMY09,ABD+08,BH13,LS15,HNW19,JLW26].

An important refinement changes the metric so that the approximation
need only fool a restricted class of measurements. Brandão,
Christandl, and Yard obtained an improved de Finetti-type bound with
only logarithmic local-dimension dependence under one-way LOCC
measurements, yielding quasipolynomial separability algorithms for the
one-way LOCC and Hilbert–Schmidt norms[BCY11a,BCY11b].
Brandão and Christandl extended these quantitative
symmetric-extension bounds to the multipartite setting[BC12].
Brandão and Harrow subsequently proved
stronger de Finetti bounds under local measurements, with applications
to quantum complexity and polynomial optimization[BH13]. Li and
Smith extended this theory to fully adaptive one-way LOCC measurements;
among other consequences, they show that polynomially many unentangled
proofs add no power to QMA when the verifier is restricted to one-way
LOCC[LS15]. Such results are closely related to distinguishability
norms and quantum data hiding[MWW09,BCY11a,BH13,LS15].

The quantitative content of a finite de Finetti theorem is its
convergence rate. Classically, thett-variable marginal of an
exchangeable sequence of lengthNNis withinO​(t2/N)O(t^{2}/N)in total
variation of a mixture of i.i.d. laws, independently of the size of
the sample space[DF80]. Quantum trace-norm bounds necessarily
depend on the local Hilbert-space dimension, but the correct dependence
was left open. Christandl, König, Mitchison, and Renner devoted a
section to optimality, asked whether their bounds could be improved,
and concluded that the dimension dependence was
unclear[CKMR07, Secs. II.C and V]. For bosonic states onSymN⁡(ℂd)\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathbb{C}^{d}), the standard constructive theorem gives errorO​(d/N)O(d/N)for two-site marginals[CKMR07,Chi11,LNR15]; Lewin, Nam, and
Rougerie later singled out improving thisdd-dependence as an open
problem[LNR15, Remark 2.2].

We settle this question by proving optimal trace-norm upper bounds. The
main result improves the two-site bosonic error fromO​(d/N)O(d/N)toO​(d/N)O(\sqrt{d}/N), and purification gives anO​(d/N)O(d/N)upper bound for
arbitrary exchangeable states.
The square-root improvement is exactly what moves several
quantum-complexity problems from exponential to subexponential
dependence on the local dimension.

The sharp bosonic theorem is the engine behind the paper, but it is not
the end of the development. We next prove a de Finetti theorem for
two-sided Bose-symmetric(n,m)(n,m)-extendible states: every such
bipartite state onA⊗BA\otimes Bis withinO​(min⁡{dA,dB}n​m)O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}}{nm}}\right)

in trace distance of the separable states. For balanced extensions
with equal local dimensions, this becomesO​(d/n)O(\sqrt{d}/n). The
two-sided theorem provides a common interface for three complexity
applications.
Realizing the two-sided relaxation as the image of a quantum channel
gives a subexponential disentangler. Optimizing an accepting operator
over the same relaxation gives the subexponential Best Separable State
algorithm, and the Harrow–Montanaro reduction then yields trace-norm
separability testing[HM13].

The optimal bosonic trace-norm theorem is also the starting point for
the first dimension-free bosonic de Finetti theorem in
Hilbert–Schmidt norm, with upper boundO​(N−1/2)O(N^{-1/2}). We also obtain
trace-norm bounds for higher marginals, with errorO​(t​d/N)O(t\sqrt{d}/N)for
bosonic states andO​(t​d/N)O(td/N)for exchangeable states. We state these
results precisely next.

## 1.2Our results

## 1.2.1Optimal trace-norm de Finetti

Letℋ=ℂd\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{C}^{d}. We use the following notations for trace distanceDtr​(ρ,σ):=12​‖ρ−σ‖1,Dtr​(ρ,𝒦):=infτ∈𝒦Dtr​(ρ,τ).D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\sigma):=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\rho-\sigma\right\rVert_{1},\qquad D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\mathcal{K}):=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{K}}D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\tau).

A state inD​(SymN⁡ℋ)\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{Sym}^{N}\mathcal{H})isbosonic, while a state onℋ⊗N\mathcal{H}^{\otimes N}isexchangeableif it is permutation invariant.
For either type, we writeρN(t)\rho_{N}^{(t)}for thett-site marginal.

For2≤t≤N2\leq t\leq N, define the Hartree mixtures and tensor-power
mixtures bySEPH(t)⁡(d)\displaystyle\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d):=conv{|u⟩⟨u|⊗t:u∈ℂd,∥u∥=1},\displaystyle:=\operatorname{conv}\{\lvert u\rangle\langle u\rvert^{\otimes t}:u\in\mathbb{C}^{d},\ \left\lVert u\right\rVert=1\},(1.1)SEPP(t)⁡(d)\displaystyle\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(d):=conv⁡{σ⊗t:σ∈D​(ℂd)}.\displaystyle:=\operatorname{conv}\{\sigma^{\otimes t}:\sigma\in\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{d})\}.(1.2)

The subscriptsH\mathrm{H}andP\mathrm{P}stand for Hartree and
tensor power, respectively. The sets oftt-site marginals ofNN-site bosonic and exchangeable states areBOSN(t)⁡(d)\displaystyle\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(t)}(d):={ρN(t):ρN∈D​(SymN⁡(ℂd))},\displaystyle:=\left\{\rho_{N}^{(t)}:\rho_{N}\in\mathrm{D}\bigl(\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathbb{C}^{d})\bigr)\right\},(1.3)EXCHN(t)⁡(d)\displaystyle\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(t)}(d):={σN(t):σN∈D​((ℂd)⊗N),Uπ​σN​Uπ†=σNfor every​π∈𝔖N}.\displaystyle:=\left\{\sigma_{N}^{(t)}:\begin{array}[]{l}\sigma_{N}\in\mathrm{D}((\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes N}),\\[-2.0pt]
U_{\pi}\sigma_{N}U_{\pi}^{\dagger}=\sigma_{N}\quad\text{for every }\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}\end{array}\right\}.(1.6)

Every Hartree mixture has anNN-site bosonic extension, and every
tensor-power mixture has anNN-site exchangeable extension. Thus
both marginal sets are outer relaxations:SEPH(t)⁡(d)⊆BOSN(t)⁡(d),SEPP(t)⁡(d)⊆EXCHN(t)⁡(d).\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d)\subseteq\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(t)}(d),\qquad\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(d)\subseteq\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(t)}(d).

For the nested pairℒ⊆𝒦\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{K}below,DHtr​(𝒦,ℒ):=supρ∈𝒦Dtr​(ρ,ℒ)D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathcal{K},\,\mathcal{L}):=\sup_{\rho\in\mathcal{K}}D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\mathcal{L})denotes
the worst-case trace distance from a state in𝒦\mathcal{K}toℒ\mathcal{L}. We call this theworst-case de Finetti error.

## Theorem 1.1(Bosonic two-site de Finetti upper bound;
cf. Theorem3.1).

For everyN,d≥2N,d\geq 2,DHtr​(BOSN(2)⁡(d),SEPH(2)⁡(d))≤d−12​(N−1).D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(2)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)\Bigr)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{2(N-1)}.

## Theorem 1.2(Exchangeable two-site de Finetti upper bound;
cf. Corollary3.7).

For everyN,d≥2N,d\geq 2,DHtr​(EXCHN(2)⁡(d),SEPP(2)⁡(d))≤d2−12​(N−1).D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(2)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(2)}(d)\Bigr)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d^{2}-1}}{2(N-1)}.

The exchangeable upper bound follows by applying the standard
purification and then using the bosonic theorem with dimensiond2d^{2};
the rectangular Werner examples of Christandl, König, Mitchison,
and Renner give the matchingΩ​(d/N)\Omega(d/N)lower bound, and their
symmetric purifications give the matchingΩ​(d/N)\Omega(\sqrt{d}/N)bosonic lower bound[CKMR07, Lemma II.5 and Corollary III.9].

## Higher marginals.

The two-site upper-bound dependence onNNandddpersists for
arbitrarytt, with only a linear loss intt.
The caset=2t=2is covered by the preceding two-site theorems; the
higher-marginal argument treatst≥3t\geq 3.

## Theorem 1.3(tt-site de Finetti upper bounds;
cf. Theorem4.1).

For everyd≥2d\geq 2and2≤t≤N2\leq t\leq N,DHtr​(BOSN(t)⁡(d),SEPH(t)⁡(d))\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(t)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d)\Bigr)≤4​t​d−1N,\displaystyle\leq\frac{4t\sqrt{d-1}}{N},DHtr​(EXCHN(t)⁡(d),SEPP(t)⁡(d))\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(t)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(d)\Bigr)≤4​t​d2−1N.\displaystyle\leq\frac{4t\sqrt{d^{2}-1}}{N}.

Thus no higher tensor-power loss is necessary: for fixedtt, the
two-site dependence onNNandddpersists, and the two-site examples
above show that this dependence is optimal. The linear factor inttis also unavoidable in a joint large-dimension regime; see
TheoremB.5in the appendix.

## 1.2.2Two-sided Bose-symmetric extendible states and
trace-norm applications

LetℋA=ℂdA\mathcal{H}_{A}=\mathbb{C}^{d_{A}}andℋB=ℂdB\mathcal{H}_{B}=\mathbb{C}^{d_{B}}. Recall that a bipartite
state isseparableif it is a convex combination of product
states, and writeSEP⁡(dA,dB)\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})for the set of such states. Forn,m≥1n,m\geq 1, letEXTn,m⁡(dA,dB)\operatorname{EXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B})be the set of statesρA​B\rho_{AB}admitting an extension onℋA⊗n⊗ℋB⊗m\mathcal{H}_{A}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}^{\otimes m}that is invariant under permutations
within the two blocks and whoseA1​B1A_{1}B_{1}marginal isρA​B\rho_{AB}[TDS03,JV13,JSZ22]. Its Bose-symmetric subfamilyBEXTn,m⁡(dA,dB)\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B})consists of the states admitting such an extension
supported onSymn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symm⁡(ℋB)\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{B}). This is the natural two-sided
analogue of the one-sided Bose-symmetric extension
hierarchy[DPS04,NOP09]. Here
“Bose-symmetric” denotes the same support condition called
“bosonic” above. ThusBEXTn,m\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,m}differs fromEXTn,m\operatorname{EXT}_{n,m}only
by this symmetric-support requirement. Since pure product states have tensor-power
extensions,SEP⁡(dA,dB)⊆BEXTn,m⁡(dA,dB)⊆EXTn,m⁡(dA,dB).\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\subseteq\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B})\subseteq\operatorname{EXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B}).

The familiar one-sidedkk-extendibility hierarchy is the(1,k)(1,k)case,
up to exchanging the two parties. Our bound applies to every pair of
extension levels, depends on the smaller local dimension, and captures
the multiplicative gain when both extension levels grow. At the
one-sided endpoints, specialized de Finetti bounds can be stronger.

## Theorem 1.4(De Finetti upper bound for two-sided
Bose-symmetric extendible states;
cf. Theorem3.9).

For everyn,m,dA,dB≥1n,m,d_{A},d_{B}\geq 1,DHtr​(BEXTn,m⁡(dA,dB),SEP⁡(dA,dB))≤12​min⁡{dA,dB}−1n​m.D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B}),\,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\Bigr)\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}{nm}}.

For balanced extension levels and equal local dimensions, thed/n\sqrt{d}/ndependence is optimal up to universal constants. The
rectangular Werner construction of Christandl, König, Mitchison, and
Renner gives the matching order after symmetric purification and
grouping into two equal blocks[CKMR07].

## Corollary 1.5(De Finetti upper bound for two-sided extendible
states;
cf. Corollary3.12).

For everyn,m,dA,dB≥1n,m,d_{A},d_{B}\geq 1,DHtr​(EXTn,m⁡(dA,dB),SEP⁡(dA,dB))≤12​min⁡{dA2,dB2}−1n​m.D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{EXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B}),\,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\Bigr)\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\min\{d_{A}^{2},d_{B}^{2}\}-1}{nm}}.

This follows by taking the canonical purification of the two-block
extension and then applying the preceding theorem with local
dimensionsdA2d_{A}^{2}anddB2d_{B}^{2}.
In the balanced case, the resultingd/nd/ndependence for arbitrary
two-sided extensions is also optimal in a joint high-dimensional
regime, as witnessed by the rectangular Werner examples
of[CKMR07].

The Bose-symmetric theorem above is the common interface for the three
trace-norm applications. For the disentangler,BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB)\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B})is realized as the image of a channel: the
theorem controls every possible output, while tensor-power inputs give
exact coverage of the separable states. For BSS, we optimize the
verifier’s accepting operator overBEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB)\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B}).
Because every feasible state is close to separable, the resulting
relaxation approximateshSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)with an additive error independent
of the accepting operatorMM. Trace-norm weak membership then follows
from this BSS approximation.

All three applications therefore have resource bounds at the same
scale, subexponential in the local dimension. WhendA=dB=dd_{A}=d_{B}=d, the
BSS SDP acts onSymn⁡(ℂd)⊗Symn⁡(ℂd)\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathbb{C}^{d})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathbb{C}^{d}), whose dimension is(d+n−1n)2.\binom{d+n-1}{n}^{2}.

The disentangler instead takes2​n2nunrestricteddd-dimensional input
registers, grouped asA1,…,AnA_{1},\ldots,A_{n}andB1,…,BnB_{1},\ldots,B_{n}, so its
input dimension isD=d2​nD=d^{2n}. Both resources have logarithmO​(n​log⁡d)O(n\log d).
The sharp errorO​(d/n)O(\sqrt{d}/n)allowsn=Θ​(d/ε)n=\Theta(\sqrt{d}/\varepsilon), so both resource bounds areexp⁡(O~​(d/ε))\exp(\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{d}/\varepsilon)). AnO​(d/n)O(d/n)convergence bound
would instead requiren=Θ​(d/ε)n=\Theta(d/\varepsilon), returning to the
exhaustive-search scale.

## Disentanglers.

A disentangler was proposed as a way to replace two unentangled quantum
proofs by one ordinary quantum proof. It is a channelΛ:D​(ℂD)→D​(ℂd⊗ℂd)\Lambda:\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{D})\to\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d})with two guarantees. Every output
ofΛ\Lambdaisε\varepsilon-close to some separable state, so an
entangled input cannot create much entanglement at the output.
Conversely, every separable state isδ\delta-close to some output, so
the image covers the entire separable set. Such a channel is called an(ε,δ)(\varepsilon,\delta)-disentangler. For sufficiently small errors, iflog⁡D\log Dwere polylogarithmic inddand the channel could be
implemented efficiently, composing it with aQMA​(2)\mathrm{QMA}(2)verifier would
simulate the two unentangled proofs using one ordinary QMA witness.

Watrous’s conjecture, recorded by Aaronson, Beigi, Drucker, Fefferman,
and Shor in 2008[ABD+08], proposed an information-theoretic
obstruction: it predicted thatD=exp⁡(Ω​(d))D=\exp(\Omega(d))whenever the fixed errors satisfyε+δ<1\varepsilon+\delta<1, whereddis the local output dimension. The
conjecture had survived for almost two decades. The two-sided
Bose-symmetric de Finetti theorem gives a channel with exact coverage,δ=0\delta=0, whose input dimension is subexponential in the local output
dimensiondd.

## Theorem 1.6(Subexponential disentanglers).

For every fixedε∈(0,1)\varepsilon\in(0,1)and everyd≥2d\geq 2, there is an(ε,0)(\varepsilon,0)-disentanglerΛ:D​(ℂD)⟶D​(ℂd⊗ℂd)\Lambda:\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{D})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d})

withlog⁡D=Oε​(d​log⁡d).\log D=O_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{d}\log d).

In particular, there is a(1/4,0)(1/4,0)-disentangler satisfying
the same dimension bound.

The image of the channel isBEXTn,n⁡(d,d)\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d,d).
Theorem1.4gives
soundness, while tensor-power inputs give exact coverage; the explicit
construction appears in Subsection5.1. For anmm-qubit output register, whered=2md=2^{m}, our channel still needsΘε​(2m/2​m)\Theta_{\varepsilon}(2^{m/2}m)input qubits, so the result does not implyQMA​(2)=QMA\mathrm{QMA}(2)=\mathrm{QMA}. Its significance is instead structural: the
conjectured exponential obstruction in the local dimension fails,
leaving open the possibility of substantially smaller disentanglers.

Akibue, Kato, and Tani recently proved the conjectured exponential
lower bound forstrong disentanglers[AKT24]. For every
reference systemRRand every joint input, their definition requires
the output onA​B​RABRto be close to a state separable acrossA:B​RA:BR;
that is, one output must be approximately disentangled from both the
other output and the reference. Our channel guarantees only that the
reduced output onA​BABis close to separable acrossA:BA:Band need not
satisfy this stronger requirement. Thus the two results separate the
original and strong notions of disentangling rather than contradicting
one another.

## Best Separable State.

The second application asks for the largest acceptance probability of
a measurement on a separable state. Given its accepting operatorMM, satisfying0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq Ionℂd⊗ℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d}, theBest Separable State(BSS) value ishSEP(M):=maxσ∈SEP⁡(d,d)Tr(Mσ)=max‖x‖=1‖y‖=1⟨x⊗y|M|x⊗y⟩.h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M):=\max_{\sigma\in\operatorname{SEP}(d,d)}\operatorname{Tr}(M\sigma)=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\lVert x\right\rVert=1\\
\left\lVert y\right\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\langle x\otimes y\rvert M\lvert x\otimes y\rangle.

The equality holds because the linear objectiveTr⁡(M​σ)\operatorname{Tr}(M\sigma)is maximized over the separable set by a
pure product state. Operationally,MMis the accepting operator of a
verifier, andhSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)is the largest acceptance probability
achievable by two unentangled messages. This is exactly the
optimization core ofQMA​(2)\mathrm{QMA}(2). From a classical perspective,
it is also a basic tensor-optimization problem, linked through standard
reductions to the landscape surrounding the2→42\to 4norm and Unique
Games[BBH+12].

For constant accuracy, the direct net-search algorithm takesexp⁡(O​(d))\exp(O(d))time. Before this work, noexp⁡(o​(d))\exp(o(d))algorithm was
known for general explicit BSS—in other words, no nontrivial
improvement over exhaustive search was known. Barak, Kothari, and
Steurer came closest: under the perfect-completeness promisehSEP​(M)=1h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)=1, they obtained an algorithm running in time
subexponential in the local dimension and explicitly asked whether
that assumption could be removed[BKS17, Remark 1.4]. The
two-sided Bose-symmetric extension SDP hierarchy removes it.

## Theorem 1.7(Best separable state without perfect completeness;
cf. Theorem5.2).

Let0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq Ibe explicitly given onℂd⊗ℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d}. For everyε∈(0,1]\varepsilon\in(0,1], the valuehSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)can be approximated to additive errorε\varepsilondeterministically. Up to a factor polynomial in the input bit
complexity, the running time isexp⁡(O~​(dε)).\exp\left(\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right).

The guarantee in Theorem1.7is proved in
Subsection5.2by evaluatinghBEXTn,n​(M)h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M). The additive error is independent of the value
ofhSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M), so the guarantee applies to every choice of
completeness and soundness parametersc>sc>sand removes perfect
completeness for explicit BSS.
The generic de Finetti argument does not exploit perfect completeness
and therefore does not recover the ETH-optimal bound established by the
direct SoS analysis[JWX26].

## Trace-norm separability testing.

The third application is separability testing: given an explicitly
described bipartite stateρ\rho, decide whether it is separable.
Since exact membership is not numerically robust, we use the standard
weak-membership formulation, with distance toSEP⁡(d,d)\operatorname{SEP}(d,d)measured in
trace distance. Given a toleranceε>0\varepsilon>0, the task is to
distinguishρ∈SEP⁡(d,d)fromDtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(d,d))≥ε,\rho\in\operatorname{SEP}(d,d)\qquad\text{from}\qquad D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d,d)\bigr)\geq\varepsilon,

with no requirement whenρ\rholies in the interveningε\varepsilon-neighborhood. Approximating the distance to the separable
set is a stronger, quantitative version of this problem.

The direction we need—from BSS optimization to this weak-membership
problem—is due to Harrow and
Montanaro[HM13, Proposition 16]. They show that an additive
approximation tohSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)valid for every accepting operatorMMgives trace-norm weak membership for the separable set with only a
constant-factor loss in accuracy.
This is sharper than generic convex-optimization reductions, which lose
polynomial factors in the dimension[GLS93]. In our setting, the reduction is
visible from trace-distance duality:Dtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(d,d))=max0⪯M⪯I⁡{Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hSEP​(M)}.D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d,d)\bigr)=\max_{0\preceq M\preceq I}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\right\}.

HereMMis the accepting operator of the two-outcome measurement that
best separatesρ\rhofrom every separable state, whilehSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)is the largest acceptance probability of that same
measurement on a separable state. Our new input to the
Harrow–Montanaro reduction is a
subexponential-time approximation tohSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)valid for every
accepting operator0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq I. Optimizing the resulting
estimate overMMgives the distance to separability.

Weak membership for the separable set with promise gap1/poly⁡(d)1/\operatorname{poly}(d)is NP-hard[Gur03,Gha10].
Much less is known in the constant-gap regime. In particular, before
this work no trace-norm algorithm running in time subexponential in the
local dimension was known nor ruled out. The standardO​(d/N)O(d/N)de Finetti analysis reaches constant error only at levelN=Ω​(d)N=\Omega(d), again at exponential cost; the quasipolynomial
algorithms in weaker LOCC and Hilbert–Schmidt geometries do not imply a
trace-norm algorithm[BCY11b]. Our subexponential algorithm forhSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)gives the first such subexponential
trace-norm weak-membership algorithm.

## Theorem 1.8(Trace-norm separability testing;
cf. Theorem5.4).

Letρ\rhobe an explicitly given state onℂd⊗ℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d}. For everyε∈(0,1]\varepsilon\in(0,1], the trace distance fromρ\rhoto the separable
states can be approximated to additive errorε\varepsilondeterministically. Up to a factor polynomial in the input bit
complexity, the running time isexp⁡(O~​(dε)).\exp\left(\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right).

Harrow and Montanaro connected BSS through reductions with more than a
dozen problems, including injective tensor norms, channel output norms,
mean-field ground-state energy, minimum output Rényi-22and
min-entropies, and planted clique[HM13, Sec. 4.2]. Our algorithm
therefore transfers to every problem in this network that reduces to
BSS, with the resulting running time determined by the dimension and
accuracy losses of the reduction.

## 1.2.3From optimal trace norm to dimension-free
Hilbert–Schmidt norm

The classical finite de Finetti theorem is dimension free, while
dimension-free quantum convergence cannot hold in trace norm.
More practically, the familiarO​(d/N)O(\sqrt{d}/N)trace-norm bounds requireN=Ω​(d)N=\Omega(\sqrt{d})even for constant accuracy, making de Finetti-based
algorithms prohibitively expensive in many applications. These two
obstacles motivated de Finetti theorems under restricted
measurements[BC12,BH13,LS15]. Rougerie further asked whether a
dimension-independent quantum analogue of the classical
Diaconis–Freedman theorem could be
found[Rou15, Remark 4.2(4)]. The optimal trace-norm theorem motivates another
look at this question. We show that Hilbert–Schmidt geometry restores
dimension-free convergence, with the optimal dimension-independent
rate.

We use Hilbert–Schmidt distanceDHS​(ρ,σ):=‖ρ−σ‖2,DHS​(ρ,𝒦):=infτ∈𝒦DHS​(ρ,τ),D_{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho,\sigma):=\left\lVert\rho-\sigma\right\rVert_{2},\qquad D_{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho,\mathcal{K}):=\inf_{\tau\in\mathcal{K}}D_{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho,\tau),

and useDHHS​(𝒦,ℒ)D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}(\mathcal{K},\,\mathcal{L})for the analogous worst-case
de Finetti error in Hilbert–Schmidt distance.

## Theorem 1.9(Dimension-free bosonic Hilbert–Schmidt de
Finetti upper bound; cf. Theorem6.1).

For everyN,d≥2N,d\geq 2,DHHS​(BOSN(2)⁡(d),SEPH(2)⁡(d))=O​(N−1/2),D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(2)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)\Bigr)=O(N^{-1/2}),

with a universal implied constant.

This gives a qualitatively new, dimension-free de Finetti theorem for
bosonic states. ItsN−1/2N^{-1/2}dependence is optimal when the
dimension is allowed to grow: the lower bound in
TheoremB.6adapts the bosonic pair construction of
Jiang, Tacla, and Caves[JTC17]. Unlike in trace norm, the
purification argument does not give the same conclusion for arbitrary
exchangeable states:
tracing out the purifying registers need not contract
Hilbert–Schmidt norm. Whether an analogous dimension-free theorem
holds in the exchangeable setting remains open.

## Theorem 1.10(Dimension-free Hilbert–Schmidt upper bound for
two-sided Bose-symmetric extendible states; cf. Corollary6.8).

For everyn,dA,dB≥1n,d_{A},d_{B}\geq 1,DHHS​(BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB),SEP⁡(dA,dB))=O​(n−1/2),D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B}),\,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\Bigr)=O(n^{-1/2}),

with a universal implied constant.

This rate is likewise optimal when the local dimensions may grow.
TheoremB.7groups the same
adapted pair construction into two equal blocks.

Because the Hilbert–Schmidt norm is its own dual, the state
approximation also controlshSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)for every Hermitian objective
matrixMMof bounded Hilbert–Schmidt norm. Adding a multiple of the
identity toMMmerely shifts every feasible value by the same known
constant, so only its traceless part matters. For positive-semidefinite
objectives, Shi and Wu previously obtained a polynomial-time
approximation at fixed accuracy and bounded Hilbert–Schmidt norm by anε\varepsilon-net method[SW15]. Here the conclusion for arbitrary
Hermitian objectives follows instead from a constant-level
symmetric-extension SDP: at every fixed accuracy, it suffices to taken=O​(ε−2)n=O(\varepsilon^{-2}), so the resulting semidefinite program is
polynomial in the local dimensions. The same de Finetti estimate also
improves the quasipolynomial weak-membership guarantee of Brandão,
Christandl, and Yard[BCY11b].
Concurrent work of Malavolta[Mal26]gives a randomized
polynomial-time algorithm for constant-gap Euclidean separability.

## Theorem 1.11(Polynomial-timehSEPh_{\operatorname{SEP}}approximation;
cf. Theorem7.1).

For every fixedε>0\varepsilon>0,hSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)admits a deterministic
additive-ε\varepsilonapproximation in time polynomial in the local
dimensions wheneverMMis explicitly given and its traceless part has
Hilbert–Schmidt normO​(1)O(1).

## Theorem 1.12(Polynomial-time Hilbert–Schmidt weak membership;
cf. Theorem7.2).

For every fixedε>0\varepsilon>0, given an explicitly described stateρ∈D​(ℂdA⊗ℂdB)\rho\in\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{A}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d_{B}}), its Hilbert–Schmidt distanceDHS​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB))D_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr)can be approximated to additive
errorε\varepsilonin deterministic time
polynomial in the local dimensions. In the same running time one can
solve Hilbert–Schmidt weak membership, distinguishingρ∈SEP⁡(dA,dB)fromDHS​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB))≥ε,\rho\in\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\qquad\text{from}\qquad D_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr)\geq\varepsilon,

with no requirement in the intervening regime.Two-site trace-norm de FinettiTheorems1.1,1.2tt-site trace-norm de FinettiTheorem1.3Two-sided Bose-symmetric extendible statesTheorem1.4Bosonic Hilbert–Schmidt de FinettiTheorem1.9Subexponential disentanglersTheorem1.6SubexponentialhSEPh_{\operatorname{SEP}}algorithmTheorem1.7Trace-norm separability testingTheorem1.8Furtherconsequencesvia Harrow–Montanaroreductionsmean-field energy, tensor/channel norms,Rényi entropies, planted cliqueHilbert–SchmidthSEPh_{\operatorname{SEP}}Theorem1.11Hilbert–SchmidtseparabilitytestingTheorem1.12

## 1.3Technical overview

The sharp two-site bosonic trace-norm theorem is the engine of the
paper. We first explain its argmax proof, obtain the exchangeable
theorem by purification, and develop the rectangular version of
argmax rounding. The rectangular estimate directly gives the
two-sided de Finetti theorem and its trace-norm applications. The
higher-marginal proof then combines the same estimate with a symmetry
test. Finally, a separate spectral-truncation argument builds on the
sharp trace-norm bound to prove the dimension-free bosonic
Hilbert–Schmidt theorem.

## 1.3.1Optimal two-site trace-norm de Finetti

We begin with the optimal bosonic and exchangeable two-site bounds
stated in Theorems1.1and1.2.

## From de Finetti to a symmetric-tensor SDP integrality gap.

The route to the proof began with a different problem. In our recent
work on sum-of-squares algorithms[JWX26], we developed argmax rounding in
pursuit of an ETH-optimal analysis of the SoS algorithm for Best Separable State
in the perfect-completeness case. There the idea is quite natural: build a
high-moment polynomial from the SoS solution, choose the direction
where it is largest, and use the local optimality conditions at this
direction for rounding.

The connection between symmetric extensions and SoS/SDP relaxations
reaches back at least to the Doherty–Parrilo–Spedalieri (DPS)
hierarchy, which casts symmetric extendibility as an SDP test for
separability[DPS04]. The duality between SoS relaxations on the
sphere and the DPS hierarchy is explicitly recorded by Fang and
Fawzi[FF21]. By comparison, the associated rounding viewpoint
seems to have been explored less extensively; see, however,
Rao[Rao24]for related recent work on Hermitian SoS rounding and
quantum de Finetti theorems.

In our setting, trace-norm duality makes this interface concrete: the
Hartree optimum of a distinguishing witness is a polynomial
optimization over the sphere, while the level-NNbosonic optimum
is the corresponding symmetric-tensor SDP relaxation. The argmax
viewpoint is especially natural here because the de Finetti theorem is
information-theoretic: unlike in the algorithmic setting, there is no
need to find the maximizing direction efficiently. We may choose it
exactly and exploit its local optimality conditions without regard to
running time. This shared geometry leads to the optimal de Finetti
rate, and the parallel with the earlier analyses goes further.

The BKS argument obtains its rounding direction by Gaussian rounding,
averaging over random directions and then conditioning[BKS17].
Standard constructive de Finetti proofs likewise first choose a
universal rounding map, usually by averaging tensor powers over
Haar-random directions. Given a bosonic state, this fixed map produces
one mixture that must approximate its marginal against every
measurement at once. The resulting argument gives the familiarO​(d/N)O(d/N)bosonic trace-norm bound[CKMR07,Chi11,Har13,LNR15].

Our proof replaces this averaging by an objective-dependent extremal
choice: the rounding is tailored to the distinguishing witness. To make
that choice, we reverse the order. Trace-norm duality first exposes a
bounded Hermitian witnessMM, with‖M‖∞≤1\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1, for
the approximation error; only then do we
round an optimizer of the symmetric-tensor SDP relaxation forMM.
That such dependence can reveal
extra information is already suggested by measurement-restricted de
Finetti theorems[BH13,LS15]and the value de Finetti theorem for
stoquastic verification[GGJ26]. We do not need a single map that
succeeds simultaneously for all witnesses. This is a weaker demand, but
the dual formulation below says that it is exactly enough.

To make this precise, letM∈Herm⁡(Sym2⁡ℋ)M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathcal{H})and defineh​(M)\displaystyle h(M):=max‖u‖=1⟨u⊗2|M|u⊗2⟩,\displaystyle:=\max_{\left\lVert u\right\rVert=1}\langle u^{\otimes 2}\rvert M\lvert u^{\otimes 2}\rangle,M[N]\displaystyle M^{[N]}:=(N2)−1​∑1≤i<j≤NMi​jon​SymN⁡ℋ,\displaystyle:=\binom{N}{2}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}M_{ij}\quad\text{on }\operatorname{Sym}^{N}\mathcal{H},

whereMi​jM_{ij}acts asMMon sitesi,ji,jand as the identity on every
other site. Thush​(M)h(M)is the original quartic optimization, whileλmax​(M[N])=maxρN∈D​(SymN⁡ℋ)⁡Tr⁡(M[N]​ρN)\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]})=\max_{\rho_{N}\in\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{Sym}^{N}\mathcal{H})}\operatorname{Tr}(M^{[N]}\rho_{N})

is the value of its level-NNsymmetric-tensor SDP relaxation, the
bosonic form of the corresponding SoS relaxation. After dualizing trace distance
and using permutation symmetry, the worst-case de Finetti error is exactlyDHtr​(BOSN(2)⁡(d),SEPH(2)⁡(d))=12​sup‖M‖∞≤1{λmax​(M[N])−h​(M)}.D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(2)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)\Bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1}\left\{\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]})-h(M)\right\}.(1.7)

So the de Finetti problem has become a familiar TCS question: bound the
integrality gap of an SDP relaxation. We no longer need to construct
the approximating mixture. For each bounded Hermitian witnessMM, it
is enough to round an optimizer of the corresponding relaxation
with lossO​(d/N)O(\sqrt{d}/N).

## The argmax rounding object.

FixMMand letψ∈SymN⁡(ℋ)\psi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathcal{H})be a unit top eigenvector ofM[N]M^{[N]}. The argmax principle gives a natural rounding object:
choose the tensor poweru⊗Nu^{\otimes N}having maximum overlap withψ\psi. After fixing its phase, writec:=⟨u⊗N,ψ⟩>0.c:=\langle u^{\otimes N},\psi\rangle>0.

The argmax lemma provides the basic tools for analyzing this rounding
object. Take the partial inner product ofψ\psiwithuuon all but
two sites:ηu:=(⟨u|⊗(N−2)⊗Iℋ⊗2)ψ∈Sym2(ℋ).\eta_{u}:=(\langle u\rvert^{\otimes(N-2)}\otimes I_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes 2}})\psi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}).

Concretely, the lemma saysηu=c​u⊗2+ζu,ζu∈Sym2⁡(u⟂),supv⟂u‖v‖=1|⟨v⊗2,ζu⟩|≤cN−1.\eta_{u}=c\,u^{\otimes 2}+\zeta_{u},\qquad\zeta_{u}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(u^{\perp}),\qquad\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\perp u\\
\left\lVert v\right\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\langle v^{\otimes 2},\zeta_{u}\rangle\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{N-1}.(1.8)

Here is why. Perturbuutoward any orthogonal directionvvand
differentiate the overlap withψ\psi. The first derivative says that
the component with one copy ofvvvanishes. The second derivative
says that the component with two copies ofvvis onlyO​(c/N)O(c/N).
Doing this for everyv⟂uv\perp ugives
(1.8). In words, the partial inner
product is the tensor squareu⊗2u^{\otimes 2}, scaled bycc, plus an error
living entirely in directions orthogonal touuand having small
overlap with every orthogonal squarev⊗2v^{\otimes 2}. These are precisely
the two tools
supplied by the argmax lemma: the mixed component vanishes, and the
fully orthogonal component is small against every square. This is the
bosonic counterpart of the pseudo-covariance lemma underlying our SoS
argmax rounding[JWX26].

## Bounding the integrality gap.

We now use the information supplied by the argmax lemma to compareλmax​(M[N])\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]})withh​(M)h(M). By symmetry, the liftM[N]M^{[N]}can be evaluated on sites1,21,2, and henceλmax​(M[N])​c=⟨M​u⊗2,ηu⟩.\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]})c=\langle Mu^{\otimes 2},\eta_{u}\rangle.(1.9)

LetBu:=PSym2⁡(u⟂)​M​u⊗2.B_{u}:=P_{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(u^{\perp})}Mu^{\otimes 2}.

The component ofM​u⊗2Mu^{\otimes 2}alongu⊗2u^{\otimes 2}contributes the feasible
tensor-square value⟨u⊗2|M|u⊗2⟩≤h(M)\langle u^{\otimes 2}\rvert M\lvert u^{\otimes 2}\rangle\leq h(M); the component inu∨u⟂u\vee u^{\perp}is orthogonal toηu\eta_{u}. Equations
(1.8) and
(1.9) therefore leave the exact identity(λmax(M[N])−⟨u⊗2|M|u⊗2⟩)c=⟨Bu,ζu⟩.\bigl(\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]})-\langle u^{\otimes 2}\rvert M\lvert u^{\otimes 2}\rangle\bigr)c=\langle B_{u},\zeta_{u}\rangle.

Choose an orthonormal basis ofu⟂u^{\perp}and identify symmetric
two-tensors with complex symmetric matrices. WriteCuC_{u}for the
matrix representingBuB_{u}andZuZ_{u}for the matrix representingζu\zeta_{u}. The argmax bound
(1.8) says‖Zu‖∞≤c/(N−1)\left\lVert Z_{u}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq c/(N-1). Moreover,CuC_{u}is a(d−1)×(d−1)(d-1)\times(d-1)matrix and‖Cu‖2=‖Bu‖≤‖M‖∞\left\lVert C_{u}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert B_{u}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}. Its trace norm is
therefore at mostd−1​‖M‖∞\sqrt{d-1}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}. Schatten duality now gives|⟨Bu,ζu⟩|=|Tr⁡(Cu†​Zu)|≤c​d−1N−1​‖M‖∞.\left\lvert\langle B_{u},\zeta_{u}\rangle\right\rvert=\left\lvert\operatorname{Tr}(C_{u}^{\dagger}Z_{u})\right\rvert\leq\frac{c\sqrt{d-1}}{N-1}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}.

Consequently,λmax​(M[N])≤h​(M)+d−1N−1​‖M‖∞.\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]})\leq h(M)+\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{N-1}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}.

Together with (1.7), this proves the sharpO​(d/N)O(\sqrt{d}/N)theorem. The important point is that argmax rounding
leaves the single matrix pairingTr⁡(Cu†​Zu)\operatorname{Tr}(C_{u}^{\dagger}Z_{u}). Passing from
the Hilbert–Schmidt norm of the(d−1)×(d−1)(d-1)\times(d-1)matrixCuC_{u}to its
trace norm costs onlyd−1\sqrt{d-1}, which is exactly the improvement
we need.

The exchangeable theorem follows by purifying each site into
dimensiond2d^{2}, applying the bosonic theorem, and tracing out the
purifying registers.

## Two-sided extendibility.

The same proof extends directly to two symmetric blocks. For a
Hermitian operatorMMonℋA⊗ℋB\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}, averageMMover then​mnmcross-block pairs to form the rectangular liftM[n,m]M^{[n,m]}. Maximizing
the overlap of a top eigenvector withx⊗n⊗y⊗mx^{\otimes n}\otimes y^{\otimes m}, the
same first- and second-order argument givesλmax​(M[n,m])−hSEP​(M)≤min⁡{dA,dB}−1n​m​‖M‖∞.\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M^{[n,m]}\bigr)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\leq\sqrt{\frac{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}{nm}}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}.

Trace-distance duality yields
Theorem1.4, and a canonical purification
of the two blocks yields Corollary1.5.

## The disentangler from the two-sided theorem.

The disentangler in Theorem1.6is obtained by
realizingBEXTn,n⁡(d,d)\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d,d)as the image of a channel: project theA1,…,AnA_{1},\ldots,A_{n}registers and theB1,…,BnB_{1},\ldots,B_{n}registers ontoSymn⁡(ℂd)\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathbb{C}^{d}), sending rejected mass to a fixed product state, and
then retain onlyA1​B1A_{1}B_{1}. Every output is Bose-symmetric(n,n)(n,n)-extendible, while every separable state has an exact preimage
obtained from tensor powers of its product-state decomposition.
Choosingn=O​(d/ε)n=O(\sqrt{d}/\varepsilon)gives an(ε,0)(\varepsilon,0)-disentangler
with input dimensionD=d2​nD=d^{2n}, and hencelog⁡D=Oε​(d​log⁡d)\log D=O_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{d}\log d).

## BSS and weak membership from the same theorem.

For an accepting operator0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq IonA​BAB, optimize
overBEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB)\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B})and writehBEXTn,n​(M):=maxρ∈BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB)⁡Tr⁡(M​ρ).h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M):=\max_{\rho\in\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B})}\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho).

Every separable state is feasible, while the two-sided theorem places
every feasible state withinO​(min⁡{dA,dB}/n)O(\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}}/n)of the separable states.
Consequently,hSEP​(M)≤hBEXTn,n​(M)≤hSEP​(M)+O​(min⁡{dA,dB}n).h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\leq h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M)\leq h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)+O\left(\frac{\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}}}{n}\right).

This bound, valid for everyMM, gives the BSS approximation in
Theorem1.7. The Harrow–Montanaro
reduction[HM13, Proposition 16]then gives trace-norm weak
membership, and the equivalent trace-distance dual formulation gives
the additive distance approximation in
Theorem1.8.

## 1.3.2tt-site trace-norm de Finetti

## The1:(t−1)1:(t-1)cut.

For thett-site bounds in Theorem1.3, trace-distance
duality asks us to compare the value of a witnessMMwith its Hartree
optimum. For0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq IonSymt⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{t}(\mathcal{H}), this optimum isht(M):=max‖u‖=1⟨u⊗t|M|u⊗t⟩.h_{t}(M):=\max_{\left\lVert u\right\rVert=1}\langle u^{\otimes t}\rvert M\lvert u^{\otimes t}\rangle.

The two-site argument suggests first enforcing unentanglement across a
bipartition. The natural choice is the1:(t−1)1:(t-1)cutℋA:=ℋ,ℋB:=Symt−1⁡(ℋ),Symt⁡(ℋ)⊆ℋA⊗ℋB.\mathcal{H}_{A}:=\mathcal{H},\qquad\mathcal{H}_{B}:=\operatorname{Sym}^{t-1}(\mathcal{H}),\qquad\operatorname{Sym}^{t}(\mathcal{H})\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}.

This cut exposes one factor of dimensiondd, so the loss in rectangular
argmax rounding depends ond−1\sqrt{d-1}rather than on the much larger
dimension ofSymt−1⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{t-1}(\mathcal{H}). It also fits the bosonic extension naturally:
organizing theNNsites intonnindividual sites andmmblocks oft−1t-1sites makesρN(t)\rho_{N}^{(t)}Bose-symmetric(n,m)(n,m)-extendible acrossℋA:ℋB\mathcal{H}_{A}:\mathcal{H}_{B}. The associated rectangular lift is therefore the two-sided
symmetric-extension relaxation of product optimization across this cut.

The argmax principle supplies the main rounding step. The value of the
relaxation is the largest eigenvalue of the rectangular lift. Letψ\psibe
a unit top eigenvector of the rectangular lift. Argmax rounding chooses(x,y)∈arg​max‖v‖=1‖w‖=1⁡|⟨v⊗n⊗w⊗m,ψ⟩|,(x,y)\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\lVert v\right\rVert=1\\
\left\lVert w\right\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\langle v^{\otimes n}\otimes w^{\otimes m},\psi\rangle\right\rvert,

wherev,x∈ℋAv,x\in\mathcal{H}_{A}andw,y∈ℋBw,y\in\mathcal{H}_{B}, and returns the product vectorx⊗yx\otimes y. This enforces unentanglement across the1:(t−1)1:(t-1)cut, but
it does not yet imply thaty=x⊗(t−1)y=x^{\otimes(t-1)}, as required for a Hartree
vector.

## Symmetry propagates unentanglement.

To propagate this unentanglement to the remaining cuts, we use the
symmetry test in the spirit of the disentangler from unentanglement of
Jeronimo and Wu[JW24]. LetΠt\Pi_{t}be the orthogonal projector fromℋA⊗ℋB\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}ontoSymt⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{t}(\mathcal{H}). A product vector in this symmetric
subspace must be a tensor power. Define the symmetry-test rejection
probability byδsym:=1−⟨x⊗y|Πt|x⊗y⟩.\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}:=1-\langle x\otimes y\rvert\Pi_{t}\lvert x\otimes y\rangle.

Permutation averaging gives1−|⟨x⊗(t−1),y⟩|2≤t​δsym.1-\left\lvert\langle x^{\otimes(t-1)},y\rangle\right\rvert^{2}\leq t\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}.

Thus, when the rounded product passes the symmetry test with high
probability, its unentanglement across the1:(t−1)1:(t-1)cut propagates to
the remaining cuts, andx⊗yx\otimes yis close to the tensor powerx⊗tx^{\otimes t}.
This is the content of
Lemma4.4; its linear dependence onttsharpens
the corresponding one-versus-many estimate in[JW24, Theorem 23].

## Bounding the integrality gap.

We incorporate the symmetry test before rectangular argmax rounding so
that the witness value and the rejection probability are
controlled through the same rounded product. ExtendMMby zero outsideSymt⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{t}(\mathcal{H})and introduce the mixed testMθ:=(1−θ)​Πt+θ​M,0<θ<1.M_{\theta}:=(1-\theta)\Pi_{t}+\theta M,\qquad 0<\theta<1.

It performs the symmetry test with weight1−θ1-\thetaand evaluatesMMwith weightθ\theta. Every bosonictt-site marginal passes the
symmetry test with certainty. Ifρ~n,m\widetilde{\rho}_{n,m}is its grouped
extension, then1−θ+θ​Tr⁡(M​ρN(t))=Tr⁡(Mθ[n,m]​ρ~n,m)≤λmax​(Mθ[n,m]).1-\theta+\theta\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M\rho_{N}^{(t)}\bigr)=\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M_{\theta}^{[n,m]}\widetilde{\rho}_{n,m}\bigr)\leq\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M_{\theta}^{[n,m]}\bigr).

The right-hand side is the rectangular symmetric-extension relaxation
ofhSEP​(Mθ)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M_{\theta}). Applying rectangular argmax rounding to this
lift producesx⊗yx\otimes y. On that product vector,⟨x⊗y|Mθ|x⊗y⟩=(1−θ)(1−δsym)+θ⟨x⊗y|M|x⊗y⟩,\langle x\otimes y\rvert M_{\theta}\lvert x\otimes y\rangle=(1-\theta)(1-\delta_{\mathrm{sym}})+\theta\langle x\otimes y\rvert M\lvert x\otimes y\rangle,

so rejection lowers the mixed-test value. Lemma4.4controls the replacement ofx⊗yx\otimes ybyx⊗tx^{\otimes t}in terms of the same
quantity; the proof keeps these two contributions coupled rather than
first establishing a separate bound onδsym\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}.

Letβ\betadenote the error in rectangular argmax rounding. Combining
the rectangular argmax estimate with the symmetry-test bounds givesθ​(Tr⁡(M​ρN(t))−ht​(M))≤−(1−θ)​δsym+(θ​t+β​(1−θ))​δsym+β​θ.\theta\left(\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M\rho_{N}^{(t)}\bigr)-h_{t}(M)\right)\leq-(1-\theta)\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}+\bigl(\theta\sqrt{t}+\beta(1-\theta)\bigr)\sqrt{\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}}+\beta\theta.(1.10)

Choosing the two extension levels andθ\thetaappropriately in this
inequality gives the claimedO​(t​d/N)O(t\sqrt{d}/N)bound. Trace-distance
duality proves the bosonic theorem, and symmetric purification gives
the exchangeable theorem.

## 1.3.3From optimal trace norm to dimension-free
Hilbert–Schmidt norm

The dimension-free bosonic Hilbert–Schmidt de Finetti theorem stated
in Theorem1.9uses the sharp trace-norm
theorem in a different way. On a subspace of dimension less thanNN,
thed/N\sqrt{d}/Ntrace-norm bound is alreadyO​(N−1/2)O(N^{-1/2}). The spectral
subspace of the one-site marginal corresponding to eigenvalues above1/N1/Nhas dimension less thanNN. The proof applies the trace-norm
theorem on this spectral head and controls every block meeting the
complementary tail directly in Hilbert–Schmidt norm.

## Spectral truncation.

We first reduce to a pure bosonic stateρN=|ψ⟩⟨ψ|\rho_{N}=\lvert\psi\rangle\langle\psi\rvertand look
at its one-site marginalρ1\rho_{1}. DefineP:=𝟏(1/N,∞)​(ρ1),Q:=I−P.P:=\mathbf{1}_{(1/N,\infty)}(\rho_{1}),\qquad Q:=I-P.

We callP​ℋP\mathcal{H}the spectral head andQ​ℋQ\mathcal{H}the spectral tail. SinceTr⁡ρ1=1\operatorname{Tr}\rho_{1}=1,rank⁡P<N,Q​ρ1​Q⪯1N​Q.\operatorname{rank}P<N,\qquad Q\rho_{1}Q\preceq\frac{1}{N}Q.

Letρ2head:=(P⊗P)​ρ2​(P⊗P)\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}:=(P\otimes P)\rho_{2}(P\otimes P)

be the compression of the two-site marginal to the head.
The proof constructs a Hartree mixtureτhead\tau_{\mathrm{head}}, supported onSym2⁡(P​ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(P\mathcal{H})and having the same trace asρ2head\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}. A second Hartree mixtureτres\tau_{\mathrm{res}}, supported onSym2⁡(Q​ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H}), fills the missing
trace. Thusτ:=τhead+τres∈SEPH(2)⁡(d).\tau:=\tau_{\mathrm{head}}+\tau_{\mathrm{res}}\in\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d).

The three estimates enter through the error decomposition‖ρ2−τ‖2≤‖ρ2head−τhead‖2⏟round the high-eigenvalue compression+‖ρ2−ρ2head‖2⏟remove all blocks meetingthe spectral tail+‖τres‖2⏟restore the missing trace.\left\lVert\rho_{2}-\tau\right\rVert_{2}\leq\underbrace{\left\lVert\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}-\tau_{\mathrm{head}}\right\rVert_{2}}_{\text{round the high-eigenvalue compression}}+\underbrace{\left\lVert\rho_{2}-\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}\right\rVert_{2}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{remove all blocks meeting}\\
\text{the spectral tail}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\left\lVert\tau_{\mathrm{res}}\right\rVert_{2}}_{\text{restore the missing trace}}.(1.11)

The head compression is not itself anNN-site marginal, so we decomposeψ\psiaccording to the numberkkof sites in the head. In the
component with exactlykksites inP​ℋP\mathcal{H}, the probability that a
prescribed pair lies in the head isk​(k−1)N​(N−1)\frac{k(k-1)}{N(N-1)}, whereas the sharp trace-norm theorem costs only the
square root of the head dimension divided byk−1k-1. The factork−1k-1cancels, and the fact that the head dimension is belowNNleaves anO​(N−1/2)O(N^{-1/2})Hilbert–Schmidt error. This cancellation is exactly
where the optimal two-site theorem enters the dimension-free argument.

For the remaining terms, decomposeSym2⁡ℋ\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathcal{H}asSym2⁡(P​ℋ)⊕(P​ℋ∨Q​ℋ)⊕Sym2⁡(Q​ℋ).\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(P\mathcal{H})\oplus(P\mathcal{H}\vee Q\mathcal{H})\oplus\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H}).

The cutoffQ​ρ1​Q⪯Q/NQ\rho_{1}Q\preceq Q/Ngives operator-norm control on the two
diagonal compressions containing at least oneQQdirection. An ordinary
Schmidt decomposition handlesP​ℋ∨Q​ℋP\mathcal{H}\vee Q\mathcal{H}, while its symmetric form,
or Takagi decomposition, handlesSym2⁡(Q​ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H}). These decompositions
reduce the estimates to partial inner products ofψ\psiwith orthogonal vectors inQ​ℋQ\mathcal{H}, each controlled by the cutoff.
Positive-block estimates first combine the mixed and tail–tail
diagonal bounds into an operator-norm bound on the full tail block and
then control the off-diagonal head–tail blocks. Thus removing every
block meetingQ​ℋQ\mathcal{H}costs onlyO​(N−1/2)O(N^{-1/2})in Hilbert–Schmidt norm.
Finally, restore the missing
trace by adding the Haar-averaged Hartree mixture onSym2⁡(Q​ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H}); its
Hilbert–Schmidt norm isO​(1/N)O(1/N). Substitution into
(1.11) proves the dimension-freeO​(N−1/2)O(N^{-1/2})theorem.

## Two-sided extensions.

The dimension-free Hilbert–Schmidt de Finetti theorem above extends
to two-sided Bose-symmetric extendible states:DHHS​(BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB),SEP⁡(dA,dB))=O​(n−1/2).D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\bigl(\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B}),\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr)=O(n^{-1/2}).

This is the content of Theorem1.10.
Since the Hilbert–Schmidt norm is self-dual, the two-sided
Hilbert–Schmidt bound gives analogues of the BSS and weak-membership
results above.
Takingn=O​(ε−2)n=O(\varepsilon^{-2})yields the polynomial-timehSEPh_{\operatorname{SEP}}approximation and separability testing in
Theorems1.11and1.12.

## 2Preliminaries

All Hilbert spaces in this note are finite-dimensional and complex. We denote
them byℋ\mathcal{H}, adding subscripts to indicate their roles, and take their inner
products to be conjugate-linear in the first argument. Forz∈ℂz\in\mathbb{C}, we writeℜ⁡(z)\Re(z)andℑ⁡(z)\Im(z)for its real and imaginary parts, respectively. We writeL​(ℋ)\mathrm{L}(\mathcal{H})for the space of linear operators onℋ\mathcal{H}andHerm⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H})for its real subspace of Hermitian operators; andD​(ℋ)⊆Herm⁡(ℋ)\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{H})\subseteq\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H})the density operators. For a vectorvv, the notation‖v‖\left\lVert v\right\rVertdenotes its Hilbert-space norm. Foru∈ℋu\in\mathcal{H}, we writeu⟂:={v∈ℋ:⟨u,v⟩=0}u^{\perp}:=\{v\in\mathcal{H}:\langle u,v\rangle=0\}; more generally,𝒰⟂\mathcal{U}^{\perp}denotes the orthogonal complement of a subspace𝒰\mathcal{U}, with the ambient Hilbert space understood from context.
ForX∈L​(ℋ)X\in\mathrm{L}(\mathcal{H}), let|X|:=X†​X\left\lvert X\right\rvert:=\sqrt{X^{\dagger}X}and define the Schatten norms by‖X‖p:=(Tr⁡|X|p)1p(1≤p<∞),‖X‖∞:=maxj⁡sj​(X),\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{p}:=\bigl(\operatorname{Tr}\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{p}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{p}}\quad(1\leq p<\infty),\qquad\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\infty}:=\max_{j}s_{j}(X),

wheresj​(X)s_{j}(X)are the singular values ofXX. Thus‖X‖1\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{1},‖X‖2\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{2}, and‖X‖∞\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{\infty}are the trace, Hilbert–Schmidt, and
operator norms, respectively.
We use normalized trace distance and unnormalized Hilbert–Schmidt
distance:Dtr​(ρ,σ):=12​‖ρ−σ‖1,D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\sigma):=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\rho-\sigma\right\rVert_{1},(2.1)

andDHS​(ρ,σ):=‖ρ−σ‖2.D_{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho,\sigma):=\left\lVert\rho-\sigma\right\rVert_{2}.(2.2)

For probability distributions,DTVD_{\mathrm{TV}}denotes total-variation
distance.
Thus every displayed trace-distance constant is twice as large when
the error is measured by‖ρ−σ‖1\left\lVert\rho-\sigma\right\rVert_{1}instead.

Fork≥1k\geq 1, let𝔖k\mathfrak{S}_{k}act onℋ⊗k\mathcal{H}^{\otimes k}by permuting
the tensor factors, and writeUπU_{\pi}for the operator corresponding
toπ∈𝔖k\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}. The symmetric tensor power isSymk⁡ℋ:={Ψ∈ℋ⊗k:Uπ​Ψ=Ψ​for every​π∈𝔖k},\operatorname{Sym}^{k}\mathcal{H}:=\left\{\Psi\in\mathcal{H}^{\otimes k}:U_{\pi}\Psi=\Psi\ \text{for every }\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\right\},

and we setSym0⁡ℋ:=ℂ\operatorname{Sym}^{0}\mathcal{H}:=\mathbb{C}.

## 2.1Separable and extendible states

The de Finetti theorems comparett-site marginals of bosonic or
exchangeable states, and bipartite states admitting two-sided
extensions, with specific separable state families. We now define the
states appearing in these comparisons.

## Separable state families.

Fixd≥1d\geq 1andt≥1t\geq 1. The two separable families used below areSEPH(t)⁡(d)\displaystyle\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d):=conv{|u⟩⟨u|⊗t:u∈ℂd,∥u∥=1},\displaystyle={}\operatorname{conv}\left\{\lvert u\rangle\langle u\rvert^{\otimes t}:u\in\mathbb{C}^{d},\ \left\lVert u\right\rVert=1\right\},SEPP(t)⁡(d)\displaystyle\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(d):=conv⁡{σ⊗t:σ∈D​(ℂd)}.\displaystyle={}\operatorname{conv}\left\{\sigma^{\otimes t}:\sigma\in\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{d})\right\}.

The subscriptsH\mathrm{H}andP\mathrm{P}stand for Hartree and
tensor power, respectively. Since pure states are density operators,SEPH(t)⁡(d)⊆SEPP(t)⁡(d).\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d)\subseteq\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(d).

The familiesSEPH(t)⁡(d)\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d)andSEPP(t)⁡(d)\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(d)are the approximants
in the bosonic and exchangeable de Finetti theorems, respectively.

## Bosonic states.

LetN≥tN\geq t. A stateρN\rho_{N}isbosonicifρN∈D​(SymN⁡(ℂd))\rho_{N}\in\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathbb{C}^{d})). Its marginal on the firstttsites isρN(t):=Trt+1,…,N⁡ρN.\rho_{N}^{(t)}:=\operatorname{Tr}_{t+1,\ldots,N}\rho_{N}.

The set of these marginals isBOSN(t)⁡(d):={ρN(t):ρN∈D​(SymN⁡(ℂd))}.\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(t)}(d):=\left\{\rho_{N}^{(t)}:\rho_{N}\in\mathrm{D}\bigl(\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathbb{C}^{d})\bigr)\right\}.

Extending Hartree states by tensor powers and tracing out extension
sites giveSEPH(t)⁡(d)⊆BOSN+1(t)⁡(d)⊆BOSN(t)⁡(d).\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d)\subseteq\operatorname{BOS}_{N+1}^{(t)}(d)\subseteq\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(t)}(d).

## Exchangeable states.

A stateρN∈D​((ℂd)⊗N)\rho_{N}\in\mathrm{D}((\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes N})isexchangeableifUπ​ρN​Uπ†=ρNfor every​π∈𝔖N.U_{\pi}\rho_{N}U_{\pi}^{\dagger}=\rho_{N}\qquad\text{for every }\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}.

The set of all suchtt-site marginals isEXCHN(t)(d):={ρN(t):ρN∈D​((ℂd)⊗N),Uπ​ρN​Uπ†=ρNfor every​π∈𝔖N}.\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(t)}(d):=\left\{\rho_{N}^{(t)}:\begin{array}[]{l}\rho_{N}\in\mathrm{D}\bigl((\mathbb{C}^{d})^{\otimes N}\bigr),\\[-2.0pt]
U_{\pi}\rho_{N}U_{\pi}^{\dagger}=\rho_{N}\quad\text{for every }\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}\end{array}\right\}.

Tensor powers extend exchangeably, and every bosonic state is
exchangeable. ThusSEPP(t)⁡(d)⊆EXCHN+1(t)⁡(d)⊆EXCHN(t)⁡(d),BOSN(t)⁡(d)⊆EXCHN(t)⁡(d).\begin{gathered}\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(d)\subseteq\operatorname{EXCH}_{N+1}^{(t)}(d)\subseteq\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(t)}(d),\\
\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(t)}(d)\subseteq\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(t)}(d).\end{gathered}

## Two-sided extendible states.

FordA,dB≥1d_{A},d_{B}\geq 1, define the bipartite separable states bySEP(dA,dB):=conv{|u⟩⟨u|⊗|v⟩⟨v|:u∈ℂdA,v∈ℂdB,∥u∥=∥v∥=1}.\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B}):=\operatorname{conv}\left\{\lvert u\rangle\langle u\rvert\otimes\lvert v\rangle\langle v\rvert:u\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{A}},\ v\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{B}},\ \left\lVert u\right\rVert=\left\lVert v\right\rVert=1\right\}.

Forn,m≥1n,m\geq 1, the(n,m)(n,m)-extendible states are the marginalsEXTn,m(dA,dB):={ρ~A1​B1:ρ~∈D​((ℂdA)⊗n⊗(ℂdB)⊗m),ρ~​is invariant under permutations within each block}.\operatorname{EXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B}):=\left\{\widetilde{\rho}_{A_{1}B_{1}}:\begin{array}[]{l}\widetilde{\rho}\in\mathrm{D}\bigl((\mathbb{C}^{d_{A}})^{\otimes n}\otimes(\mathbb{C}^{d_{B}})^{\otimes m}\bigr),\\[-2.0pt]
\widetilde{\rho}\text{ is invariant under permutations within each block}\end{array}\right\}.

This is the standard notion of two-sided
extendibility[TDS03,JV13,JSZ22]. Requiring the extension to be
supported on the symmetric subspace of each block gives the
Bose-symmetric extendible statesBEXTn,m⁡(dA,dB):={ρ~A1​B1:ρ~∈D​(Symn⁡(ℂdA)⊗Symm⁡(ℂdB))}.\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B}):=\left\{\widetilde{\rho}_{A_{1}B_{1}}:\widetilde{\rho}\in\mathrm{D}\bigl(\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{A}})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{B}})\bigr)\right\}.(2.3)

This is the two-sided analogue of Bose-symmetric extendibility
in[NOP09]. Ifn′≥nn^{\prime}\geq nandm′≥mm^{\prime}\geq m, tracing out extension
sites and extending every term in a pure-product decomposition by
tensor powers giveSEP⁡(dA,dB)\displaystyle\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})⊆BEXTn′,m′⁡(dA,dB)⊆BEXTn,m⁡(dA,dB)⊆EXTn,m⁡(dA,dB),\displaystyle\subseteq\operatorname{BEXT}_{n^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(d_{A},d_{B})\subseteq\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B})\subseteq\operatorname{EXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B}),(2.4)EXTn′,m′⁡(dA,dB)\displaystyle\operatorname{EXT}_{n^{\prime},m^{\prime}}(d_{A},d_{B})⊆EXTn,m⁡(dA,dB).\displaystyle\subseteq\operatorname{EXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B}).EXCHN(t)⁡(d)\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(t)}(d)BOSN(t)⁡(d)\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(t)}(d)SEPP(t)⁡(d)\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(d)SEPH(t)⁡(d)\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d)(a) Bosonic and exchangeable statesEXTn,m⁡(dA,dB)\operatorname{EXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B})BEXTn,m⁡(dA,dB)\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B})SEP⁡(dA,dB)\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})(b) Two-sided extendible states
