Nesting records containment. From left to right, the dotted markers
represent the worst-case de Finetti errors for(EXCHN(t),SEPP(t))(\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(t)},\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}),(BOSN(t),SEPH(t))(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(t)},\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}),(BEXTn,m,SEP)(\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,m},\operatorname{SEP}), and(EXTn,m,SEP)(\operatorname{EXT}_{n,m},\operatorname{SEP}). Marker lengths are
schematic.

## 2.2Hausdorff notation and convex duality

For⋆∈{tr,HS}\star\in\{\mathrm{tr},\mathrm{HS}\}and a nonempty compact set𝒦\mathcal{K}of states, writeD⋆​(ρ,𝒦):=infσ∈𝒦D⋆​(ρ,σ).D_{\star}(\rho,\mathcal{K}):=\inf_{\sigma\in\mathcal{K}}D_{\star}(\rho,\sigma).(2.5)

For two nonempty compact sets𝒦\mathcal{K}andℒ\mathcal{L}of states,
their Hausdorff distance isDH⋆​(𝒦,ℒ):=max⁡{supρ∈𝒦D⋆​(ρ,ℒ),supσ∈ℒD⋆​(σ,𝒦)}.\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\star}(\mathcal{K},\,\mathcal{L})=\max\left\{\sup_{\rho\in\mathcal{K}}D_{\star}(\rho,\mathcal{L}),\sup_{\sigma\in\mathcal{L}}D_{\star}(\sigma,\mathcal{K})\right\}.(2.6)

In particular, ifℒ⊆𝒦\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{K}, thenDH⋆​(𝒦,ℒ)=supρ∈𝒦D⋆​(ρ,ℒ).D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\star}(\mathcal{K},\,\mathcal{L})=\sup_{\rho\in\mathcal{K}}D_{\star}(\rho,\mathcal{L}).(2.7)

Figure2summarizes the relevant
containments. Consequently, every de Finetti error in the paper has
the formDH⋆​(𝒦,ℒ)D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\star}(\mathcal{K},\,\mathcal{L}), with the
larger finite-extension set𝒦\mathcal{K}first and its separable subsetℒ\mathcal{L}second. The four instances are(BOSN(t)⁡(d),SEPH(t)⁡(d)),(EXCHN(t)⁡(d),SEPP(t)⁡(d)),(BEXTn,m⁡(dA,dB),SEP⁡(dA,dB)),(EXTn,m⁡(dA,dB),SEP⁡(dA,dB)).\begin{gathered}\bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(t)}(d),\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d)\bigr),\qquad\bigl(\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(t)}(d),\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(d)\bigr),\\
\bigl(\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B}),\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr),\qquad\bigl(\operatorname{EXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B}),\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr).\end{gathered}

These quantities are exactly the corresponding worst-case de Finetti
errors.

For a nonempty compact set𝒞⊆D​(ℋ)\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{H}), writeh𝒞​(M):=maxρ∈𝒞⁡Tr⁡(M​ρ)(M∈Herm⁡(ℋ))h_{\mathcal{C}}(M):=\max_{\rho\in\mathcal{C}}\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)\qquad(M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}))(2.8)

for its support function. The following lemma collects the distance
dualities used throughout the paper.

## Lemma 2.1(Distance duality for compact convex state sets).

Letℒ⊆D​(ℋ)\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{H})be nonempty, compact, and convex.
For everyρ∈D​(ℋ)\rho\in\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{H}),Dtr​(ρ,ℒ)\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\mathcal{L})=max0⪯M⪯I⁡{Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hℒ​(M)}\displaystyle=\max_{0\preceq M\preceq I}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\mathcal{L}}(M)\right\}=12​maxM∈Herm⁡(ℋ)‖M‖∞≤1⁡{Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hℒ​(M)},\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H})\\
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\mathcal{L}}(M)\right\},(2.9)DHS​(ρ,ℒ)\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho,\mathcal{L})=maxM∈Herm⁡(ℋ)‖M‖2≤1⁡{Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hℒ​(M)}\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H})\\
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\mathcal{L}}(M)\right\}=maxM∈Herm⁡(ℋ)Tr⁡M=0,‖M‖2≤1⁡{Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hℒ​(M)}.\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H})\\
\operatorname{Tr}M=0,\ \left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\mathcal{L}}(M)\right\}.(2.10)

If𝒦⊆D​(ℋ)\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{H})is also nonempty, compact, and convex,
andℒ⊆𝒦\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{K}, thenDHtr​(𝒦,ℒ)\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}(\mathcal{K},\,\mathcal{L})=max0⪯M⪯I⁡{h𝒦​(M)−hℒ​(M)}\displaystyle=\max_{0\preceq M\preceq I}\left\{h_{\mathcal{K}}(M)-h_{\mathcal{L}}(M)\right\}=12​maxM∈Herm⁡(ℋ)‖M‖∞≤1⁡{h𝒦​(M)−hℒ​(M)},\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H})\\
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\{h_{\mathcal{K}}(M)-h_{\mathcal{L}}(M)\right\},(2.11)DHHS​(𝒦,ℒ)\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}(\mathcal{K},\,\mathcal{L})=maxM∈Herm⁡(ℋ)Tr⁡M=0,‖M‖2≤1⁡{h𝒦​(M)−hℒ​(M)}.\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H})\\
\operatorname{Tr}M=0,\ \left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\{h_{\mathcal{K}}(M)-h_{\mathcal{L}}(M)\right\}.(2.12)

## Proof.

For statesρ\rhoandσ\sigma, trace-norm duality andTr⁡(ρ−σ)=0\operatorname{Tr}(\rho-\sigma)=0giveDtr​(ρ,σ)=max0⪯M⪯I⁡Tr⁡(M​(ρ−σ))=12​maxM∈Herm⁡(ℋ)‖M‖∞≤1⁡Tr⁡(M​(ρ−σ)).D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\sigma)=\max_{0\preceq M\preceq I}\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M(\rho-\sigma)\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H})\\
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M(\rho-\sigma)\bigr).

Hilbert–Schmidt self-duality similarly givesDHS​(ρ,σ)=maxM∈Herm⁡(ℋ)‖M‖2≤1⁡Tr⁡(M​(ρ−σ)).D_{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho,\sigma)=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H})\\
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M(\rho-\sigma)\bigr).

In each case, Sion’s finite-dimensional minimax
theorem[Sio58]exchanges the minimum overσ∈ℒ\sigma\in\mathcal{L}with the maximum over the corresponding compact
convex witness set. This proves both identities in
(2.9) and the first identity in
(2.10).

To obtain the formula withTr⁡M=0\operatorname{Tr}M=0, letq=dimℋq=\dim\mathcal{H}and setM0:=M−Tr⁡Mq​I.M_{0}:=M-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}M}{q}I.

ReplacingMMbyM0M_{0}leavesTr⁡(M​ρ)−hℒ​(M)\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\mathcal{L}}(M)unchanged, while orthogonality to the
identity gives‖M0‖2≤‖M‖2\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}. Thus the maximum may be
restricted to traceless witnesses.

Finally, take the maximum overρ∈𝒦\rho\in\mathcal{K}in the state-to-set
formulas. The two remaining maxima may be interchanged, and
maximization overρ\rhoreplacesTr⁡(M​ρ)\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)byh𝒦​(M)h_{\mathcal{K}}(M). Equation (2.7)
then gives (2.11) and
(2.12).
∎

## 2.3Linear-algebra estimates

## Fact 2.2(Positive block estimates).

LetA⪰0A\succeq 0act onℋA\mathcal{H}_{A}, letB⪰0B\succeq 0act onℋB\mathcal{H}_{B}, and
letX:ℋB→ℋAX:\mathcal{H}_{B}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}. Then(AXX†B)⪰0\begin{pmatrix}A&X\\
X^{\dagger}&B\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0

if and only ifX=A1/2​K​B1/2X=A^{1/2}KB^{1/2}

for a contractionK:ℋB→ℋAK:\mathcal{H}_{B}\to\mathcal{H}_{A}. In this case,‖(AXX†B)‖∞≤‖A‖∞+‖B‖∞,‖X‖22≤‖B‖∞​Tr⁡A.\left\|\begin{pmatrix}A&X\\
X^{\dagger}&B\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\infty},\qquad\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\infty}\operatorname{Tr}A.(2.13)

## Proof.

The factorization statement is[Bha07, Proposition 1.3.2]. It remains to verify the norm
estimates. Fora:=‖A‖∞a:=\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\infty}andb:=‖B‖∞b:=\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\infty},⟨u⊕v|(AXX†B)|u⊕v⟩≤(a∥u∥+b∥v∥)2≤(a+b)(∥u∥2+∥v∥2),\langle u\oplus v\rvert\begin{pmatrix}A&X\\
X^{\dagger}&B\end{pmatrix}\lvert u\oplus v\rangle\leq\left(\sqrt{a}\left\lVert u\right\rVert+\sqrt{b}\left\lVert v\right\rVert\right)^{2}\leq(a+b)\left(\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2}+\left\lVert v\right\rVert^{2}\right),

which proves the operator-norm bound. The factorization also givesX​X†=A1/2​K​B​K†​A1/2⪯‖B‖∞​A,XX^{\dagger}=A^{1/2}KBK^{\dagger}A^{1/2}\preceq\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\infty}A,

and taking traces proves the bound on‖X‖2\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{2}.
∎

## Fact 2.3(Discarding a positive block).

Under the hypotheses of Fact2.2, suppose thatM:=(AXX†B)⪰0.M:=\begin{pmatrix}A&X\\
X^{\dagger}&B\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0.

Then‖M−(A000)‖22≤‖B‖∞​(2​Tr⁡A+Tr⁡B).\left\|M-\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\infty}\bigl(2\operatorname{Tr}A+\operatorname{Tr}B\bigr).(2.14)

In particular, ifTr⁡M=1\operatorname{Tr}M=1, then the right-hand side is at most2​‖B‖∞2\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\infty}.

## Proof.

Block orthogonality in Hilbert–Schmidt inner product gives‖(0XX†B)‖22=2​‖X‖22+‖B‖22.\left\|\begin{pmatrix}0&X\\
X^{\dagger}&B\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{2}^{2}=2\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Since‖B‖22≤‖B‖∞​Tr⁡B\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\infty}\operatorname{Tr}B,
the bound on‖X‖2\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{2}in
(2.13) proves
(2.14).
∎

## 2.4Tensor norms and symmetric tensors

## Injective and projective tensor norms.

Let𝒳\mathcal{X}and𝒴\mathcal{Y}be finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. Forz∈𝒳⊗𝒴z\in\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}, define the injective and projective tensor norms by‖z‖ε\displaystyle\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{\varepsilon}:=sup‖x‖=1‖y‖=1|⟨x⊗y,z⟩|,\displaystyle:={}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\lVert x\right\rVert=1\\
\left\lVert y\right\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\langle x\otimes y,z\rangle\right\rvert,(2.15)‖z‖π\displaystyle\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{\pi}:=inf{∑j|aj|:z=∑jajxj⊗yj,∥xj∥=∥yj∥=1}.\displaystyle:={}\inf\left\{\sum_{j}\left\lvert a_{j}\right\rvert:z=\sum_{j}a_{j}x_{j}\otimes y_{j},\ \left\lVert x_{j}\right\rVert=\left\lVert y_{j}\right\rVert=1\right\}.(2.16)

The unadorned norm‖z‖\left\lVert z\right\rVertremains the Hilbert-space norm. After
choosing orthonormal bases of𝒳\mathcal{X}and𝒴\mathcal{Y}, letZZbe the coefficient
matrix ofzz. The singular-value decomposition, equivalently the
Schmidt decomposition ofzz, gives‖z‖ε=‖Z‖∞,‖z‖=‖Z‖2,‖z‖π=‖Z‖1.\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{\varepsilon}=\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{\infty},\qquad\left\lVert z\right\rVert=\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{2},\qquad\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{\pi}=\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{1}.(2.17)

In particular, ifSchmidt−rank⁡(z)\operatorname{Schmidt-rank}(z)denotes the number
of nonzero Schmidt coefficients ofzz, Schatten-norm duality and
Cauchy–Schwarz give|⟨z,w⟩|≤‖z‖π​‖w‖ε,‖z‖π≤Schmidt−rank⁡(z)​‖z‖.\left\lvert\langle z,w\rangle\right\rvert\leq\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{\pi}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{\varepsilon},\qquad\left\lVert z\right\rVert_{\pi}\leq\sqrt{\operatorname{Schmidt-rank}(z)}\,\left\lVert z\right\rVert.(2.18)

The orthogonal projection ontoSymk⁡ℋ\operatorname{Sym}^{k}\mathcal{H}is the symmetrization
operatorΠsym(k):=1k!​∑π∈𝔖kUπ.\Pi_{\mathrm{sym}}^{(k)}:=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}}U_{\pi}.(2.19)

For a standard treatment of the symmetric subspace, see
Harrow[Har13].

## Orthogonal decompositions.

An orthogonal decomposition of the one-site space induces a
corresponding decomposition of every symmetric tensor power; see, for
example,[FH91, Appendix B, Eq. (B.2)]. We record the precise
form and its number-operator interpretation used in
Section6.

## Fact 2.4(Symmetric power of an orthogonal sum).

Letℋ=ℋ0⊕ℋ1\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{1}be an orthogonal decomposition. For every
integern≥1n\geq 1, there is a natural orthogonal decompositionSymn⁡ℋ≅⨁m=0nSymm⁡(ℋ0)⊗Symn−m⁡(ℋ1).\operatorname{Sym}^{n}\mathcal{H}\cong\bigoplus_{m=0}^{n}\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{n-m}(\mathcal{H}_{1}).(2.20)

IfPPis the projection ontoℋ0\mathcal{H}_{0}, the summand indexed bymmis
the eigenvalue-mmeigenspace of∑j=1nPj\sum_{j=1}^{n}P_{j}onSymn⁡ℋ\operatorname{Sym}^{n}\mathcal{H},
wherePj=I⊗(j−1)⊗P⊗I⊗(n−j).P_{j}=I^{\otimes(j-1)}\otimes P\otimes I^{\otimes(n-j)}.

Forn=2n=2, the decomposition isSym2⁡ℋ=Sym2⁡(ℋ0)⊕(ℋ0∨ℋ1)⊕Sym2⁡(ℋ1),\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathcal{H}=\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{0})\oplus(\mathcal{H}_{0}\vee\mathcal{H}_{1})\oplus\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{1}),(2.21)

whereℋ0∨ℋ1\mathcal{H}_{0}\vee\mathcal{H}_{1}is spanned by the vectorsp∨q:=p⊗q+q⊗p2,p∈ℋ0,q∈ℋ1.p\vee q:=\frac{p\otimes q+q\otimes p}{\sqrt{2}},\qquad p\in\mathcal{H}_{0},\quad q\in\mathcal{H}_{1}.

## Partial inner products.

Fork≥1k\geq 1andu∈ℋu\in\mathcal{H}, define the partial-inner-product mapCu:Symkℋ⟶Symk−1ℋ,Ψ⟼(⟨u|⊗I)Ψ.C_{u}:\operatorname{Sym}^{k}\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sym}^{k-1}\mathcal{H},\qquad\Psi\longmapsto(\langle u\rvert\otimes I)\Psi.

Fork≥2k\geq 2andϕ∈Sym2⁡ℋ\phi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathcal{H}, similarly defineCϕ:Symkℋ⟶Symk−2ℋ,Ψ⟼(⟨ϕ|⊗I)Ψ.C_{\phi}:\operatorname{Sym}^{k}\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sym}^{k-2}\mathcal{H},\qquad\Psi\longmapsto(\langle\phi\rvert\otimes I)\Psi.

HereIIacts on the remaining tensor factors, and the symmetric
degree will always be clear from context. WhenCuC_{u}acts on a tensor
of symmetric degree zero, we set it equal to zero.

Equivalent calculations in the standard bosonic formalism appear
in[LNR15, Sec. 4]; we use only partial-inner-product notation
here.

## Fact 2.5(Partial inner products and marginals).

ForΞ∈Symk−1⁡ℋ\Xi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{k-1}\mathcal{H}, the adjoint ofCuC_{u}isCu∗​(Ξ)=Πsym(k)​(u⊗Ξ)=1k​∑j=1kU1​j​(u⊗Ξ),C_{u}^{*}(\Xi)=\Pi_{\mathrm{sym}}^{(k)}(u\otimes\Xi)=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}U_{1j}(u\otimes\Xi),(2.22)

whereU1​jU_{1j}swaps the first andjjth tensor factors, withU11=IU_{11}=I. Moreover, ifk≥2k\geq 2,Ψ∈Symk⁡ℋ\Psi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{k}\mathcal{H}is a unit vector, andρ1,ρ2\rho_{1},\rho_{2}are the one- and two-site marginals of|Ψ⟩⟨Ψ|\lvert\Psi\rangle\langle\Psi\rvert, then⟨u|ρ1|u⟩=∥Cu(Ψ)∥2,⟨ϕ|ρ2|ϕ⟩=∥Cϕ(Ψ)∥2.\langle u\rvert\rho_{1}\lvert u\rangle=\left\lVert C_{u}(\Psi)\right\rVert^{2},\qquad\langle\phi\rvert\rho_{2}\lvert\phi\rangle=\left\lVert C_{\phi}(\Psi)\right\rVert^{2}.(2.23)

## Fact 2.6(Bounds for symmetric partial inner products).

For everyu∈ℋu\in\mathcal{H}andΞ∈Symk−1⁡ℋ\Xi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{k-1}\mathcal{H},‖Cu∗​(Ξ)‖2=1k​(‖u‖2​‖Ξ‖2+(k−1)​‖Cu​(Ξ)‖2).\left\lVert C_{u}^{*}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}=\frac{1}{k}\left(\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\Xi\right\rVert^{2}+(k-1)\left\lVert C_{u}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}\right).(2.24)

For every orthonormal family(ui)(u_{i})and every symmetric tensorΨ\Psi,∑i‖Cui​(Ψ)‖2≤‖Ψ‖2.\sum_{i}\left\lVert C_{u_{i}}(\Psi)\right\rVert^{2}\leq\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert^{2}.(2.25)

Consequently, ifu1,…,uℓu_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell}are orthonormal, then∑i=1ℓ‖Cui∗​(Ξ)‖2≤ℓ+k−1k​‖Ξ‖2.\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\left\lVert C_{u_{i}}^{*}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{\ell+k-1}{k}\left\lVert\Xi\right\rVert^{2}.(2.26)

In particular, the right-hand side is at most2​‖Ξ‖22\left\lVert\Xi\right\rVert^{2}whenℓ≤k+1\ell\leq k+1.

## Proof.

The casek=1k=1of (2.24) is immediate.
Fork≥2k\geq 2, expanding the symmetrizer in
(2.22) gives‖Cu∗​(Ξ)‖2\displaystyle\left\lVert C_{u}^{*}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}=1k​∑j=1k⟨u⊗Ξ,U1​j​(u⊗Ξ)⟩\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left\langle u\otimes\Xi,U_{1j}(u\otimes\Xi)\right\rangle=1k​(‖u‖2​‖Ξ‖2+(k−1)​‖Cu​(Ξ)‖2).\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\left(\left\lVert u\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\Xi\right\rVert^{2}+(k-1)\left\lVert C_{u}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}\right).

Indeed, symmetry ofΞ\Ximakes the terms withj≥2j\geq 2equal, and
their common value is⟨Ξ,(|u⟩⟨u|⊗I)Ξ⟩=∥Cu(Ξ)∥2\langle\Xi,(\lvert u\rangle\langle u\rvert\otimes I)\Xi\rangle=\left\lVert C_{u}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}. This proves
(2.24).

For (2.25), letPU:=∑i|ui⟩⟨ui|P_{U}:=\sum_{i}\lvert u_{i}\rangle\langle u_{i}\rvertbe the orthogonal projection onto the span
of the orthonormal family(ui)(u_{i}). Then∑i‖Cui​(Ψ)‖2\displaystyle\sum_{i}\left\lVert C_{u_{i}}(\Psi)\right\rVert^{2}=∑i⟨Ψ,(|ui⟩⟨ui|⊗I)Ψ⟩\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left\langle\Psi,\bigl(\lvert u_{i}\rangle\langle u_{i}\rvert\otimes I\bigr)\Psi\right\rangle=⟨Ψ,(PU⊗I)​Ψ⟩\displaystyle=\left\langle\Psi,(P_{U}\otimes I)\Psi\right\rangle≤‖Ψ‖2,\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Psi\right\rVert^{2},

because0⪯PU⪯I0\preceq P_{U}\preceq I. Summing
(2.24) overu1,…,uℓu_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell}and
applying (2.25) toΞ\Xigives
(2.26).
∎

## 3Two-site de Finetti upper bounds

We first prove the bosonic two-site bound by argmax rounding.
Symmetric purification gives the exchangeable bound. Rectangular
argmax rounding gives the two-sided Bose-symmetric bound, and a second
symmetric purification removes the Bose-symmetry assumption.

## 3.1Bosonic states: the argmax principle

## Theorem 3.1(Bosonic two-site de Finetti upper bound).

For everyN,d≥2N,d\geq 2,DHtr​(BOSN(2)⁡(d),SEPH(2)⁡(d))≤d−12​(N−1).D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(2)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)\Bigr)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{2(N-1)}.(3.1)

To prove the theorem, associate with eachM∈Herm⁡(Sym2⁡(ℋ))M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}))the quantitiesaMa_{M},h​(M)h(M), and the
normalizedNN-site liftM[N]M^{[N]}defined byaM​(u)\displaystyle a_{M}(u):=⟨u⊗2|M|u⊗2⟩,\displaystyle:=\langle u^{\otimes 2}\rvert M\lvert u^{\otimes 2}\rangle,(3.2)h​(M)\displaystyle h(M):=max‖u‖=1⁡aM​(u),\displaystyle:=\max_{\left\lVert u\right\rVert=1}a_{M}(u),(3.3)M[N]\displaystyle M^{[N]}:=(N2)−1​∑1≤i<j≤NMi​jon​SymN⁡(ℋ).\displaystyle:=\binom{N}{2}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}M_{ij}\quad\text{on }\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathcal{H}).(3.4)

HereMi​jM_{ij}acts asMMon sitesi,ji,jand as the identity elsewhere. Since every two-site marginal of a bosonic state is supported onSym2⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}), no extension ofMMoutside that subspace is needed.

The proof has three steps. First, trace-norm duality identifies the de
Finetti error with the largest gapλmax​(M[N])−h​(M)\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]})-h(M)over bounded HermitianMM. Next, given
a top eigenvectorψ\psiofM[N]M^{[N]}, the argmax principle chooses as
the rounding object a unit vectoruumaximizing|⟨u⊗N,ψ⟩|\left\lvert\langle u^{\otimes N},\psi\rangle\right\rvert. The complex-sphere argmax lemma
provides the basic tools for analyzing this rounding object. After
taking the partial inner product withuuon the otherN−2N-2sites,
first-order optimality eliminates the mixed component of the resulting
two-site vector, while second-order optimality controls its component
inSym2⁡(u⟂)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(u^{\perp}). Finally, Takagi decomposition converts this
structural information into the integrality-gap boundλmax​(M[N])−h​(M)≤d−1N−1​‖M‖∞.\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]})-h(M)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{N-1}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}.

## 3.1.1An exact dual problem

Trace-norm duality reduces the approximation problem to a gap detected
by a bounded Hermitian witnessMM. Its maximum over Hartree states ish​(M)h(M), whereas its maximum over two-site marginals of bosonicNN-site states isλmax​(M[N])\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]}). The following lemma makes
this reduction exact.

## Lemma 3.2(Exact trace-norm duality).

For everyN,d≥2N,d\geq 2,DHtr​(BOSN(2)⁡(d),SEPH(2)⁡(d))=12​supM∈Herm⁡(Sym2⁡ℋ)‖M‖∞≤1{λmax​(M[N])−h​(M)}.D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(2)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)\Bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathcal{H})\\
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\{\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]})-h(M)\right\}.(3.5)

## Proof.

The support function of the Hartree mixtures ishSEPH(2)⁡(d)​(M)=h​(M).h_{\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)}(M)=h(M).

For the bosonic marginal set, permutation symmetry giveshBOSN(2)⁡(d)​(M)=maxρN∈D​(SymN⁡ℋ)⁡Tr⁡(M[N]​ρN)=λmax​(M[N]).h_{\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(2)}(d)}(M)=\max_{\rho_{N}\in\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{Sym}^{N}\mathcal{H})}\operatorname{Tr}(M^{[N]}\rho_{N})=\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]}).

By duality (Lemma2.1), these two
identities prove (3.5).
∎

## 3.1.2The argmax rounding object

The tensor-power vectors{u⊗N:u∈ℋ}\{u^{\otimes N}:u\in\mathcal{H}\}spanSymN⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathcal{H}); see also[Har13, Theorem 3]. Thus, for every
nonzeroψ∈SymN⁡(ℋ)\psi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathcal{H}), the argmax principle gives a natural
rounding object: chooseu∈arg​max‖v‖=1⁡|⟨v⊗N,ψ⟩|,u\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\left\lVert v\right\rVert=1}\left\lvert\langle v^{\otimes N},\psi\rangle\right\rvert,

and useu⊗Nu^{\otimes N}as the rounding object.
Fix a unit vectorψ∈SymN⁡(ℋ)\psi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathcal{H}), and letu⊗Nu^{\otimes N}be this
argmax rounding object. After adjusting the phase ofuu, setc:=⟨u⊗N,ψ⟩>0.c:=\langle u^{\otimes N},\psi\rangle>0.(3.6)

Taking the partial inner product withuuonN−2N-2sites givesηu:=(⟨u|⊗(N−2)⊗Iℋ⊗2)ψ∈Sym2(ℋ).\eta_{u}:=(\langle u\rvert^{\otimes(N-2)}\otimes I_{\mathcal{H}^{\otimes 2}})\psi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}).(3.7)

Writeu∨u⟂:=span{u⊗v+v⊗u:v⟂u}⊆Sym2(ℋ).u\vee u^{\perp}:=\operatorname{span}\{u\otimes v+v\otimes u:v\perp u\}\subseteq\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}).

The next lemma provides the basic tools for analyzing this rounding
object. It is the bosonic counterpart of the reweighted
pseudo-covariance lemma underlying our SoS argmax
rounding[JWX26]. Hereηu\eta_{u}plays the role of the reweighted
pseudo-covariance. The mechanism is concrete:
first-order optimality eliminates its mixedu∨u⟂u\vee u^{\perp}component,
while second-order optimality bounds the remainingSym2⁡(u⟂)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(u^{\perp})component against every squarev⊗2v^{\otimes 2}.

## Lemma 3.3(Complex-sphere argmax estimate).

With the notation above,ηu=c​u⊗2+ζu,ζu∈Sym2⁡(u⟂),\eta_{u}=c\,u^{\otimes 2}+\zeta_{u},\qquad\zeta_{u}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(u^{\perp}),(3.8)

andsupv⟂u‖v‖=1|⟨v⊗2,ζu⟩|≤cN−1.\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\perp u\\
\left\lVert v\right\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\langle v^{\otimes 2},\zeta_{u}\rangle\right\rvert\leq\frac{c}{N-1}.(3.9)

## Proof.

Fix a unit vectorv⟂uv\perp uand, fort∈ℂt\in\mathbb{C}, setut:=u+t​v1+|t|2,g​(t):=⟨ut⊗N,ψ⟩.u_{t}:=\frac{u+tv}{\sqrt{1+\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}}},\qquad g(t):=\langle u_{t}^{\otimes N},\psi\rangle.

By the choice ofuu, the function|g​(t)|2\left\lvert g(t)\right\rvert^{2}is maximized att=0t=0. For0≤k≤N0\leq k\leq N, writeγk:=⟨v⊗k⊗u⊗(N−k),ψ⟩.\gamma_{k}:=\langle v^{\otimes k}\otimes u^{\otimes(N-k)},\psi\rangle.

Symmetry ofψ\psigives the exact expansiong​(t)=(1+|t|2)−N/2​∑k=0N(Nk)​t¯k​γk.g(t)=(1+\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2})^{-N/2}\sum_{k=0}^{N}\binom{N}{k}\overline{t}^{\,k}\gamma_{k}.

In particular,γ0=c\gamma_{0}=c. Setα\displaystyle\alpha:=γ1=⟨v⊗u⊗(N−1),ψ⟩,\displaystyle:=\gamma_{1}=\langle v\otimes u^{\otimes(N-1)},\psi\rangle,β\displaystyle\beta:=γ2=⟨v⊗2⊗u⊗(N−2),ψ⟩.\displaystyle:=\gamma_{2}=\langle v^{\otimes 2}\otimes u^{\otimes(N-2)},\psi\rangle.

The exact expansion yieldsg​(t)=(1+|t|2)−N/2​(c+N​t¯​α+(N2)​t¯2​β+O​(|t|3)).g(t)=(1+\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2})^{-N/2}\left(c+N\overline{t}\,\alpha+\binom{N}{2}\overline{t}^{\,2}\beta+O(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{3})\right).(3.10)

The normalization factor has no linear term, and hence the first-order
term in|g​(t)|2\left\lvert g(t)\right\rvert^{2}is2​N​c​ℜ⁡(t¯​α).2Nc\,\Re(\overline{t}\alpha).

To see why this forcesα=0\alpha=0, fix anyξ∈ℂ\xi\in\mathbb{C}and restrict to
the real one-parameter curvet=r​ξt=r\xi,r∈ℝr\in\mathbb{R}. Sincet=0t=0is a

Thus0=dd​r​|g​(r​ξ)|2|r=0=2​N​c​ℜ⁡(ξ¯​α).0=\left.\frac{d}{dr}\left\lvert g(r\xi)\right\rvert^{2}\right|_{r=0}=2Nc\,\Re(\overline{\xi}\alpha).

Takingξ=1\xi=1givesℜ⁡(α)=0\Re(\alpha)=0, while takingξ=i\xi=igivesℑ⁡(α)=0\Im(\alpha)=0. Thereforeα=0\alpha=0. Keeping the normalization
factor explicit in the first line and then Taylor-expanding it in the
second, we obtain|g​(t)|2\displaystyle\left\lvert g(t)\right\rvert^{2}=(1+|t|2)−N​(c2+2​(N2)​c​ℜ⁡(t¯2​β)+O​(|t|3))\displaystyle=(1+\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2})^{-N}\left(c^{2}+2\binom{N}{2}c\,\Re(\overline{t}^{\,2}\beta)+O(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{3})\right)=(1−N​|t|2+O​(|t|4))​(c2+2​(N2)​c​ℜ⁡(t¯2​β)+O​(|t|3))\displaystyle=\left(1-N\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}+O(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{4})\right)\left(c^{2}+2\binom{N}{2}c\,\Re(\overline{t}^{\,2}\beta)+O(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{3})\right)=c2−N​c2​|t|2+2​(N2)​c​ℜ⁡(t¯2​β)+O​(|t|3).\displaystyle=c^{2}-Nc^{2}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}+2\binom{N}{2}c\,\Re(\overline{t}^{\,2}\beta)+O(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{3}).(3.11)

Here we used(1+|t|2)−N=1−N​|t|2+O​(|t|4)(1+\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2})^{-N}=1-N\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}+O(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{4}). Its product with the
constant termc2c^{2}contributes−N​c2​|t|2-Nc^{2}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}. Its products with terms of order|t|2\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}or
higher areO​(|t|4)O(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{4})and are therefore absorbed into theO​(|t|3)O(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{3})remainder.

Now writet=r​ei​θt=re^{i\theta}and chooseθ\thetaso thate−2​i​θ​β=|β|e^{-2i\theta}\beta=\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert. Along this real one-parameter
perturbation,|g​(r​ei​θ)|2=c2+(−N​c2+2​(N2)​c​|β|)​r2+O​(r3).\left\lvert g(re^{i\theta})\right\rvert^{2}=c^{2}+\left(-Nc^{2}+2\binom{N}{2}c\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\right)r^{2}+O(r^{3}).

Sincer=0r=0is a local maximum, the quadratic coefficient is
nonpositive:2​(N2)​c​|β|≤N​c2.2\binom{N}{2}c\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\leq Nc^{2}.

Therefore|β|≤c/(N−1)\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\leq c/(N-1).

It remains to translate these variational conditions into a statement
aboutηu\eta_{u}. The orthogonal decompositionSym2⁡(ℋ)=ℂ​u⊗2⊕(u∨u⟂)⊕Sym2⁡(u⟂)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H})=\mathbb{C}u^{\otimes 2}\oplus(u\vee u^{\perp})\oplus\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(u^{\perp})

is adapted to this partial inner product. By definition,⟨u⊗2,ηu⟩=c,⟨v⊗u,ηu⟩=α=0(v⟂u).\langle u^{\otimes 2},\eta_{u}\rangle=c,\qquad\langle v\otimes u,\eta_{u}\rangle=\alpha=0\quad(v\perp u).

Sinceηu\eta_{u}is symmetric, the second identity eliminates its entireu∨u⟂u\vee u^{\perp}component. Thusηu=c​u⊗2+ζu\eta_{u}=c\,u^{\otimes 2}+\zeta_{u}for someζu∈Sym2⁡(u⟂)\zeta_{u}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(u^{\perp}). Finally, for every unitv⟂uv\perp u,⟨v⊗2,ζu⟩=⟨v⊗2,ηu⟩=β,\langle v^{\otimes 2},\zeta_{u}\rangle=\langle v^{\otimes 2},\eta_{u}\rangle=\beta,

so the bound onβ\betais exactly (3.9).
∎

## 3.1.3Bounding the integrality gap

We now use the information supplied by the argmax lemma to bound the
integrality gap. The remaining ingredient is the Takagi
decomposition, the symmetric form of the singular-value, or Schmidt,
decomposition. It says that a symmetric two-tensor can be written asζ=∑isi​ui⊗2,\zeta=\sum_{i}s_{i}u_{i}^{\otimes 2},

where theuiu_{i}are orthonormal andsi≥0s_{i}\geq 0. This form identifies
the relevant tensor norms with matrix Schatten norms.

To record the corresponding matrix statement, letℋ\mathcal{H}be anmm-dimensional complex Hilbert space with an orthonormal basis(ei)i=1m(e_{i})_{i=1}^{m}. Everyζ∈Sym2⁡(ℋ)\zeta\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H})has a unique representationζ=∑i,j=1mZi​j​ei⊗ej,Z𝖳=Z.\zeta=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}Z_{ij}e_{i}\otimes e_{j},\qquad Z^{\mathsf{T}}=Z.(3.12)

We writeZ=Mat⁡(ζ)Z=\operatorname{Mat}(\zeta). AlthoughZZneed
not be Hermitian, its complex symmetry implies a Takagi
decomposition[HJ12, Cor. 4.4.4]Z=U​Σ​U𝖳,Z=U\Sigma U^{\mathsf{T}},(3.13)

whereUUis unitary andΣ\Sigmais diagonal with nonnegative entries.
The diagonal entries ofΣ\Sigmaare the singular values ofZZ. A
change of orthonormal basis replacesZZby a unitary congruence, so its
Schatten norms are basis independent.

The injective and projective tensor norms from
Section2.4have especially simple descriptions onSym2⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}). Namely, Takagi decomposition shows that they may be
computed using only symmetric rank-one tensors.

## Fact 3.4(Takagi decomposition and symmetric tensor norms).

For everyζ∈Sym2⁡(ℋ)\zeta\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}),‖ζ‖ε\displaystyle\left\lVert\zeta\right\rVert_{\varepsilon}=sup‖v‖=1|⟨v⊗2,ζ⟩|=‖Mat⁡(ζ)‖∞,\displaystyle=\sup_{\left\lVert v\right\rVert=1}\left\lvert\langle v^{\otimes 2},\zeta\rangle\right\rvert=\left\lVert\operatorname{Mat}(\zeta)\right\rVert_{\infty},(3.14)‖ζ‖π\displaystyle\left\lVert\zeta\right\rVert_{\pi}=inf{∑k|ak|:ζ=∑kakvk⊗2,∥vk∥=1}=∥Mat(ζ)∥1.\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\sum_{k}\left\lvert a_{k}\right\rvert:\zeta=\sum_{k}a_{k}v_{k}^{\otimes 2},\ \left\lVert v_{k}\right\rVert=1\right\}=\left\lVert\operatorname{Mat}(\zeta)\right\rVert_{1}.(3.15)

## Proof.

WriteZ=U​Σ​U𝖳Z=U\Sigma U^{\mathsf{T}}as in
(3.13). Our inner-product convention gives⟨v⊗2,ζ⟩=v¯𝖳​Z​v¯.\langle v^{\otimes 2},\zeta\rangle=\overline{v}^{\mathsf{T}}Z\overline{v}.

Asvvranges over the unit sphere, so doesy=U𝖳​v¯y=U^{\mathsf{T}}\overline{v}. Hence|⟨v⊗2,ζ⟩|=|∑iΣi​i​yi2|≤∑iΣi​i​|yi|2≤maxi⁡Σi​i.\left\lvert\langle v^{\otimes 2},\zeta\rangle\right\rvert=\left\lvert\sum_{i}\Sigma_{ii}y_{i}^{2}\right\rvert\leq\sum_{i}\Sigma_{ii}\left\lvert y_{i}\right\rvert^{2}\leq\max_{i}\Sigma_{ii}.

Takingyyto be a coordinate vector corresponding to the largest
diagonal entry shows that the supremum over squares equals‖Z‖∞\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{\infty}. By (2.17), this is precisely‖ζ‖ε\left\lVert\zeta\right\rVert_{\varepsilon}, proving (3.14).

Ifuiu_{i}are the columns ofUU, the Takagi decomposition givesζ=∑iΣi​i​ui⊗2,\zeta=\sum_{i}\Sigma_{ii}u_{i}^{\otimes 2},

so the infimum over square decompositions is at most∑iΣi​i=‖Z‖1=‖ζ‖π\sum_{i}\Sigma_{ii}=\left\lVert Z\right\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert\zeta\right\rVert_{\pi}. Conversely, every
square decomposition is admissible in the definition of the ordinary
projective norm (2.16), so the infimum is at
least‖ζ‖π\left\lVert\zeta\right\rVert_{\pi}. This proves
(3.15).
∎

ForM∈Herm⁡(Sym2⁡(ℂd))M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})), defineτ​(M):=sup‖u‖=1‖PSym2⁡(u⟂)​M​u⊗2‖π,\tau(M):=\sup_{\left\lVert u\right\rVert=1}\left\lVert P_{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(u^{\perp})}Mu^{\otimes 2}\right\rVert_{\pi},(3.16)

where the projective norm is taken across the two copies ofu⟂u^{\perp}.

## Proposition 3.5(Integrality gap from argmax rounding).

For everyN,d≥2N,d\geq 2and everyM∈Herm⁡(Sym2⁡(ℂd))M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{d})),λmax​(M[N])−h​(M)≤τ​(M)N−1.\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]})-h(M)\leq\frac{\tau(M)}{N-1}.(3.17)

Consequently,λmax​(M[N])−h​(M)≤d−1N−1​‖M‖∞.\lambda_{\max}(M^{[N]})-h(M)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{N-1}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}.(3.18)

## Proof.

Letψ∈SymN⁡(ℋ)\psi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathcal{H})be a unit top eigenvector ofM[N]M^{[N]}, with
eigenvalueλ\lambda. Apply the argmax principle toψ\psi, and letu⊗Nu^{\otimes N}be the resulting rounding object, withccandηu\eta_{u}as
in Lemma3.3. Since bothu⊗Nu^{\otimes N}andψ\psiare symmetric,λ​c\displaystyle\lambda c=⟨u⊗N,M[N]​ψ⟩=⟨u⊗N,M12​ψ⟩=⟨M​u⊗2,ηu⟩.\displaystyle=\langle u^{\otimes N},M^{[N]}\psi\rangle=\langle u^{\otimes N},M_{12}\psi\rangle=\langle Mu^{\otimes 2},\eta_{u}\rangle.(3.19)

Decompose orthogonallyM​u⊗2=aM​(u)​u⊗2+ξu+Bu,ξu∈u∨u⟂,Bu∈Sym2⁡(u⟂).Mu^{\otimes 2}=a_{M}(u)u^{\otimes 2}+\xi_{u}+B_{u},\qquad\xi_{u}\in u\vee u^{\perp},\quad B_{u}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(u^{\perp}).(3.20)

Combining (3.19) and (3.8) with
the orthogonal decomposition (3.20) gives(λ−aM​(u))​c\displaystyle(\lambda-a_{M}(u))c=⟨M​u⊗2,ηu⟩−aM​(u)​c\displaystyle=\langle Mu^{\otimes 2},\eta_{u}\rangle-a_{M}(u)c=⟨aM​(u)​u⊗2+ξu+Bu,c​u⊗2+ζu⟩−aM​(u)​c\displaystyle=\langle a_{M}(u)u^{\otimes 2}+\xi_{u}+B_{u},c\,u^{\otimes 2}+\zeta_{u}\rangle-a_{M}(u)c=⟨Bu,ζu⟩.\displaystyle=\langle B_{u},\zeta_{u}\rangle.(3.21)

Lemma3.3and (3.14) imply‖ζu‖ε≤cN−1.\left\lVert\zeta_{u}\right\rVert_{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{c}{N-1}.

Taking absolute values in (3.21), using
(2.18), and dividing byc>0c>0gives|λ−aM​(u)|≤‖Bu‖πN−1≤τ​(M)N−1.\left\lvert\lambda-a_{M}(u)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\left\lVert B_{u}\right\rVert_{\pi}}{N-1}\leq\frac{\tau(M)}{N-1}.

BecauseaM​(u)≤h​(M)a_{M}(u)\leq h(M),λ−h​(M)≤λ−aM​(u)≤|λ−aM​(u)|,\lambda-h(M)\leq\lambda-a_{M}(u)\leq\left\lvert\lambda-a_{M}(u)\right\rvert,

which proves (3.17).

Choose an orthonormal basis ofu⟂u^{\perp}, and letMatu⁡(Bu)\operatorname{Mat}_{u}(B_{u})be the
corresponding complex symmetric matrix from (3.12).
Its Schatten norms are independent of this choice. By
(3.15), and becauseMatu⁡(Bu)\operatorname{Mat}_{u}(B_{u})has size(d−1)×(d−1)(d-1)\times(d-1),‖Bu‖π\displaystyle\left\lVert B_{u}\right\rVert_{\pi}=‖Matu⁡(Bu)‖1\displaystyle=\left\lVert\operatorname{Mat}_{u}(B_{u})\right\rVert_{1}≤d−1​‖Matu⁡(Bu)‖2\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{d-1}\left\lVert\operatorname{Mat}_{u}(B_{u})\right\rVert_{2}=d−1​‖Bu‖\displaystyle=\sqrt{d-1}\left\lVert B_{u}\right\rVert≤d−1​‖M‖∞.\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{d-1}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}.

Taking the supremum overuugives (3.18).
∎

## Proof of Theorem3.1.

Insert (3.18) into (3.5). This givesDHtr​(BOSN(2)⁡(d),SEPH(2)⁡(d))≤d−12​(N−1).∎D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(2)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)\Bigr)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{2(N-1)}.\qed

## Remark 3.6.

The dimension factor enters only through the inequality‖ξ‖π≤d−1​‖ξ‖\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{\pi}\leq\sqrt{d-1}\left\lVert\xi\right\rVertforξ∈Sym2⁡(u⟂)\xi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(u^{\perp}). Thus the stronger estimate
(3.17) is dimension independent for classes of
Hermitian operators
satisfyingτ​(M)=O​(‖M‖∞)\tau(M)=O(\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}).

## 3.1.4Exchangeable states via symmetric purification

## Corollary 3.7(Exchangeable two-site de Finetti upper bound).

For everyN,d≥2N,d\geq 2,DHtr​(EXCHN(2)⁡(d),SEPP(2)⁡(d))≤d2−12​(N−1).D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(2)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(2)}(d)\Bigr)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d^{2}-1}}{2(N-1)}.(3.22)

The corollary follows from Theorem3.1through the
standard symmetric-purification lemma of Christandl, König,
Mitchison, and Renner[CKMR07, Lemma II.5].

## Lemma 3.8(Symmetric purification).

LetσN\sigma_{N}be exchangeable onℋ⊗N\mathcal{H}^{\otimes N}, wheredimℋ=d\dim\mathcal{H}=d. There is a Hilbert spaceℋE≅ℋ\mathcal{H}_{E}\cong\mathcal{H}and a unit vectorψN∈SymN⁡(ℋE⊗ℋ)\psi_{N}\in\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathcal{H}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{H})

such thatTrℋE⊗N|ψN⟩⟨ψN|=σN.\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{E}^{\otimes N}}\lvert\psi_{N}\rangle\langle\psi_{N}\rvert=\sigma_{N}.

## Proof of Corollary3.7.

Apply Lemma3.8and setρ~N=|ψN⟩⟨ψN|\widetilde{\rho}_{N}=\lvert\psi_{N}\rangle\langle\psi_{N}\rvert. This is a bosonic state with
one-site dimensiond2d^{2}. Theorem3.1gives a
probability measureμ\muon unit vectorsϕ∈ℋE⊗ℋ\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{H}such
thatDtr(ρ~N(2),∫|ϕ⟩⟨ϕ|⊗2dμ(ϕ))≤d2−12​(N−1).D_{\mathrm{tr}}\left(\widetilde{\rho}_{N}^{(2)},\int\lvert\phi\rangle\langle\phi\rvert^{\otimes 2}\,d\mu(\phi)\right)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d^{2}-1}}{2(N-1)}.

For eachϕ\phi, defineτϕ=TrℋE|ϕ⟩⟨ϕ|∈D(ℋ)\tau_{\phi}=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{E}}\lvert\phi\rangle\langle\phi\rvert\in\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{H}). Sitewise partial trace
maps|ϕ⟩⟨ϕ|⊗2\lvert\phi\rangle\langle\phi\rvert^{\otimes 2}toτϕ⊗2\tau_{\phi}^{\otimes 2}, so contractivity givesDtr​(σN(2),∫τϕ⊗2​𝑑μ​(ϕ))≤d2−12​(N−1).D_{\mathrm{tr}}\left(\sigma_{N}^{(2)},\int\tau_{\phi}^{\otimes 2}\,d\mu(\phi)\right)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d^{2}-1}}{2(N-1)}.

The approximating state belongs toSEPP(2)⁡(d)\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(2)}(d), which proves
(3.22).
∎

## 3.2Two-sided extendibility

## Theorem 3.9(De Finetti upper bound for two-sided Bose-symmetric
extendible states).

For everyn,m,dA,dB≥1n,m,d_{A},d_{B}\geq 1,DHtr​(BEXTn,m⁡(dA,dB),SEP⁡(dA,dB))≤12​min⁡{dA,dB}−1n​m.D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B}),\,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\Bigr)\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}{nm}}.(3.23)

The proof follows the same pattern as the bosonic argument above:
rectangular argmax rounding bounds the integrality gap, and the same
estimate will also be used for higher marginals in
Section4. Trace-distance duality then gives
the theorem, while a two-block symmetric purification removes the
Bose-symmetry assumption.

## 3.2.1Rectangular argmax rounding

LetℋA\mathcal{H}_{A}andℋB\mathcal{H}_{B}be finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, and writedA:=dimℋAd_{A}:=\dim\mathcal{H}_{A}anddB:=dimℋBd_{B}:=\dim\mathcal{H}_{B}. ForM∈Herm⁡(ℋA⊗ℋB)M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}), defineaM​(x,y)\displaystyle a_{M}(x,y):=⟨x⊗y|M|x⊗y⟩,\displaystyle:=\langle x\otimes y\rvert M\lvert x\otimes y\rangle,(3.24)hSEP​(M)\displaystyle h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M):=max‖x‖=1‖y‖=1⁡aM​(x,y).\displaystyle:=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\lVert x\right\rVert=1\\
\left\lVert y\right\rVert=1\end{subarray}}a_{M}(x,y).(3.25)

SinceSEP⁡(dA,dB)\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})is the convex hull of pure product states,hSEP​(M)=maxσ∈SEP⁡(dA,dB)⁡Tr⁡(M​σ).h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)=\max_{\sigma\in\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})}\operatorname{Tr}(M\sigma).(3.26)

Forn,m≥1n,m\geq 1, its normalized rectangular lift isM[n,m]:=1n​m​∑i=1n∑j=1mMAi​Bjon​Symn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symm⁡(ℋB).M^{[n,m]}:=\frac{1}{nm}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}M_{A_{i}B_{j}}\quad\text{on }\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{B}).(3.27)

HereMAi​BjM_{A_{i}B_{j}}acts asMMonAi​BjA_{i}B_{j}and as the identity on all
other registers.
Ifωn,m∈D​(Symn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symm⁡(ℋB))\omega_{n,m}\in\mathrm{D}\bigl(\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{B})\bigr),
permutation symmetry within theAA- andBB-blocks givesTr⁡(M[n,m]​ωn,m)=Tr⁡(M​(ωn,m)A1​B1).\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M^{[n,m]}\omega_{n,m}\bigr)=\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M(\omega_{n,m})_{A_{1}B_{1}}\bigr).(3.28)

Given a unit top eigenvector ofM[n,m]M^{[n,m]}, rectangular argmax rounding
selects a product vector from the corresponding overlap maximization.
The following estimate is the direct rectangular analogue of
Proposition3.5. Its proof is deferred to
AppendixA.

## Proposition 3.10(Rectangular argmax estimate).

LetM∈Herm⁡(ℋA⊗ℋB)M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})andn,m≥1n,m\geq 1, and letψ\psibe a unit
top eigenvector ofM[n,m]M^{[n,m]}. Choose the argmax rounding objectx⊗yx\otimes yby(x,y)∈arg​maxv∈ℋA,w∈ℋB‖v‖=‖w‖=1⁡|⟨v⊗n⊗w⊗m,ψ⟩|.(x,y)\in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{H}_{A},\ w\in\mathcal{H}_{B}\\
\left\lVert v\right\rVert=\left\lVert w\right\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\left\lvert\langle v^{\otimes n}\otimes w^{\otimes m},\psi\rangle\right\rvert.(3.29)

Thenλmax​(M[n,m])−aM​(x,y)≤1n​m​‖(Px⟂⊗Py⟂)​M​(x⊗y)‖π.\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M^{[n,m]}\bigr)-a_{M}(x,y)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{nm}}\left\lVert(P_{x^{\perp}}\otimes P_{y^{\perp}})M(x\otimes y)\right\rVert_{\pi}.(3.30)

As in the symmetric case, the pointwise estimate immediately gives an
integrality-gap bound. Defineτ​(M):=sup‖x‖=1‖y‖=1‖(Px⟂⊗Py⟂)​M​(x⊗y)‖π.\tau(M):=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\lVert x\right\rVert=1\\
\left\lVert y\right\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\left\lVert(P_{x^{\perp}}\otimes P_{y^{\perp}})M(x\otimes y)\right\rVert_{\pi}.(3.31)

## Corollary 3.11(Integrality gap from rectangular argmax rounding).

For everyM∈Herm⁡(ℋA⊗ℋB)M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})andn,m≥1n,m\geq 1,λmax​(M[n,m])−hSEP​(M)≤τ​(M)n​m,\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M^{[n,m]}\bigr)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\leq\frac{\tau(M)}{\sqrt{nm}},(3.32)

and henceλmax​(M[n,m])−hSEP​(M)≤min⁡{dimℋA,dimℋB}−1n​m​‖M‖∞.\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M^{[n,m]}\bigr)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\leq\sqrt{\frac{\min\{\dim\mathcal{H}_{A},\dim\mathcal{H}_{B}\}-1}{nm}}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}.(3.33)

## Proof.

Apply Proposition3.10and use the definitions

(2.18) gives‖(Px⟂⊗Py⟂)​M​(x⊗y)‖π\displaystyle\left\lVert(P_{x^{\perp}}\otimes P_{y^{\perp}})M(x\otimes y)\right\rVert_{\pi}≤min⁡{dimℋA,dimℋB}−1​‖(Px⟂⊗Py⟂)​M​(x⊗y)‖\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\min\{\dim\mathcal{H}_{A},\dim\mathcal{H}_{B}\}-1}\left\lVert(P_{x^{\perp}}\otimes P_{y^{\perp}})M(x\otimes y)\right\rVert≤min⁡{dimℋA,dimℋB}−1​‖M‖∞.\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\min\{\dim\mathcal{H}_{A},\dim\mathcal{H}_{B}\}-1}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}.

## 3.2.2Two-sided de Finetti bounds

The rectangular gap immediately gives a de Finetti bound for arbitrary

Fixρ∈BEXTn,m⁡(dA,dB)\rho\in\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B})and choose an extensionρ~\widetilde{\rho}from (2.3).
By duality (Lemma2.1),Dtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB))=12​maxM∈Herm⁡(ℋA⊗ℋB)‖M‖∞≤1⁡{Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hSEP​(M)}.D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})\\
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\right\}.(3.34)

(3.28) and
Corollary3.11giveTr⁡(M​ρ)−hSEP​(M)\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)=Tr⁡(M[n,m]​ρ~)−hSEP​(M)\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M^{[n,m]}\widetilde{\rho}\bigr)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)≤λmax​(M[n,m])−hSEP​(M)\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M^{[n,m]}\bigr)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)≤min⁡{dA,dB}−1n​m​‖M‖∞.\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}{nm}}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}.(3.35)

Inserting this estimate into (3.34) and taking

We now remove the Bose-symmetry assumption by symmetric purification.

For everyn,m,dA,dB≥1n,m,d_{A},d_{B}\geq 1,DHtr​(EXTn,m⁡(dA,dB),SEP⁡(dA,dB))≤12​min⁡{dA2,dB2}−1n​m.D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{EXT}_{n,m}(d_{A},d_{B}),\,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\Bigr)\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\min\{d_{A}^{2},d_{B}^{2}\}-1}{nm}}.(3.36)

## 4tt-site de Finetti upper bounds

We now extend the two-site de Finetti bounds tott-site marginals.

## Theorem 4.1(tt-site de Finetti upper bounds).

For everyd≥2d\geq 2and3≤t≤N3\leq t\leq N,DHtr​(BOSN(t)⁡(d),SEPH(t)⁡(d))\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(t)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d)\Bigr)≤4​t​d−1N,\displaystyle\leq\frac{4t\sqrt{d-1}}{N},(4.1)DHtr​(EXCHN(t)⁡(d),SEPP(t)⁡(d))\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\Bigl(\operatorname{EXCH}_{N}^{(t)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(d)\Bigr)≤4​t​d2−1N.\displaystyle\leq\frac{4t\sqrt{d^{2}-1}}{N}.(4.2)

We first prove the bosonic bound; symmetric purification then gives the
exchangeable bound.
ForM∈Herm⁡(Symt⁡ℋ)M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{Sym}^{t}\mathcal{H}), defineht(M):=max‖u‖=1⟨u⊗t|M|u⊗t⟩.h_{t}(M):=\max_{\left\lVert u\right\rVert=1}\langle u^{\otimes t}\rvert M\lvert u^{\otimes t}\rangle.

This is the maximum expectation ofMMoverSEPH(t)⁡(d)\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d).

Trace-distance duality reduces the problem to boundingTr⁡(M​ρN(t))−ht​(M)\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho_{N}^{(t)})-h_{t}(M). We distinguish one tensor factor from the
othert−1t-1factors and viewρN(t)\rho_{N}^{(t)}as a bipartite state onℋ⊗Symt−1⁡(ℋ).\mathcal{H}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{t-1}(\mathcal{H}).

Grouping theNNsites intonnindividual sites andmmblocks oft−1t-1sites makes this state Bose-symmetric(n,m)(n,m)-extendible. This
two-sided extendibility lets rectangular argmax rounding produce a
product vectorx⊗yx\otimes y, enforcing unentanglement across the1:(t−1)1:(t-1)cut. The challenge is to propagate this
unentanglement to the remaining cuts:yyneed not equalx⊗(t−1)x^{\otimes(t-1)}, sox⊗yx\otimes yneed not be a Hartree vector.

The symmetry test is what propagates this unentanglement. We
incorporate it into the objective before performing argmax rounding.
The bosonic marginal passes the test with certainty, whereas the
rounded product loses value according to its symmetry-test rejection
probability. The same rejection probability controls the cost of
replacingx⊗yx\otimes ybyx⊗tx^{\otimes t}.
Choosing the weight of the symmetry test to balance this cost against
the rectangular argmax rounding error gives the claimed rate. The
rest of the section makes these steps precise.

We begin with the dual formulation of the bosonic bound.

## Lemma 4.2(Exact trace-distance duality).

For everyd≥2d\geq 2,t>2t>2, and everyγ∈D​(Symt⁡ℋ)\gamma\in\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{Sym}^{t}\mathcal{H}),Dtr​(γ,SEPH(t)⁡(d))=supM∈Herm⁡(Symt⁡ℋ)0⪯M⪯I{Tr⁡(M​γ)−ht​(M)}.D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\gamma,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d)\bigr)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\operatorname{Sym}^{t}\mathcal{H})\\
0\preceq M\preceq I\end{subarray}}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\gamma)-h_{t}(M)\right\}.(4.3)

## Proof.

The support function ofSEPH(t)⁡(d)\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(d)isht​(M)h_{t}(M). By duality
(Lemma2.1), this gives
(4.3). Since all states involved
are supported onSymt⁡ℋ\operatorname{Sym}^{t}\mathcal{H}, the witness may be restricted to that
subspace.
∎

## 4.1Two-sided extendibility across the1:(t−1)1:(t-1)cut

Every Hartree vector factors across this cut asu⊗t=u⊗u⊗(t−1).u^{\otimes t}=u\otimes u^{\otimes(t-1)}.

The first step drops the requirementy=x⊗(t−1)y=x^{\otimes(t-1)}and allows
arbitrary productsx⊗yx\otimes y. We choose the1:(t−1)1:(t-1)cut because its
smaller side has dimensiondd, which is the favorable dimension in the
rectangular argmax estimate, and because Bose symmetry supplies exactly
the corresponding two-sided extension.

Fixt>2t>2and setℋA:=ℋ,ℋB:=Symt−1⁡(ℋ).\mathcal{H}_{A}:=\mathcal{H},\qquad\mathcal{H}_{B}:=\operatorname{Sym}^{t-1}(\mathcal{H}).

We use the natural inclusionSymt⁡(ℋ)⊆ℋA⊗ℋB\operatorname{Sym}^{t}(\mathcal{H})\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}obtained by distinguishing the first
tensor factor.

## Fact 4.3(Two-sided extendibility of the marginal).

Letn,m≥1n,m\geq 1satisfyn+(t−1)​m≤Nn+(t-1)m\leq N. For everyρN∈D​(SymN⁡ℋ)\rho_{N}\in\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{Sym}^{N}\mathcal{H}), the marginalρN(t)\rho_{N}^{(t)}, viewed as a
state onℋA⊗ℋB\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}, is Bose-symmetric(n,m)(n,m)-extendible.

## Proof.

Trace outN−n−(t−1)​mN-n-(t-1)msites and rebracket the remaining tensor factors
asnnindividual sites andmmblocks oft−1t-1sites:∙A1​⋯​∙An[∙⋯∙]B1t−1​sites​⋯​[∙⋯∙]Bmt−1​sites.\underset{A_{1}}{\bullet}\ \cdots\ \underset{A_{n}}{\bullet}\qquad\qquad\overset{t-1\text{ sites}}{\underset{B_{1}}{[\,\bullet\ \cdots\ \bullet\,]}}\ \cdots\ \overset{t-1\text{ sites}}{\underset{B_{m}}{[\,\bullet\ \cdots\ \bullet\,]}}.

Letρ~n,m\widetilde{\rho}_{n,m}be the resulting marginal. BecauseρN\rho_{N}is supported onSymN⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{N}(\mathcal{H}), this marginal is supported onSymn+(t−1)​m⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{n+(t-1)m}(\mathcal{H}). It is therefore supported onℋB\mathcal{H}_{B}within eachBjB_{j}, and every permutation of thennAA-sites or of themmBB-blocks acts as the identity on its support. Consequently,ρ~n,m∈D​(Symn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symm⁡(ℋB))\widetilde{\rho}_{n,m}\in\mathrm{D}\bigl(\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{B})\bigr).
Moreover, everyAi​BjA_{i}B_{j}consists ofttdistinct original sites, so(ρ~n,m)Ai​Bj=ρN(t)(\widetilde{\rho}_{n,m})_{A_{i}B_{j}}=\rho_{N}^{(t)}for alli,ji,j.
∎

## 4.2From product vectors to tensor powers

Rectangular argmax rounding produces a product vectorx⊗yx\otimes yacross
the chosen1:(t−1)1:(t-1)cut, but it need not satisfyy=x⊗(t−1)y=x^{\otimes(t-1)}. The symmetry test propagates this unentanglement to
the remaining cuts.

LetΠt\Pi_{t}be the orthogonal projector fromℋA⊗ℋB=ℋ⊗Symt−1⁡(ℋ)\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}=\mathcal{H}\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{t-1}(\mathcal{H})ontoSymt⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{t}(\mathcal{H}). It tests whether
the product vector lies in the symmetric subspace. The following lemma
quantifies the test: a high probability of passing forcesyyto be
close tox⊗(t−1)x^{\otimes(t-1)}. It also bounds the component ofΠt​(x⊗y)\Pi_{t}(x\otimes y)andM​(x⊗y)M(x\otimes y)orthogonal to both rounded factors.

## Lemma 4.4(Approximate symmetry of a product vector).

Letx∈ℋx\in\mathcal{H}andy∈Symt−1⁡(ℋ)y\in\operatorname{Sym}^{t-1}(\mathcal{H})be unit vectors, and define
the symmetry-test rejection probabilityδsym(x,y):=1−⟨x⊗y|Πt|x⊗y⟩.\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}(x,y):=1-\langle x\otimes y\rvert\Pi_{t}\lvert x\otimes y\rangle.

SetP⟂:=Px⟂⊗Py⟂P_{\perp}:=P_{x^{\perp}}\otimes P_{y^{\perp}}.
Then1−|⟨x⊗(t−1),y⟩|2≤t​δsym​(x,y).1-\left\lvert\langle x^{\otimes(t-1)},y\rangle\right\rvert^{2}\leq t\,\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}(x,y).(4.4)

If0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq Iacts onSymt⁡ℋ\operatorname{Sym}^{t}\mathcal{H}and is extended by zero
toℋA⊗ℋB\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}, then⟨x⊗y|M|x⊗y⟩≤ht(M)+t​δsym​(x,y).\langle x\otimes y\rvert M\lvert x\otimes y\rangle\leq h_{t}(M)+\sqrt{t\,\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}(x,y)}.(4.5)

Moreover,‖P⟂​Πt​(x⊗y)‖π\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{\perp}\Pi_{t}(x\otimes y)\right\rVert_{\pi}≤d−1​δsym​(x,y),\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{d-1}\,\sqrt{\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}(x,y)},(4.6)‖P⟂​M​(x⊗y)‖π\displaystyle\left\lVert P_{\perp}M(x\otimes y)\right\rVert_{\pi}≤d−1.\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{d-1}.(4.7)

## Proof.

Becauseyyis symmetric in itst−1t-1factors, averaging all
permutations reduces to averaging over thettpossible locations of
the distinguished first factor:Πt=1t​∑j=1tU1​j,U11=I.\Pi_{t}=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{j=1}^{t}U_{1j},\qquad U_{11}=I.

Letγy\gamma_{y}be the one-site marginal of|y⟩⟨y|\lvert y\rangle\langle y\rvert. Sinceyyis symmetric,⟨x⊗y|U1​j|x⊗y⟩=⟨x|γy|x⟩(2≤j≤t).\langle x\otimes y\rvert U_{1j}\lvert x\otimes y\rangle=\langle x\rvert\gamma_{y}\lvert x\rangle\qquad(2\leq j\leq t).

It follows that⟨x⊗y|Πt|x⊗y⟩=1+(t−1)⟨x|γy|x⟩t.\langle x\otimes y\rvert\Pi_{t}\lvert x\otimes y\rangle=\frac{1+(t-1)\langle x\rvert\gamma_{y}\lvert x\rangle}{t}.(4.8)

PutQx=I−|x⟩⟨x|Q_{x}=I-\lvert x\rangle\langle x\rvert. OnSymt−1⁡(ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{t-1}(\mathcal{H}), the operator𝒩x:=∑j=1t−1(Qx)j\mathcal{N}_{x}:=\sum_{j=1}^{t-1}(Q_{x})_{j}

counts how many of thet−1t-1factors lie inx⟂x^{\perp}. Its kernel isℂ​x⊗(t−1)\mathbb{C}x^{\otimes(t-1)}, and all other eigenvalues are at least one. ThereforeI−|x⊗(t−1)⟩⟨x⊗(t−1)|⪯𝒩x.I-\lvert x^{\otimes(t-1)}\rangle\langle x^{\otimes(t-1)}\rvert\preceq\mathcal{N}_{x}.

Taking the expectation inyyand using (4.8),1−|⟨x⊗(t−1),y⟩|2\displaystyle 1-\left\lvert\langle x^{\otimes(t-1)},y\rangle\right\rvert^{2}≤(t−1)(1−⟨x|γy|x⟩)\displaystyle\leq(t-1)\bigl(1-\langle x\rvert\gamma_{y}\lvert x\rangle\bigr)=t​δsym​(x,y),\displaystyle=t\,\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}(x,y),

which proves (4.4).

The trace distance between the pure states|x⊗y⟩⟨x⊗y|\lvert x\otimes y\rangle\langle x\otimes y\rvertand|x⟩⟨x|⊗t\lvert x\rangle\langle x\rvert^{\otimes t}is1−|⟨x⊗(t−1),y⟩|2≤t​δsym​(x,y).\sqrt{1-\left\lvert\langle x^{\otimes(t-1)},y\rangle\right\rvert^{2}}\leq\sqrt{t\,\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}(x,y)}.

Since0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq Iand⟨x⊗t|M|x⊗t⟩≤ht(M)\langle x^{\otimes t}\rvert M\lvert x^{\otimes t}\rangle\leq h_{t}(M), trace-distance duality
gives (4.5).

For the bounds on the projected components, first note that orthogonality ofΠt\Pi_{t}gives‖(I−Πt)​(x⊗y)‖2=δsym​(x,y).\left\lVert(I-\Pi_{t})(x\otimes y)\right\rVert^{2}=\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}(x,y).

SinceP⟂​(x⊗y)=0P_{\perp}(x\otimes y)=0,P⟂​Πt​(x⊗y)=−P⟂​(I−Πt)​(x⊗y).P_{\perp}\Pi_{t}(x\otimes y)=-P_{\perp}(I-\Pi_{t})(x\otimes y).

Thus its Hilbert-space norm is at mostδsym​(x,y)\sqrt{\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}(x,y)}. Every vector inx⟂⊗y⟂x^{\perp}\otimes y^{\perp}has Schmidt rank at mostd−1d-1, so
(2.18) proves
(4.6). The same rank bound and‖P⟂​M​(x⊗y)‖≤‖M‖∞≤1\left\lVert P_{\perp}M(x\otimes y)\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1

prove (4.7).

A related, quantitatively weaker estimate appears in the
one-versus-many slicing de Finetti theorem of Jeronimo and
Wu[JW24, Theorem 23]. After normalizingΠt​(x⊗y)\Pi_{t}(x\otimes y), that
theorem gives squared overlap1−O​(t3​δsym​(x,y))1-O(t^{3}\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}(x,y))with some tensor powerv⊗tv^{\otimes t}. Estimate (4.4) improves the cubic
loss to a linear one and identifies the direction asxx. This sharper
dependence is essential for the linear dependence onttin
Theorem4.1.

## 4.3Bounding the integrality gap

We now combine rectangular argmax rounding with the symmetry estimate.
ForMθ=(1−θ)​Πt+θ​MM_{\theta}=(1-\theta)\Pi_{t}+\theta M, the grouped bosonic marginal is
feasible for the rectangular lift and retains the score1−θ+θ​Tr⁡(M​ρN(t))1-\theta+\theta\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho_{N}^{(t)}). Rectangular argmax rounding
producesx⊗yx\otimes y, and Lemma4.4controls its
replacement byx⊗tx^{\otimes t}. Optimizing overθ\thetabalances the two
estimates and gives the integrality-gap bound required by
Lemma4.2.

## Proposition 4.5(Rounding across the1:(t−1)1:(t-1)cut).

Letd≥2d\geq 2,3≤t≤N3\leq t\leq N, and letn,m≥2n,m\geq 2satisfyn+(t−1)​m≤Nn+(t-1)m\leq N. For everyρN∈D​(SymN⁡ℋ)\rho_{N}\in\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{Sym}^{N}\mathcal{H})and every0⪯M⪯ISymt⁡ℋ0\preceq M\preceq I_{\operatorname{Sym}^{t}\mathcal{H}},Tr⁡(M​ρN(t))−ht​(M)≤(t+1)​d−1n​m.\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M\rho_{N}^{(t)}\bigr)-h_{t}(M)\leq(\sqrt{t}+1)\sqrt{\frac{d-1}{nm}}.

## Proof.

Letρ~n,m\widetilde{\rho}_{n,m}be the grouped state from
Fact4.3.
For0<θ<10<\theta<1, extendMMby zero outsideSymt⁡ℋ\operatorname{Sym}^{t}\mathcal{H}and consider
the mixed testMθ:=(1−θ)​Πt+θ​Mon​ℋA⊗ℋB.M_{\theta}:=(1-\theta)\Pi_{t}+\theta M\quad\text{on }\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}.

BecauseρN(t)\rho_{N}^{(t)}is supported onSymt⁡ℋ\operatorname{Sym}^{t}\mathcal{H}, the projectorΠt\Pi_{t}acts as the identity on its support. ThereforeTr⁡(Mθ​ρN(t))\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M_{\theta}\rho_{N}^{(t)}\bigr)=(1−θ)​Tr⁡(Πt​ρN(t))+θ​Tr⁡(M​ρN(t))\displaystyle=(1-\theta)\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(\Pi_{t}\rho_{N}^{(t)}\bigr)+\theta\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M\rho_{N}^{(t)}\bigr)=1−θ+θ​Tr⁡(M​ρN(t)).\displaystyle=1-\theta+\theta\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M\rho_{N}^{(t)}\bigr).

By Fact4.3,(ρ~n,m)A1​B1=ρN(t)(\widetilde{\rho}_{n,m})_{A_{1}B_{1}}=\rho_{N}^{(t)}. Hence the
rectangular-lift identity (3.28) gives1−θ+θ​Tr⁡(M​ρN(t))\displaystyle 1-\theta+\theta\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M\rho_{N}^{(t)}\bigr)=Tr⁡(Mθ​ρN(t))=Tr⁡(Mθ[n,m]​ρ~n,m)\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M_{\theta}\rho_{N}^{(t)}\bigr)=\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M_{\theta}^{[n,m]}\widetilde{\rho}_{n,m}\bigr)≤λmax​(Mθ[n,m]).\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M_{\theta}^{[n,m]}\bigr).(4.9)

Rectangular argmax rounding.Proposition3.10, applied toMθM_{\theta},
gives the first-stage product vectorx⊗yx\otimes y. SetP⟂:=Px⟂⊗Py⟂P_{\perp}:=P_{x^{\perp}}\otimes P_{y^{\perp}}. Moving its product-value term
to the right-hand side givesλmax(Mθ[n,m])≤⟨x⊗y|Mθ|x⊗y⟩+1n​m∥P⟂Mθ(x⊗y)∥π.\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M_{\theta}^{[n,m]}\bigr)\leq\langle x\otimes y\rvert M_{\theta}\lvert x\otimes y\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{nm}}\left\lVert P_{\perp}M_{\theta}(x\otimes y)\right\rVert_{\pi}.(4.10)

We use the symmetry test to round this product vector tox⊗tx^{\otimes t}and estimate the two terms on the right using the same rejection
probability. Writeδ=δsym​(x,y)\delta=\delta_{\mathrm{sym}}(x,y)for this probability andβ:=d−1n​m.\beta:=\sqrt{\frac{d-1}{nm}}.

The product value ofMθM_{\theta}is⟨x⊗y|Mθ|x⊗y⟩\displaystyle\langle x\otimes y\rvert M_{\theta}\lvert x\otimes y\rangle=(1−θ)(1−δ)+θ⟨x⊗y|M|x⊗y⟩\displaystyle=(1-\theta)(1-\delta)+\theta\langle x\otimes y\rvert M\lvert x\otimes y\rangle≤(1−θ)​(1−δ)+θ​(ht​(M)+t​δ)\displaystyle\leq(1-\theta)(1-\delta)+\theta\bigl(h_{t}(M)+\sqrt{t\delta}\bigr)=1−θ+θ​ht​(M)−(1−θ)​δ+θ​t​δ,\displaystyle=1-\theta+\theta h_{t}(M)-(1-\theta)\delta+\theta\sqrt{t\delta},(4.11)

where the inequality is (4.5). The triangle
inequality and the two bounds on projected components in

## 5Applications of the optimal de Finetti theorem

The optimal trace-norm theorem has three applications whose resource
bounds are subexponential in the local dimension at fixed accuracy. The
disentangler construction uses the statement that every two-sided
Bose-symmetric extendible state is close to separable. The same
two-sided extension relaxation approximates Best Separable State, and
its error guarantee also gives an algorithm for trace-norm separability
testing.

## 5.1Subexponential disentanglers

Recall that a channelΛ:D​(ℂD)⟶D​(ℂd⊗ℂd)\Lambda:\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{D})\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d})

is an(ε,δ)(\varepsilon,\delta)-disentanglerif[ABD+08]supρ∈D​(ℂD)Dtr​(Λ​(ρ),SEP⁡(d,d))\displaystyle\sup_{\rho\in\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{D})}D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\Lambda(\rho),\operatorname{SEP}(d,d)\bigr)≤ε,\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon,(5.1)supσ∈SEP⁡(d,d)infρ∈D​(ℂD)Dtr​(Λ​(ρ),σ)\displaystyle\sup_{\sigma\in\operatorname{SEP}(d,d)}\inf_{\rho\in\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{D})}D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\Lambda(\rho),\sigma\bigr)≤δ.\displaystyle\leq\delta.(5.2)

The first condition is soundness: every output is close to separable.
The second is coverage: every separable state is close to some output.
A sufficiently small and efficiently implementable disentangler would
simulate two unentangled quantum proofs using one ordinary QMA witness.
The original Watrous disentangler conjecture predictslog⁡D=Ω​(d)\log D=\Omega(d)for fixedε,δ\varepsilon,\deltasatisfyingε+δ<1\varepsilon+\delta<1[ABD+08]. The construction below therefore disproves the original Watrous disentangler
conjecture.

## Theorem 5.1(Subexponential disentanglers).

Letd≥2d\geq 2andn≥1n\geq 1, and setℋA=ℋB=ℂd\mathcal{H}_{A}=\mathcal{H}_{B}=\mathbb{C}^{d}. There is a
channelΛd,n:D​(ℋA⊗n⊗ℋB⊗n)⟶D​(ℋA⊗ℋB)\Lambda_{d,n}:\mathrm{D}\bigl(\mathcal{H}_{A}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}^{\otimes n}\bigr)\longrightarrow\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})

which is an(εd,n,0)(\varepsilon_{d,n},0)-disentangler, whereεd,n:=min⁡{1,d−12​n}.\varepsilon_{d,n}:=\min\left\{1,\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{2n}\right\}.(5.3)

Consequently, for every fixedε∈(0,1)\varepsilon\in(0,1), there is an(ε,0)(\varepsilon,0)-disentangler with input dimensionDDsatisfyinglog⁡D=Oε​(d​log⁡d)\log D=O_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{d}\log d). In particular, one may take(ε,δ)=(1/4,0)(\varepsilon,\delta)=(1/4,0).

## Proof.

LetΠnA\Pi_{n}^{A}andΠnB\Pi_{n}^{B}be the orthogonal projections ontoSymn⁡(ℋA)\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})andSymn⁡(ℋB)\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{B}), respectively, and setPn:=ΠnA⊗ΠnB.P_{n}:=\Pi_{n}^{A}\otimes\Pi_{n}^{B}.

Choose unit vectorsa0∈ℋAa_{0}\in\mathcal{H}_{A}andb0∈ℋBb_{0}\in\mathcal{H}_{B}, and letωn:=|a0⟩⟨a0|⊗n⊗|b0⟩⟨b0|⊗n.\omega_{n}:=\lvert a_{0}\rangle\langle a_{0}\rvert^{\otimes n}\otimes\lvert b_{0}\rangle\langle b_{0}\rvert^{\otimes n}.

The trace-preserving projection ontoSymn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symn⁡(ℋB)\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{B})is the channel𝒫n​(ρ):=Pn​ρ​Pn+Tr⁡((I−Pn)​ρ)​ωn.\mathcal{P}_{n}(\rho):=P_{n}\rho P_{n}+\operatorname{Tr}\bigl((I-P_{n})\rho\bigr)\omega_{n}.(5.4)

Indeed, the first term retains the symmetric component, while the
second replaces the rejected component by the fixed pure product stateωn\omega_{n}in the same subspace. Define the disentangler by retaining
theA1​B1A_{1}B_{1}registers:Λd,n​(ρ):=TrA2:n​B2:n⁡𝒫n​(ρ)=(𝒫n​(ρ))A1​B1.\Lambda_{d,n}(\rho):=\operatorname{Tr}_{A_{2:n}B_{2:n}}\mathcal{P}_{n}(\rho)=\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{n}(\rho)\bigr)_{A_{1}B_{1}}.(5.5)

The state𝒫n​(ρ)\mathcal{P}_{n}(\rho)is supported onSymn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symn⁡(ℋB)\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{B}). Hence every output ofΛd,n\Lambda_{d,n}belongs toBEXTn,n⁡(d,d)\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d,d), and
Theorem3.9givesDtr​(Λd,n​(ρ),SEP⁡(d,d))≤εd,n.D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\Lambda_{d,n}(\rho),\operatorname{SEP}(d,d)\bigr)\leq\varepsilon_{d,n}.(5.6)

Coverage is exact: ifσ=∑jpj|aj⟩⟨aj|⊗|bj⟩⟨bj|\sigma=\sum_{j}p_{j}\lvert a_{j}\rangle\langle a_{j}\rvert\otimes\lvert b_{j}\rangle\langle b_{j}\rvertis separable, thenσ~n:=∑jpj|aj⟩⟨aj|⊗n⊗|bj⟩⟨bj|⊗n\widetilde{\sigma}_{n}:=\sum_{j}p_{j}\lvert a_{j}\rangle\langle a_{j}\rvert^{\otimes n}\otimes\lvert b_{j}\rangle\langle b_{j}\rvert^{\otimes n}

is supported on the range ofPnP_{n}. Therefore𝒫n​(σ~n)=σ~n\mathcal{P}_{n}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{n})=\widetilde{\sigma}_{n}andΛd,n​(σ~n)=σ\Lambda_{d,n}(\widetilde{\sigma}_{n})=\sigma.

For fixedε∈(0,1)\varepsilon\in(0,1), choosen:=⌈d−12​ε⌉.n:=\left\lceil\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{2\varepsilon}\right\rceil.

The input space has dimensionDd,n=d2​nD_{d,n}=d^{2n}, solog⁡Dd,n=2​n​log⁡d=Θε​(d​log⁡d).\log D_{d,n}=2n\log d=\Theta_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{d}\log d).

This proves Theorem5.1. Takingn=⌈2​d−1⌉n=\lceil 2\sqrt{d-1}\rceilgives the stated(1/4,0)(1/4,0)parameters.
∎

## 5.2Best Separable State without perfect completeness

LetℋA≅ℂdA\mathcal{H}_{A}\cong\mathbb{C}^{d_{A}}andℋB≅ℂdB\mathcal{H}_{B}\cong\mathbb{C}^{d_{B}}. Best Separable
State (BSS) asks for the largest expectation of an operator0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq Ion a separable state. WhenMMis the accepting
operator of aQMA​(2)\mathrm{QMA}(2)verifier, this is its largest
acceptance probability over two unentangled proofs. ForM∈Herm⁡(ℋA⊗ℋB)M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}), writehSEP(M):=maxσ∈SEP⁡(dA,dB)Tr(Mσ)=max‖x‖=1‖y‖=1⟨x⊗y|M|x⊗y⟩.h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M):=\max_{\sigma\in\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})}\operatorname{Tr}(M\sigma)=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left\lVert x\right\rVert=1\\
\left\lVert y\right\rVert=1\end{subarray}}\langle x\otimes y\rvert M\lvert x\otimes y\rangle.(5.7)

For an accepting operator0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq Iandn≥1n\geq 1, setM[n,n]\displaystyle M^{[n,n]}:=1n2​∑i=1n∑j=1nMAi​Bj,\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}M_{A_{i}B_{j}},(5.8)hBEXTn,n​(M)\displaystyle h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M):=maxρ∈BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB)⁡Tr⁡(M​ρ)=λmax​(M[n,n]),\displaystyle=\max_{\rho\in\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B})}\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)=\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M^{[n,n]}\bigr),

onSymn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symn⁡(ℋB)\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{B}), with each summand acting as the
identity on all unlisted tensor factors. The last equality follows from
permutation symmetry within each block.

## Theorem 5.2(Subexponential BSS).

Let0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq Iact onℋA⊗ℋB\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}. For everyn≥1n\geq 1,hSEP​(M)≤hBEXTn,n​(M)≤hSEP​(M)+min⁡{dA,dB}−12​n.h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\leq h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M)\leq h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)+\frac{\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}}{2n}.(5.9)

Consequently, for an explicitly given accepting operatorMMonℂd⊗ℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d},hSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)can be approximated to additive errorε∈(0,1]\varepsilon\in(0,1]deterministically. Up to a factor polynomial in
the bit complexity ofMM, the running time isexp⁡(O​(dε​log⁡(2+ε​d))).\exp\left(O\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon}\log(2+\varepsilon\sqrt{d})\right)\right).(5.10)

The promisehSEP​(M)≥ch_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\geq cversushSEP​(M)≤sh_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\leq sis decidable within the same bound withε=Θ​(c−s)\varepsilon=\Theta(c-s).

## Proof.

Writeδn:=min⁡{dA,dB}−12​n.\delta_{n}:=\frac{\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}}{2n}.

The inclusion (2.4) gives the lower bound in
(5.9). For the upper bound, everyρ∈BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB)\rho\in\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B})is withinδn\delta_{n}of a
separable state by Theorem3.9; since0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq I,Tr⁡(M​ρ)≤hSEP​(M)+δn\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)\leq h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)+\delta_{n}.

The two-sided lift acts on a space of dimensionLdA,dB,n=(dA+n−1n)​(dB+n−1n).L_{d_{A},d_{B},n}=\binom{d_{A}+n-1}{n}\binom{d_{B}+n-1}{n}.

Its entries are computable in time polynomial inLdA,dB,nL_{d_{A},d_{B},n}and the
bit complexity ofMM. FordA=dB=dd_{A}=d_{B}=d, taken=⌈d−1/ε⌉n=\lceil\sqrt{d-1}/\varepsilon\rceiland compute its largest eigenvalue
to precisionε/2\varepsilon/2. Thenδn≤ε/2\delta_{n}\leq\varepsilon/2, and the standard
binomial estimate giveslog⁡Ld,d,n=O​(dε​log⁡(2+ε​d)).\log L_{d,d,n}=O\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon}\log(2+\varepsilon\sqrt{d})\right).

This proves (5.10); takingε=Θ​(c−s)\varepsilon=\Theta(c-s)proves the promise statement.
∎

The algorithm of Barak, Kothari, and Steurer for explicitly given BSS
instances assumes perfect completeness, meaninghSEP​(M)=1h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)=1in the
yes case. Theorem5.2removes this assumption while
retaining a running time subexponential in the local
dimension[BKS17, Remark 1.4].

## 5.3Trace-norm separability testing

Given a bipartite stateρ\rho, trace-norm separability testing asks
whetherρ\rhois separable or is at trace distance at leastε\varepsilonfrom every separable state. This is the weak-membership problem for the
separable set in trace distance. The BSS algorithm from the preceding
subsection gives a semidefinite relaxation that approximateshSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)with an additive error independent ofMM. General
reductions between weak optimization and weak membership can lose
polynomial factors in the
dimension[GLS93]. Harrow and Montanaro proved the direction needed
here with only a constant-factor loss in
accuracy[HM13, Proposition 16]. Applying their reduction to our
BSS approximation gives the claimed algorithm. By duality
(Lemma2.1),Dtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB))=max0⪯M⪯I⁡{Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hSEP​(M)}.D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr)=\max_{0\preceq M\preceq I}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\right\}.(5.11)

For every operator0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq I,
Theorem5.2giveshSEP​(M)≤hBEXTn,n​(M)≤hSEP​(M)+δn,δn:=min⁡{dA,dB}−12​n.h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\leq h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M)\leq h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)+\delta_{n},\qquad\delta_{n}:=\frac{\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}}{2n}.(5.12)

ReplacinghSEPh_{\operatorname{SEP}}by this relaxation in
(5.11), defineD^n​(ρ):=max0⪯M⪯I⁡{Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hBEXTn,n​(M)}.\widehat{D}_{n}(\rho):=\max_{0\preceq M\preceq I}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M)\right\}.(5.13)

## Proposition 5.3(From weak optimization to weak membership).

For every stateρ∈D​(ℋA⊗ℋB)\rho\in\mathrm{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})and everyn≥1n\geq 1,Dtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB))−δn≤D^n​(ρ)≤Dtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB)).D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr)-\delta_{n}\leq\widehat{D}_{n}(\rho)\leq D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr).(5.14)

In particular,D^n​(ρ)=0\widehat{D}_{n}(\rho)=0wheneverρ\rhois separable. Moreover, (5.13) is the semidefinite programmaximizeTr⁡(M​ρ)−t\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-t(5.15)subject to0⪯M⪯I,\displaystyle 0\preceq M\preceq I,M[n,n]⪯t​ISymn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symn⁡(ℋB),\displaystyle M^{[n,n]}\preceq tI_{\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{B})},

with variablesM∈Herm⁡(ℋA⊗ℋB)M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})andt∈ℝt\in\mathbb{R}.

## Proof.

For every operator0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq I,
(5.12) givesTr⁡(M​ρ)−hSEP​(M)−δn≤Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hBEXTn,n​(M)≤Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hSEP​(M).\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)-\delta_{n}\leq\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M)\leq\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M).

Maximizing overMMand applying (5.11) proves
(5.14). Ifρ\rhois separable, the
upper bound givesD^n​(ρ)≤0\widehat{D}_{n}(\rho)\leq 0, whileM=0M=0gives the reverse
inequality. HenceD^n​(ρ)=0\widehat{D}_{n}(\rho)=0.

By (5.8),hBEXTn,n​(M)=λmax​(M[n,n])h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M)=\lambda_{\max}(M^{[n,n]}). For any Hermitian
operatorBB, the inequalityB⪯t​IB\preceq tIholds exactly whenλmax​(B)≤t\lambda_{\max}(B)\leq t. Therefore,M[n,n]⪯t​ISymn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symn⁡(ℋB)⟺t≥hBEXTn,n​(M).M^{[n,n]}\preceq tI_{\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{B})}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad t\geq h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M).

For fixedMM, the objectiveTr⁡(M​ρ)−t\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-tdecreases asttincreases,
so its maximum is attained at the smallest feasible value,t=hBEXTn,n​(M)t=h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M). Thus eliminatingttfrom
(5.15) recovers exactly
(5.13). Finally,M↦M[n,n]M\mapsto M^{[n,n]}is linear, so the matrix in the displayed
semidefinite constraint depends linearly on the optimization variables.
∎

## Theorem 5.4(Subexponential trace-norm separability algorithm).

Letρ\rhobe an explicitly given state onℂd⊗ℂd\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d}. For everyε∈(0,1]\varepsilon\in(0,1], the distanceDtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(d,d))D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d,d))can be
approximated to additive errorε\varepsilondeterministically. Up to a
factor polynomial in the bit complexity ofρ\rho, the running time isexp⁡(O​(dε​log⁡(2+ε​d))).\exp\left(O\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon}\log(2+\varepsilon\sqrt{d})\right)\right).(5.16)

In the same running time one can distinguishρ∈SEP⁡(d,d)fromDtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(d,d))≥ε.\rho\in\operatorname{SEP}(d,d)\qquad\text{from}\qquad D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d,d))\geq\varepsilon.(5.17)

## Proof.

Choosen:=⌈d−1ε⌉.n:=\left\lceil\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{\varepsilon}\right\rceil.

Thenδn≤ε/2\delta_{n}\leq\varepsilon/2, and
Proposition5.3givesDtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(d,d))−δn≤D^n​(ρ)≤Dtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(d,d)).D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d,d))-\delta_{n}\leq\widehat{D}_{n}(\rho)\leq D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d,d)).(5.18)

Solve (5.15) to additive precisionε/2\varepsilon/2, and denote the resulting value byD~n​(ρ)\widetilde{D}_{n}(\rho). By (5.18),|D~n​(ρ)−Dtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(d,d))|≤ε2+δn≤ε.\left|\widetilde{D}_{n}(\rho)-D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d,d))\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\delta_{n}\leq\varepsilon.

ThusD~n​(ρ)\widetilde{D}_{n}(\rho)is the required approximation.

The semidefinite constraintM[n,n]⪯t​ISymn⁡(ℂd)⊗Symn⁡(ℂd)M^{[n,n]}\preceq tI_{\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathbb{C}^{d})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathbb{C}^{d})}has matrix dimensionLd,d,n=(d+n−1n)2.L_{d,d,n}=\binom{d+n-1}{n}^{2}.

The remaining constraints0⪯M⪯I0\preceq M\preceq Ihave dimensiond2d^{2}.
The construction and dimension estimate in the proof of
Theorem5.2, together with standard
semidefinite optimization, therefore give
(5.16).

For the promise problem, run the additive approximation just proved with
accuracyε/3\varepsilon/3. Its output is at mostε/3\varepsilon/3whenρ\rhois
separable and at least2​ε/32\varepsilon/3whenDtr​(ρ,SEP⁡(d,d))≥εD_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d,d))\geq\varepsilon. Comparing the output withε/2\varepsilon/2distinguishes the two cases. Replacingε\varepsilonbyε/3\varepsilon/3changes the running time only by constant factors in the exponent.

## 6A dimension-free bosonic Hilbert–Schmidt de Finetti theorem

Brandão and Christandl identified local-dimension dependence as a
crucial bottleneck for quantum de Finetti theorems and showed that the
linear barrier can be broken under one-way LOCC measurements, obtaining
only logarithmic dependence on the local dimension[BC12]. In
Hilbert–Schmidt distance, the theorem below removes the local-dimension
dependence entirely for bosonic approximation.

## Theorem 6.1(Dimension-free bosonic Hilbert–Schmidt de Finetti upper bound).

For everyN,d≥2N,d\geq 2,DHHS​(BOSN(2)⁡(d),SEPH(2)⁡(d))≤min⁡{d−1N−1,8N−1}.D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(2)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)\Bigr)\leq\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{N-1},\frac{8}{\sqrt{N-1}}\right\}.(6.1)

For each fixeddd, the first term in (6.1) retains the
faster rateO​(d/N)O(\sqrt{d}/N).

## 6.1Spectral truncation and the dimension-free Hilbert–Schmidt bound

The dimension-dependent bound in (6.1) requires no new
argument. Theorem3.1gives a stateτ∈SEPH(2)⁡(d)\tau\in\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)such that‖ρN(2)−τ‖2≤‖ρN(2)−τ‖1≤d−1N−1.\left\lVert\rho_{N}^{(2)}-\tau\right\rVert_{2}\leq\left\lVert\rho_{N}^{(2)}-\tau\right\rVert_{1}\leq\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{N-1}.

It remains to prove the dimension-free bound.

Setup.By convexity ofDHS​(⋅,SEPH(2)⁡(d))D_{\mathrm{HS}}(\,\cdot\,,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)), it is enough to consider
a pure bosonic stateρN=|ψ⟩⟨ψ|\rho_{N}=\lvert\psi\rangle\langle\psi\rvertwithψ∈SymN⁡ℋ\psi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{N}\mathcal{H}a unit vector. Setρ1:=ρN(1),ρ2:=ρN(2).\rho_{1}:=\rho_{N}^{(1)},\qquad\rho_{2}:=\rho_{N}^{(2)}.

Split the spectrum ofρ1\rho_{1}at the scale1/N1/N:P:=𝟏(1/N,∞)​(ρ1),Q:=I−P,r:=rank⁡P.P:=\mathbf{1}_{(1/N,\infty)}(\rho_{1}),\qquad Q:=I-P,\qquad r:=\operatorname{rank}P.(6.2)

We callP​ℋP\mathcal{H}the spectral head andQ​ℋQ\mathcal{H}the spectral tail. The
cutoff has two immediate consequences: sinceTr⁡ρ1=1\operatorname{Tr}\rho_{1}=1, the head
has dimensionr<Nr<N, and the tail is spectrally flat,Q​ρ1​Q⪯1N​Q.Q\rho_{1}Q\preceq\frac{1}{N}Q.(6.3)

By Fact2.4, the induced two-site
decomposition isSym2⁡ℋ=Sym2⁡(P​ℋ)⊕(P​ℋ∨Q​ℋ)⊕Sym2⁡(Q​ℋ).\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathcal{H}=\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(P\mathcal{H})\oplus(P\mathcal{H}\vee Q\mathcal{H})\oplus\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H}).(6.4)

LetΠP​P\Pi_{PP},ΠP​Q\Pi_{PQ}, andΠQ​Q\Pi_{QQ}be the projections onto the
three summands. Group the two summands containing at least one tail
direction by settingΠtail:=ΠP​Q+ΠQ​Q=I−ΠP​P.\Pi_{\mathrm{tail}}:=\Pi_{PQ}+\Pi_{QQ}=I-\Pi_{PP}.

The corresponding diagonal blocks ofρ2\rho_{2}areρ2head:=ΠP​P​ρ2​ΠP​P,ρ2tail:=Πtail​ρ2​Πtail.\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}:=\Pi_{PP}\rho_{2}\Pi_{PP},\qquad\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{tail}}:=\Pi_{\mathrm{tail}}\rho_{2}\Pi_{\mathrm{tail}}.(6.5)

LetXXdenote the off-diagonal head–tail block,X:=ΠP​P​ρ2​Πtail.X:=\Pi_{PP}\rho_{2}\Pi_{\mathrm{tail}}.

Relative torange⁡ΠP​P⊕range⁡Πtail\operatorname{range}\Pi_{PP}\oplus\operatorname{range}\Pi_{\mathrm{tail}},
the state has the positive block formρ2=(ρ2headXX†ρ2tail)⪰0.\rho_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}&X\\
X^{\dagger}&\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{tail}}\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0.(6.6)

SinceTr⁡ρ2=1\operatorname{Tr}\rho_{2}=1,
Fact2.3gives‖ρ2−ρ2head‖22≤2​‖ρ2tail‖∞.\left\lVert\rho_{2}-\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 2\left\lVert\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{tail}}\right\rVert_{\infty}.

Thus the tail need not have small trace, and the off-diagonal blockXXneed not be estimated separately: an operator-norm bound on the diagonal
tail block controls the entire discarded part. The rank boundr<Nr<Nmakes the head accessible to the optimal trace-norm de Finetti theorem, while
(6.3) will forceρ2tail\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{tail}}to be small in operator norm.

Proof architecture.The proof consists of two substantive estimates followed by a
trace-restoration step. First, we construct a Hartree mixtureτhead\tau_{\mathrm{head}}that approximatesρ2head\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}and has the same trace. Second, we transfer
the one-site flatness (6.3) to the
two-site estimate‖ρ2tail‖∞=O​(N−1)\left\lVert\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{tail}}\right\rVert_{\infty}=O(N^{-1}).
Fact2.3then removes the tail at
Hilbert–Schmidt costO​(N−1/2)O(N^{-1/2}). The missing trace is restored by a
Hartree mixtureτres\tau_{\mathrm{res}}supported onSym2⁡(Q​ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H}). Setτ:=τhead+τres.\tau:=\tau_{\mathrm{head}}+\tau_{\mathrm{res}}.(6.7)

Their traces will add to one, soτ∈SEPH(2)⁡(d)\tau\in\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d). The proof
is organized around the following error decomposition:‖ρ2−τ‖2\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{2}-\tau\right\rVert_{2}=‖(ρ2head−τhead)+(ρ2−ρ2head)−τres‖2\displaystyle=\left\lVert(\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}-\tau_{\mathrm{head}})+(\rho_{2}-\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}})-\tau_{\mathrm{res}}\right\rVert_{2}≤‖ρ2head−τhead‖2+‖ρ2−ρ2head‖2+‖τres‖2\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}-\tau_{\mathrm{head}}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\rho_{2}-\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}\right\rVert_{2}+\left\lVert\tau_{\mathrm{res}}\right\rVert_{2}≤1N−1+24N−1+2​2N<8N−1.\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-1}}+\sqrt{\frac{24}{N-1}}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{N}<\frac{8}{\sqrt{N-1}}.(6.8)

The three terms in the triangle inequality are bounded, in order, by
Lemmas6.2,6.4, and6.5.

## 6.1.1Hartree approximation of the spectral head

We begin with the low-dimensional head, where the optimal trace-norm
de Finetti theorem enters.

## Lemma 6.2(Rounding the spectral head).

There is a Hartree mixtureτhead\tau_{\mathrm{head}}, supported onSym2⁡(P​ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(P\mathcal{H}), such thatTr⁡τhead=Tr⁡ρ2head,‖ρ2head−τhead‖2≤1N−1.\operatorname{Tr}\tau_{\mathrm{head}}=\operatorname{Tr}\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}},\qquad\left\lVert\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}-\tau_{\mathrm{head}}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-1}}.

## Proof.

Fact2.4, applied toℋ=P​ℋ⊕Q​ℋ\mathcal{H}=P\mathcal{H}\oplus Q\mathcal{H}, givesSymN⁡ℋ≅⨁k=0NSymk⁡(P​ℋ)⊗SymN−k⁡(Q​ℋ).\operatorname{Sym}^{N}\mathcal{H}\cong\bigoplus_{k=0}^{N}\operatorname{Sym}^{k}(P\mathcal{H})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{N-k}(Q\mathcal{H}).

LetΠk\Pi_{k}be the projection onto thekkth summand and setpk:=Tr⁡(Πk​ρN).p_{k}:=\operatorname{Tr}(\Pi_{k}\rho_{N}).

TheP​PPP-compression is block diagonal with respect to this
decomposition and vanishes on the summands withk<2k<2. Fork≥2k\geq 2,
after projecting two sites intoP​ℋP\mathcal{H}, the residual component lies inSymk−2⁡(P​ℋ)⊗SymN−k⁡(Q​ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{k-2}(P\mathcal{H})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{N-k}(Q\mathcal{H}). These residual spaces are
orthogonal for distinct values ofkk, so only the diagonal blocksΠk​ρN​Πk\Pi_{k}\rho_{N}\Pi_{k}contribute toρ2head\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}.

Forpk>0p_{k}>0, defineσk:=Πk​ρN​Πkpk∈D​(range⁡Πk),ρP,k:=TrSymN−k⁡(Q​ℋ)⁡(σk)∈D​(Symk⁡(P​ℋ)).\sigma_{k}:=\frac{\Pi_{k}\rho_{N}\Pi_{k}}{p_{k}}\in\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{range}\Pi_{k}),\qquad\rho_{P,k}:=\operatorname{Tr}_{\operatorname{Sym}^{N-k}(Q\mathcal{H})}\left(\sigma_{k}\right)\in\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{Sym}^{k}(P\mathcal{H})).

WriteρP,k(2)\rho_{P,k}^{(2)}for its two-site marginal.
Within thekkth slice, exactlykkof theNNsites lie inP​ℋP\mathcal{H}.
Thus the probability that two sites sampled without replacement both
lie inP​ℋP\mathcal{H}is(k2)(N2)=k​(k−1)N​(N−1).\frac{\binom{k}{2}}{\binom{N}{2}}=\frac{k(k-1)}{N(N-1)}.

HerePiP_{i}denotesPPacting on theiith site. Equivalently,
permutation symmetry and the identity∑i≠jPi​Pj=k​(k−1)​I\sum_{i\neq j}P_{i}P_{j}=k(k-1)Ionrange⁡Πk\operatorname{range}\Pi_{k}giveTr⁡(P1​P2​σk)=1N​(N−1)​Tr⁡((∑i≠jPi​Pj)​σk)=k​(k−1)N​(N−1).\operatorname{Tr}(P_{1}P_{2}\sigma_{k})=\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\operatorname{Tr}\left(\left(\sum_{i\neq j}P_{i}P_{j}\right)\sigma_{k}\right)=\frac{k(k-1)}{N(N-1)}.

Under the canonical identification of thekkth slice, its normalizedP​PPP-block isρP,k(2)\rho_{P,k}^{(2)}. ThereforeΠP​P​Tr3,…,N⁡(σk)​ΠP​P=k​(k−1)N​(N−1)​ρP,k(2).\Pi_{PP}\operatorname{Tr}_{3,\ldots,N}(\sigma_{k})\Pi_{PP}=\frac{k(k-1)}{N(N-1)}\rho_{P,k}^{(2)}.

UsingΠk​ρN​Πk=pk​σk\Pi_{k}\rho_{N}\Pi_{k}=p_{k}\sigma_{k}and summing the diagonal slice
contributions givesρ2head=∑2≤k≤Npk>0pk​k​(k−1)N​(N−1)​ρP,k(2).\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq k\leq N\\
p_{k}>0\end{subarray}}p_{k}\frac{k(k-1)}{N(N-1)}\rho_{P,k}^{(2)}.(6.9)

Ifr≤1r\leq 1, every term in (6.9) is Hartree, so
we may takeτhead:=ρ2head\tau_{\mathrm{head}}:=\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}. Assume
now thatr≥2r\geq 2. Applying Theorem3.1toρP,k\rho_{P,k}gives a Hartree mixtureτk\tau_{k}supported onSym2⁡(P​ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(P\mathcal{H})and satisfying‖ρP,k(2)−τk‖1≤r−1k−1.\left\lVert\rho_{P,k}^{(2)}-\tau_{k}\right\rVert_{1}\leq\frac{\sqrt{r-1}}{k-1}.(6.10)

Define the Hartree mixtureτhead:=∑2≤k≤Npk>0pk​k​(k−1)N​(N−1)τk.\tau_{\mathrm{head}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq k\leq N\\
p_{k}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{p_{k}k(k-1)}{N(N-1)}\tau_{k}.(6.11)

This operator has the same trace asρ2head\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}. The key

p_{k}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{p_{k}k(k-1)}{N(N-1)}\left\lVert\rho_{P,k}^{(2)}-\tau_{k}\right\rVert_{1}≤r−1N​(N−1)​∑k=2Npk​k\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{r-1}}{N(N-1)}\sum_{k=2}^{N}p_{k}k≤r−1N−1≤1N−1.\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{r-1}}{N-1}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-1}}.(6.12)

Forr≤1r\leq 1, the same estimate holds with zero left-hand side.
