## 6.1.2Operator-norm control of the spectral tail

The mixed and tail–tail diagonal blocks are bounded directly, after
which positive block estimates assemble them into an operator-norm
bound onρ2tail\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{tail}}.

## Lemma 6.3(Mixed and tail–tail block estimates).

With the notation above,‖ΠP​Q​ρ2​ΠP​Q‖∞\displaystyle\left\lVert\Pi_{PQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{PQ}\right\rVert_{\infty}≤4N,\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{N},(6.13)‖ΠQ​Q​ρ2​ΠQ​Q‖∞\displaystyle\left\lVert\Pi_{QQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{QQ}\right\rVert_{\infty}≤8N−1.\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{N-1}.(6.14)

## Proof.

Recall thatρN=|ψ⟩⟨ψ|\rho_{N}=\lvert\psi\rangle\langle\psi\rvert, whereψ∈SymN⁡ℋ\psi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{N}\mathcal{H}is a unit vector.

The mixed block.Letϕ∈P​ℋ∨Q​ℋ\phi\in P\mathcal{H}\vee Q\mathcal{H}be a unit vector. Its Schmidt decomposition isϕ=∑i=1rϕμi​pi∨qi,μi>0,∑iμi=1,\phi=\sum_{i=1}^{r_{\phi}}\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,p_{i}\vee q_{i},\qquad\mu_{i}>0,\qquad\sum_{i}\mu_{i}=1,(6.15)

where(pi)(p_{i})and(qi)(q_{i})are orthonormal families inP​ℋP\mathcal{H}andQ​ℋQ\mathcal{H},
respectively, andrϕ≤r<Nr_{\phi}\leq r<N. Sinceψ\psiis symmetric,Cpi∨qi(ψ)=12((⟨pi|⊗⟨qi|⊗I)+(⟨qi|⊗⟨pi|⊗I))ψ=2Cpi(Cqi(ψ)).C_{p_{i}\vee q_{i}}(\psi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bigl((\langle p_{i}\rvert\otimes\langle q_{i}\rvert\otimes I)+(\langle q_{i}\rvert\otimes\langle p_{i}\rvert\otimes I)\bigr)\psi=\sqrt{2}\,C_{p_{i}}\bigl(C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\bigr).

Although the Schmidt directionsqiq_{i}need not be eigenvectors ofρ1\rho_{1}, each is a unit vector inQ​ℋQ\mathcal{H}, so
(6.3) gives∥Cqi(ψ)∥2=⟨qi|ρ1|qi⟩=⟨qi|Qρ1Q|qi⟩≤1N.\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}=\langle q_{i}\rvert\rho_{1}\lvert q_{i}\rangle=\langle q_{i}\rvert Q\rho_{1}Q\lvert q_{i}\rangle\leq\frac{1}{N}.

Since the Schmidt weights sum to one, it follows that∑iμi​‖Cqi​(ψ)‖2≤1N​∑iμi=1N.\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i}\mu_{i}=\frac{1}{N}.(6.16)

For every unit vectorΞ∈SymN−2⁡ℋ\Xi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{N-2}\mathcal{H}, use the adjoint relation⟨Ξ,Cpi​(Cqi​(ψ))⟩=⟨Cpi∗​(Ξ),Cqi​(ψ)⟩.\langle\Xi,C_{p_{i}}(C_{q_{i}}(\psi))\rangle=\langle C_{p_{i}}^{*}(\Xi),C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\rangle.

Together with Cauchy–Schwarz,
Fact2.6, specifically
(2.26), withk=N−1k=N-1andℓ=rϕ\ell=r_{\phi}, and
(6.16), this gives|⟨Ξ,Cϕ​(ψ)⟩|\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle\Xi,C_{\phi}(\psi)\rangle\right\rvert=|∑iμi​⟨Ξ,Cpi∨qi​(ψ)⟩|\displaystyle=\left\lvert\sum_{i}\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\langle\Xi,C_{p_{i}\vee q_{i}}(\psi)\rangle\right\rvert=2​|∑iμi​⟨Ξ,Cpi​(Cqi​(ψ))⟩|\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\left\lvert\sum_{i}\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\langle\Xi,C_{p_{i}}\bigl(C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\bigr)\rangle\right\rvert=2​|∑iμi​⟨Cpi∗​(Ξ),Cqi​(ψ)⟩|\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\left\lvert\sum_{i}\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\langle C_{p_{i}}^{*}(\Xi),C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\rangle\right\rvert≤2​(∑i‖Cpi∗​(Ξ)‖2)1/2​(∑iμi​‖Cqi​(ψ)‖2)1/2\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\left(\sum_{i}\left\lVert C_{p_{i}}^{*}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i}\mu_{i}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}\right)^{1/2}≤2​rϕ+N−2N−1​1N\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\frac{r_{\phi}+N-2}{N-1}}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}≤2N.\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{N}}.

The last inequality usesrϕ<Nr_{\phi}<N.
Taking the supremum over unit vectorsΞ∈SymN−2⁡ℋ\Xi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{N-2}\mathcal{H}therefore gives‖Cϕ​(ψ)‖≤2N.\left\lVert C_{\phi}(\psi)\right\rVert\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{N}}.

Sinceϕ∈P​ℋ∨Q​ℋ\phi\in P\mathcal{H}\vee Q\mathcal{H}, we haveΠP​Q​ϕ=ϕ\Pi_{PQ}\phi=\phi.
Fact2.5, specifically the
partial-inner-product identity (2.23), now gives⟨ϕ|ΠP​Qρ2ΠP​Q|ϕ⟩=⟨ϕ|ρ2|ϕ⟩=∥Cϕ(ψ)∥2≤4N.\langle\phi\rvert\Pi_{PQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{PQ}\lvert\phi\rangle=\langle\phi\rvert\rho_{2}\lvert\phi\rangle=\left\lVert C_{\phi}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{4}{N}.

The compressionΠP​Q​ρ2​ΠP​Q\Pi_{PQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{PQ}is positive semidefinite, so
its operator norm is the supremum of the left-hand side over unit
vectorsϕ∈P​ℋ∨Q​ℋ\phi\in P\mathcal{H}\vee Q\mathcal{H}. This proves
(6.13).

The tail–tail block.Letϕ∈Sym2⁡(Q​ℋ)\phi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H})be a unit vector. A Takagi decomposition givesϕ=∑i=1mμi​qi⊗2,μ1≥μ2≥⋯≥0,∑iμi=1,\phi=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,q_{i}^{\otimes 2},\qquad\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0,\qquad\sum_{i}\mu_{i}=1,(6.17)

for an orthonormal family(qi)⊆Q​ℋ(q_{i})\subseteq Q\mathcal{H}. Splitϕ=ϕ≤N+ϕ>N,ϕ≤N:=∑i≤Nμi​qi⊗2,ϕ>N:=∑i>Nμi​qi⊗2,\phi=\phi_{\leq N}+\phi_{>N},\qquad\phi_{\leq N}:=\sum_{i\leq N}\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,q_{i}^{\otimes 2},\qquad\phi_{>N}:=\sum_{i>N}\sqrt{\mu_{i}}\,q_{i}^{\otimes 2},

with empty terms omitted. The leadingNNTakagi terms are controlled
by applying Cauchy–Schwarz with the weightsμi\mu_{i}multiplying‖Cqi​(ψ)‖2\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}, exactly as in the mixed block. The same
pointwise tail estimate gives∑i≤Nμi​‖Cqi​(ψ)‖2≤1N​∑i≤Nμi≤1N.\sum_{i\leq N}\mu_{i}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\leq N}\mu_{i}\leq\frac{1}{N}.

Therefore, for every unitΞ∈SymN−2⁡ℋ\Xi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{N-2}\mathcal{H},|⟨Ξ,Cϕ≤N​(ψ)⟩|\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle\Xi,C_{\phi_{\leq N}}(\psi)\rangle\right\rvert≤(∑i≤N‖Cqi∗​(Ξ)‖2)1/2​(∑i≤Nμi​‖Cqi​(ψ)‖2)1/2\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{i\leq N}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}^{*}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i\leq N}\mu_{i}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}\right)^{1/2}≤2N.\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{N}}.

Hence‖Cϕ≤N​(ψ)‖2≤2N.\left\lVert C_{\phi_{\leq N}}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{2}{N}.(6.18)

For the remaining Takagi terms, monotonicity of(μi)(\mu_{i})and∑iμi=1\sum_{i}\mu_{i}=1implyμN+1≤1/(N+1)\mu_{N+1}\leq 1/(N+1). There may now be arbitrarily many terms, so we
instead attach the weightsμi\mu_{i}to‖Cqi∗​(Ξ)‖2\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}^{*}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}and leave‖Cqi​(ψ)‖2\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}unweighted. Thus|⟨Ξ,Cϕ>N​(ψ)⟩|\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle\Xi,C_{\phi_{>N}}(\psi)\rangle\right\rvert≤(∑i>Nμi​‖Cqi∗​(Ξ)‖2)1/2​(∑i>N‖Cqi​(ψ)‖2)1/2.\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{i>N}\mu_{i}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}^{*}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i>N}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

To estimate the first factor, apply
Fact2.6, specifically
(2.24), withk=N−1k=N-1:∑i>Nμi​‖Cqi∗​(Ξ)‖2\displaystyle\sum_{i>N}\mu_{i}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}^{*}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}=1N−1​(∑i>Nμi+(N−2)​∑i>Nμi​‖Cqi​(Ξ)‖2)\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N-1}\left(\sum_{i>N}\mu_{i}+(N-2)\sum_{i>N}\mu_{i}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}\right)≤1N−1​(1+N−2N+1)<2N−1.\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N-1}\left(1+\frac{N-2}{N+1}\right)<\frac{2}{N-1}.

Here∑i>Nμi≤1\sum_{i>N}\mu_{i}\leq 1, while
(2.25) andμi≤μN+1≤1/(N+1)\mu_{i}\leq\mu_{N+1}\leq 1/(N+1)fori>Ni>Ngive∑i>Nμi​‖Cqi​(Ξ)‖2≤μN+1​∑i>N‖Cqi​(Ξ)‖2≤1N+1.\sum_{i>N}\mu_{i}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}\leq\mu_{N+1}\sum_{i>N}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\Xi)\right\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{1}{N+1}.

The same Bessel bound, applied toψ\psi, gives∑i>N‖Cqi​(ψ)‖2≤1.\sum_{i>N}\left\lVert C_{q_{i}}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}\leq 1.

Therefore‖Cϕ>N​(ψ)‖2≤2N−1.\left\lVert C_{\phi_{>N}}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{2}{N-1}.(6.19)

By linearity,Cϕ=Cϕ≤N+Cϕ>NC_{\phi}=C_{\phi_{\leq N}}+C_{\phi_{>N}}. Hence the triangle
inequality, together with (6.18) and
(6.19), gives‖Cϕ​(ψ)‖≤‖Cϕ≤N​(ψ)‖+‖Cϕ>N​(ψ)‖≤2N+2N−1.\left\lVert C_{\phi}(\psi)\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert C_{\phi_{\leq N}}(\psi)\right\rVert+\left\lVert C_{\phi_{>N}}(\psi)\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{N}}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{N-1}}.

Sinceϕ∈Sym2⁡(Q​ℋ)\phi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H}), we haveΠQ​Q​ϕ=ϕ\Pi_{QQ}\phi=\phi.
Fact2.5, specifically
(2.23), therefore implies⟨ϕ|ΠQ​Qρ2ΠQ​Q|ϕ⟩=⟨ϕ|ρ2|ϕ⟩=∥Cϕ(ψ)∥2≤(2N+2N−1)2≤8N−1.\langle\phi\rvert\Pi_{QQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{QQ}\lvert\phi\rangle=\langle\phi\rvert\rho_{2}\lvert\phi\rangle=\left\lVert C_{\phi}(\psi)\right\rVert^{2}\leq\left(\sqrt{\frac{2}{N}}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{N-1}}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{8}{N-1}.

Finally,ΠQ​Q​ρ2​ΠQ​Q\Pi_{QQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{QQ}is positive semidefinite, so its
operator norm is the supremum of the left-hand side over unit vectorsϕ∈Sym2⁡(Q​ℋ)\phi\in\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H}). This proves (6.14).
∎

## Lemma 6.4(Removing the spectral tail).

The head compression in (6.5) satisfies‖ρ2−ρ2head‖2≤24N−1.\left\lVert\rho_{2}-\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{24}{N-1}}.

## Proof.

The range ofΠtail\Pi_{\mathrm{tail}}is the direct sum of the mixed and
tail–tail spaces in (6.4). Relative to
this decomposition,ρ2tail=(ΠP​Q​ρ2​ΠP​QΠP​Q​ρ2​ΠQ​QΠQ​Q​ρ2​ΠP​QΠQ​Q​ρ2​ΠQ​Q)⪰0.\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{tail}}=\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{PQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{PQ}&\Pi_{PQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{QQ}\\
\Pi_{QQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{PQ}&\Pi_{QQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{QQ}\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0.

Fact2.2and
Lemma6.3therefore give‖ρ2tail‖∞\displaystyle\left\lVert\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{tail}}\right\rVert_{\infty}≤‖ΠP​Q​ρ2​ΠP​Q‖∞+‖ΠQ​Q​ρ2​ΠQ​Q‖∞\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Pi_{PQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{PQ}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert\Pi_{QQ}\rho_{2}\Pi_{QQ}\right\rVert_{\infty}≤4N+8N−1≤12N−1.\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{N}+\frac{8}{N-1}\leq\frac{12}{N-1}.(6.20)

Apply the trace-one case of
Fact2.3to the block decomposition
(6.6). This controls the diagonal tail and
the off-diagonal head–tail blocks simultaneously:‖ρ2−ρ2head‖22≤2​‖ρ2tail‖∞≤24N−1.\left\lVert\rho_{2}-\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq 2\left\lVert\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{tail}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{24}{N-1}.(6.21)

Thus positivity converts anO​(N−1)O(N^{-1})operator-norm estimate on the
discarded block into anO​(N−1/2)O(N^{-1/2})Hilbert–Schmidt estimate for
the entire discarded part, including the off-diagonal blocks.
∎

## 6.1.3Completion of the dimension-free bound

At this point both substantive estimates are complete: the head has a
Hartree approximation of the correct trace, and the entire discarded
part is small in Hilbert–Schmidt norm. It remains only to restore the
missing trace. Spreading this mass uniformly over the symmetric tail
costs onlyO​(N−1)O(N^{-1}).

## Lemma 6.5(Restoring the missing trace).

There is a Hartree mixtureτres\tau_{\mathrm{res}}, supported onSym2⁡(Q​ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H}), such thatTr⁡τres=1−Tr⁡ρ2head,‖τres‖2≤2​2N.\operatorname{Tr}\tau_{\mathrm{res}}=1-\operatorname{Tr}\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}},\qquad\left\lVert\tau_{\mathrm{res}}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{N}.

## Proof.

Setδ:=1−Tr⁡ρ2head,s:=dim(Q​ℋ).\delta:=1-\operatorname{Tr}\rho_{2}^{\mathrm{head}},\qquad s:=\dim(Q\mathcal{H}).

SinceΠtail⪯Q⊗I+I⊗Q\Pi_{\mathrm{tail}}\preceq Q\otimes I+I\otimes QonSym2⁡ℋ\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\mathcal{H}, the spectral-tail bound
(6.3) givesδ=Tr⁡(Πtail​ρ2)≤Tr⁡((Q⊗I+I⊗Q)​ρ2)=2​Tr⁡(Q​ρ1)≤2​sN.\delta=\operatorname{Tr}(\Pi_{\mathrm{tail}}\rho_{2})\leq\operatorname{Tr}\bigl((Q\otimes I+I\otimes Q)\rho_{2}\bigr)=2\operatorname{Tr}(Q\rho_{1})\leq\frac{2s}{N}.

Ifs=0s=0, thenδ=0\delta=0, and we takeτres=0\tau_{\mathrm{res}}=0.
Suppose thats≥1s\geq 1, and letμQ\mu_{Q}denote Haar probability measure
on the unit sphere ofQ​ℋQ\mathcal{H}. Defineτres:=δΠQ​QdimSym2⁡(Q​ℋ)=δ∫|u⟩⟨u|⊗2dμQ(u).\tau_{\mathrm{res}}:=\delta\frac{\Pi_{QQ}}{\dim\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H})}=\delta\int\lvert u\rangle\langle u\rvert^{\otimes 2}\,d\mu_{Q}(u).

Thusτres\tau_{\mathrm{res}}is a Hartree mixture supported onSym2⁡(Q​ℋ)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H}), and its trace isδ\delta. SincedimSym2⁡(Q​ℋ)=s​(s+1)/2\dim\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(Q\mathcal{H})=s(s+1)/2,‖τres‖2\displaystyle\left\lVert\tau_{\mathrm{res}}\right\rVert_{2}=δ​2s​(s+1)≤2​2N​ss+1≤2​2N.∎\displaystyle=\delta\sqrt{\frac{2}{s(s+1)}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{N}\sqrt{\frac{s}{s+1}}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{N}.\qed

## Completion of the proof of the dimension-free bound.

Letτhead\tau_{\mathrm{head}}andτres\tau_{\mathrm{res}}be supplied by
Lemmas6.2and6.5, and letτ\taube their sum as in
(6.7).
The two summands are Hartree mixtures whose traces add
to one, soτ∈SEPH(2)⁡(d)\tau\in\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d). The three estimates used in
(6.8) are precisely the conclusions of
Lemmas6.2,6.4, and6.5. Hence
(6.8) gives‖ρ2−τ‖2<8/N−1\left\lVert\rho_{2}-\tau\right\rVert_{2}<8/\sqrt{N-1}.
This is the dimension-free term in (6.1).
∎

## 6.2Two-sided Bose-symmetric extendible states

Since Theorem6.1is dimension free, one might first try
a tensor-product construction: pairAi​BiA_{i}B_{i}intonnbosonic sites
with local spaceℋA⊗ℋB\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}and apply the theorem. The desired
cross marginal is then obtained from the resulting two-site marginal
by tracing outB1​A2B_{1}A_{2}, and the image of each Hartree state under this
partial trace is separable. However, partial trace has
Hilbert–Schmidt2→22\to 2normdA​dB\sqrt{d_{A}d_{B}}, which destroys dimension
independence.

To avoid this loss, we instead use a direct-sum construction: the two
types of registers are placed in orthogonally labeled summands of a
single bosonic system, so that the desired state is a compression of a
two-site marginal rather than a partial trace. The
direct-sum construction is inspired by the symmetrization idea of Liu
and Wu[LW26, Sec. 4.2.1].

This label-bundling
construction is a convenient technical workaround for the applications in the next section. A more systematic
approach would repeat the spectral-truncation argument of the preceding
subsection directly for two-sided extensions, using
Theorem3.9on the low-dimensional
spectral head; we leave such a direct proof for future work.

LetℋA=ℂdA\mathcal{H}_{A}=\mathbb{C}^{d_{A}}andℋB=ℂdB\mathcal{H}_{B}=\mathbb{C}^{d_{B}}, and setℋ⊕=(|0⟩⊗ℋA)⊕(|1⟩⊗ℋB).\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}=(\lvert 0\rangle\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A})\oplus(\lvert 1\rangle\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}).

Under the standard decomposition of a symmetric power of an
orthogonal sum,Symn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symn⁡(ℋB)\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{B})identifies with the
summand ofSym2​n⁡(ℋ⊕)\operatorname{Sym}^{2n}(\mathcal{H}_{\oplus})containingnnsites of each label.

The subspace ofSym2⁡(ℋ⊕)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{\oplus})containing one site of each label
is identified withℋA⊗ℋB\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}by the isometryV:ℋA⊗ℋB⟶Sym2⁡(ℋ⊕),V​(x⊗y)=|0,x⟩⊗|1,y⟩+|1,y⟩⊗|0,x⟩2.V:\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}),\qquad V(x\otimes y)=\frac{\lvert 0,x\rangle\otimes\lvert 1,y\rangle+\lvert 1,y\rangle\otimes\lvert 0,x\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}.(6.22)

## Lemma 6.6(Label-bundling embedding).

Letρ∈BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB)\rho\in\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B}). There is a bosonic stateρ^2​n∈D​(Sym2​n⁡(ℋ⊕))\widehat{\rho}_{2n}\in\mathrm{D}(\operatorname{Sym}^{2n}(\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}))whose two-site marginal
satisfiesV†​ρ^2​n(2)​V=qn​ρ,qn:=n2​n−1.V^{\dagger}\widehat{\rho}_{2n}^{(2)}V=q_{n}\rho,\qquad q_{n}:=\frac{n}{2n-1}.(6.23)

Thus the cross-label block on the left has traceqnq_{n}. Equivalently,
measuring the two labels gives one label of each
type with probabilityqnq_{n}, and conditioned on this event the state onℋA⊗ℋB\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}is exactlyρ\rho.

## Proof.

Choose an extensionρ~\widetilde{\rho}from
(2.3). By
Fact2.4,Sym2​n⁡(ℋ⊕)≅⨁k=02​nSymk⁡(ℋA)⊗Sym2​n−k⁡(ℋB).\operatorname{Sym}^{2n}(\mathcal{H}_{\oplus})\cong\bigoplus_{k=0}^{2n}\operatorname{Sym}^{k}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{2n-k}(\mathcal{H}_{B}).

We may therefore regardρ~\widetilde{\rho}as a bosonic stateρ^2​n\widehat{\rho}_{2n}supported on thek=nk=nsummand.

It remains to compute the cross-label block of the two-site marginal.
Under this fixed-label-count identification, the block is the uniform
average of then2n^{2}cross marginals:V†​ρ^2​n(2)​V=(2​n2)−1​∑i=1n∑j=1nρ~Ai​Bj.V^{\dagger}\widehat{\rho}_{2n}^{(2)}V=\binom{2n}{2}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\widetilde{\rho}_{A_{i}B_{j}}.

Bose symmetry within each block makes everyρ~Ai​Bj\widetilde{\rho}_{A_{i}B_{j}}equal toρ\rho. HenceV†​ρ^2​n(2)​V=n2(2​n2)​ρ=n2​n−1​ρ.V^{\dagger}\widehat{\rho}_{2n}^{(2)}V=\frac{n^{2}}{\binom{2n}{2}}\rho=\frac{n}{2n-1}\rho.

The coefficientn2/(2​n2)=n/(2​n−1)n^{2}/\binom{2n}{2}=n/(2n-1)is precisely the
probability that a uniformly chosen pair contains one site of each
label. This proves (6.23).
∎

We also record how the same isometry acts on Hermitian operators.

## Fact 6.7.

ForM∈Herm⁡(ℋA⊗ℋB)M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}), defineAM:=V​M​V†A_{M}:=VMV^{\dagger}(6.24)

onSym2⁡(ℋ⊕)\operatorname{Sym}^{2}(\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}), acting as zero on(range⁡V)⟂(\operatorname{range}V)^{\perp}. Thenh​(AM)=12​max⁡{hSEP​(M),0},‖AM‖2=‖M‖2.h(A_{M})=\frac{1}{2}\max\{h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M),0\},\qquad\left\lVert A_{M}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}.(6.25)

If eitherM⪰0M\succeq 0orTr⁡M=0\operatorname{Tr}M=0, thenhSEP​(M)≥0h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\geq 0and henceh​(AM)=12​hSEP​(M)h(A_{M})=\frac{1}{2}h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M).

## Proof.

Write a unit vector inℋ⊕\mathcal{H}_{\oplus}asz=p​|0,x⟩+q​|1,y⟩,z=\sqrt{p}\,\lvert 0,x\rangle+\sqrt{q}\,\lvert 1,y\rangle,

wherex,yx,yare unit vectors andp,q≥0p,q\geq 0satisfyp+q=1p+q=1. SinceV†​z⊗2=2​p​q​x⊗yV^{\dagger}z^{\otimes 2}=\sqrt{2pq}\,x\otimes y,⟨z⊗2|AM|z⊗2⟩=2pq⟨x⊗y|M|x⊗y⟩.\langle z^{\otimes 2}\rvert A_{M}\lvert z^{\otimes 2}\rangle=2pq\langle x\otimes y\rvert M\lvert x\otimes y\rangle.

Maximizing overp,q,x,yp,q,x,ygives the first identity in
(6.25). SinceVVis an isometry,AMA_{M}is unitarily equivalent toM⊕0M\oplus 0, which proves the
Hilbert–Schmidt norm identity. Finally,hSEP​(M)≥0h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\geq 0is
immediate whenM⪰0M\succeq 0. IfTr⁡M=0\operatorname{Tr}M=0, then the average of⟨x⊗y|M|x⊗y⟩\langle x\otimes y\rvert M\lvert x\otimes y\rangleover independent Haar-random unit vectorsxxandyyisTr⁡(M)/(dA​dB)=0\operatorname{Tr}(M)/(d_{A}d_{B})=0, so its maximum is nonnegative.
∎

Theorem3.9gives the
dimension-dependent estimate below. Combining the label-bundling
embedding in Lemma6.6with
Theorem6.1gives the dimension-free estimate.

## Corollary 6.8(Dimension-free Hilbert–Schmidt de Finetti bound for
two-sided Bose-symmetric extendible states).

For everyn,dA,dB≥1n,d_{A},d_{B}\geq 1,DHHS​(BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB),SEP⁡(dA,dB))≤min⁡{min⁡{dA,dB}−1n,8​2​n−1n}.D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B}),\,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\Bigr)\leq\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}}{n},\frac{8\sqrt{2n-1}}{n}\right\}.(6.26)

## Proof of Corollary6.8.

Fixρ∈BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB)\rho\in\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B}). By
Theorem3.9, there is a stateσ∈SEP⁡(dA,dB)\sigma\in\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})such thatDtr​(ρ,σ)≤min⁡{dA,dB}−12​n.D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\sigma)\leq\frac{\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}}{2n}.

Therefore,DHS​(ρ,σ)≤‖ρ−σ‖1=2​Dtr​(ρ,σ)≤min⁡{dA,dB}−1n.D_{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho,\sigma)\leq\left\lVert\rho-\sigma\right\rVert_{1}=2D_{\mathrm{tr}}(\rho,\sigma)\leq\frac{\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}}{n}.

This proves the dimension-dependent estimate.

For the dimension-free estimate, by duality
(Lemma2.1),DHS​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB))=maxM=M†‖M‖2≤1⁡{Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hSEP​(M)}.D_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr)=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}M=M^{\dagger}\\
\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\right\}.(6.27)

The maximum may be restricted to tracelessMM: replacingMMbyM−Tr⁡MdA​dB​IM-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}M}{d_{A}d_{B}}Ileaves the objective unchanged and can only
decrease its Hilbert–Schmidt norm.
ForAM:=V​M​V†A_{M}:=VMV^{\dagger}, Fact6.7and
the tracelessness ofMMgiveh​(AM)=12​hSEP​(M),‖AM‖2=‖M‖2.h(A_{M})=\frac{1}{2}h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M),\qquad\left\lVert A_{M}\right\rVert_{2}=\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}.

Letρ^2​n\widehat{\rho}_{2n}be the bosonic state supplied by
Lemma6.6, and setε2​n:=DHHS​(BOS2​n(2)⁡(dA+dB),SEPH(2)⁡(dA+dB)).\varepsilon_{2n}:=D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{2n}^{(2)}(d_{A}+d_{B}),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d_{A}+d_{B})\Bigr).

There is a Hartree mixtureτ∈SEPH(2)⁡(dA+dB)\tau\in\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d_{A}+d_{B})such that‖ρ^2​n(2)−τ‖2≤ε2​n\left\lVert\widehat{\rho}_{2n}^{(2)}-\tau\right\rVert_{2}\leq\varepsilon_{2n}.
SinceTr⁡(AM​τ)≤h​(AM)\operatorname{Tr}(A_{M}\tau)\leq h(A_{M}), Hilbert–Schmidt Cauchy–Schwarz and
Fact6.7giveTr⁡(AM​ρ^2​n(2))≤12​hSEP​(M)+ε2​n​‖M‖2.\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(A_{M}\widehat{\rho}_{2n}^{(2)}\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{2}h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)+\varepsilon_{2n}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}.

On the other hand, the label-bundling identity
(6.23) givesTr⁡(AM​ρ^2​n(2))=n2​n−1​Tr⁡(M​ρ)\operatorname{Tr}(A_{M}\widehat{\rho}_{2n}^{(2)})=\frac{n}{2n-1}\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho).
Thus, for every tracelessMMwith‖M‖2≤1\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1,Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hSEP​(M)\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)≤2​n−1n​ε2​n​‖M‖2−12​n​hSEP​(M)\displaystyle\leq\frac{2n-1}{n}\varepsilon_{2n}\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}-\frac{1}{2n}h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)≤2​n−1n​ε2​n,\displaystyle\leq\frac{2n-1}{n}\varepsilon_{2n},

where the last inequality useshSEP​(M)≥0h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\geq 0. Taking the maximum
in (6.27) and then the supremum overρ\rhogives, by (2.7),DHHS​(BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB),SEP⁡(dA,dB))≤2​n−1n​ε2​n.D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B}),\,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\Bigr)\leq\frac{2n-1}{n}\varepsilon_{2n}.

Theorem6.1, applied withN=2​nN=2n, givesε2​n≤8/2​n−1\varepsilon_{2n}\leq 8/\sqrt{2n-1}and thereforeDHHS​(BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB),SEP⁡(dA,dB))≤8​2​n−1n.D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B}),\,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\Bigr)\leq\frac{8\sqrt{2n-1}}{n}.

## 7Hilbert–Schmidt separability algorithms

The dimension-free estimate for two-sided Bose-symmetric extendible
states has two algorithmic consequences. The two-sided extension
relaxation approximateshSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)for Hermitian objectives whose
traceless part has bounded Hilbert–Schmidt norm. Through
Hilbert–Schmidt duality, the same approximation also gives
Hilbert–Schmidt separability testing.
At fixed accuracy, the required extension level is independent of the
local dimensions, so both programs have polynomial size in those
dimensions.

LetℋA≅ℂdA\mathcal{H}_{A}\cong\mathbb{C}^{d_{A}}andℋB≅ℂdB\mathcal{H}_{B}\cong\mathbb{C}^{d_{B}}. Forn≥1n\geq 1, writeδnHS​(dA,dB):=min⁡{min⁡{dA,dB}−1n,8​2​n−1n}.\delta_{n}^{\mathrm{HS}}(d_{A},d_{B}):=\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}}{n},\frac{8\sqrt{2n-1}}{n}\right\}.(7.1)

## 7.1Separable optimization with bounded Hilbert–Schmidt norm

ForM∈Herm⁡(ℋA⊗ℋB)M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}), setm:=Tr⁡MdA​dB,M0:=M−m​I.m:=\frac{\operatorname{Tr}M}{d_{A}d_{B}},\qquad M_{0}:=M-mI.(7.2)

Recall from Subsection5.2thathBEXTn,n​(M):=maxρ∈BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB)⁡Tr⁡(M​ρ)=λmax​(M[n,n]).h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M):=\max_{\rho\in\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B})}\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)=\lambda_{\max}\bigl(M^{[n,n]}\bigr).

Adding a scalar multiple of the identity shifts the value ofMMby
the same amount on every state. HencehSEP​(M)\displaystyle h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)=m+hSEP​(M0),\displaystyle=m+h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M_{0}),(7.3)hBEXTn,n​(M)\displaystyle h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M)=m+hBEXTn,n​(M0),\displaystyle=m+h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M_{0}),‖M0‖2\displaystyle\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}≤‖M‖2.\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}.

The last inequality holds becauseM↦M0M\mapsto M_{0}is the orthogonal
projection onto the traceless subspace in the Hilbert–Schmidt inner
product.

## Theorem 7.1(Separable optimization with bounded Hilbert–Schmidt norm).

For every HermitianMMand everyn≥1n\geq 1,hSEP​(M)≤hBEXTn,n​(M)≤hSEP​(M)+δnHS​(dA,dB)​‖M0‖2.h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\leq h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M)\leq h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)+\delta_{n}^{\mathrm{HS}}(d_{A},d_{B})\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}.(7.4)

Consequently, given an explicit rational description ofMMandε∈(0,1]\varepsilon\in(0,1], one can approximatehSEP​(M)h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)to additive
errorε\varepsilondeterministically. Up to a factor polynomial inbits⁡(M)\operatorname{bits}(M)andlog⁡(1/ε)\log(1/\varepsilon), the running time isexp⁡(O​((1+min⁡{min⁡{dA,dB}−1​‖M0‖2ε,‖M0‖22ε2})​log⁡(dA+dB+‖M0‖2ε+2))).\exp\left(O\left(\left(1+\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}}{\varepsilon},\frac{\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}\right)\log\left(d_{A}+d_{B}+\frac{\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}}{\varepsilon}+2\right)\right)\right).(7.5)

In particular, for bounded‖M0‖2\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}and fixedε\varepsilon, the
running time is polynomial in the local dimensions.

## Proof.

The lower bound in (7.4) follows fromSEP⁡(dA,dB)⊆BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB)\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\subseteq\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B}). For the upper bound,
fixρ∈BEXTn,n⁡(dA,dB)\rho\in\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d_{A},d_{B}). Corollary6.8givesσ∈SEP⁡(dA,dB)\sigma\in\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})such that‖ρ−σ‖2≤δnHS​(dA,dB).\left\lVert\rho-\sigma\right\rVert_{2}\leq\delta_{n}^{\mathrm{HS}}(d_{A},d_{B}).

SinceMMandM0M_{0}differ by a scalar multiple of the identity,
Hilbert–Schmidt Cauchy–Schwarz givesTr⁡(M​ρ)−hSEP​(M)\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)=Tr⁡(M0​ρ)−hSEP​(M0)\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}(M_{0}\rho)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M_{0})≤Tr⁡(M0​(ρ−σ))\displaystyle\leq\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(M_{0}(\rho-\sigma)\bigr)≤δnHS​(dA,dB)​‖M0‖2.\displaystyle\leq\delta_{n}^{\mathrm{HS}}(d_{A},d_{B})\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}.

Maximizing overρ\rhoproves the upper bound.

For the algorithm, taken:=1+⌈min⁡{2​min⁡{dA,dB}−1​‖M0‖2ε,512​‖M0‖22ε2}⌉.n:=1+\left\lceil\min\left\{\frac{2\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}}{\varepsilon},\frac{512\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}\right\rceil.

Then the error term in (7.4) is at mostε/2\varepsilon/2.
Compute the largest eigenvalue ofM[n,n]M^{[n,n]}to additive precisionε/2\varepsilon/2. The lift acts on a space of dimensionLdA,dB,n=(dA+n−1n)​(dB+n−1n).L_{d_{A},d_{B},n}=\binom{d_{A}+n-1}{n}\binom{d_{B}+n-1}{n}.

The standard binomial estimate andn=O​(1+min⁡{min⁡{dA,dB}−1​‖M0‖2ε,‖M0‖22ε2})n=O\left(1+\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}}{\varepsilon},\frac{\left\lVert M_{0}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}\right)give
(7.5).
∎

## 7.2Hilbert–Schmidt separability testing

Given a bipartite stateρ\rho, Hilbert–Schmidt separability testing
asks whetherρ\rhois separable or is at Hilbert–Schmidt distance at
leastε\varepsilonfrom every separable state. This is weak membership
for the separable set in Hilbert–Schmidt distance. By duality
(Lemma2.1),DHS​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB))=maxM∈Herm⁡(ℋA⊗ℋB)Tr⁡M=0,‖M‖2≤1⁡{Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hSEP​(M)}.D_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr)=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})\\
\operatorname{Tr}M=0,\ \left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\right\}.(7.6)

We replacehSEPh_{\operatorname{SEP}}in
(7.6) by the relaxation from the preceding
subsection.

## Theorem 7.2(Polynomial-time Hilbert–Schmidt separability testing).

Given an explicit rational stateρ∈D​(ℂdA⊗ℂdB)\rho\in\mathrm{D}(\mathbb{C}^{d_{A}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d_{B}})andε∈(0,1]\varepsilon\in(0,1], one can
approximateDHS​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB))D_{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B}))to additive errorε\varepsilondeterministically. Up to a factor polynomial inbits⁡(ρ)\operatorname{bits}(\rho)andlog⁡(1/ε)\log(1/\varepsilon), the running time isexp⁡(O​((1+min⁡{min⁡{dA,dB}−1ε,1ε2})​log⁡(dA+dB+ε−1))).\exp\left(O\left(\left(1+\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}}{\varepsilon},\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}\right)\log(d_{A}+d_{B}+\varepsilon^{-1})\right)\right).(7.7)

In the same running time one can distinguishρ∈SEP⁡(dA,dB)fromDHS​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB))≥ε.\rho\in\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\qquad\text{from}\qquad D_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr)\geq\varepsilon.(7.8)

Thus, for every fixedε>0\varepsilon>0, Hilbert–Schmidt separability
testing is polynomial-time solvable in the explicit input dimensions.

## Proof.

Forn≥1n\geq 1, defineD^nHS​(ρ):=maxM∈Herm⁡(ℋA⊗ℋB)Tr⁡M=0,‖M‖2≤1⁡{Tr⁡(M​ρ)−hBEXTn,n​(M)}.\widehat{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho):=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})\\
\operatorname{Tr}M=0,\ \left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\left\{\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M)\right\}.(7.9)

For every feasibleMM, Theorem7.1giveshSEP​(M)≤hBEXTn,n​(M)≤hSEP​(M)+δnHS​(dA,dB).h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)\leq h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M)\leq h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(M)+\delta_{n}^{\mathrm{HS}}(d_{A},d_{B}).

Subtracting these inequalities fromTr⁡(M​ρ)\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho), maximizing overMM,
and applying (7.6) givesDHS​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB))−δnHS​(dA,dB)≤D^nHS​(ρ)≤DHS​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB)).D_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr)-\delta_{n}^{\mathrm{HS}}(d_{A},d_{B})\leq\widehat{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho)\leq D_{\mathrm{HS}}\bigl(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B})\bigr).(7.10)

SincehBEXTn,n​(M)=λmax​(M[n,n])h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M)=\lambda_{\max}(M^{[n,n]}),
the relaxation (7.9) is the
semidefinite programmaximizeTr⁡(M​ρ)−t\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}(M\rho)-t(7.11)subject toTr⁡M=0,\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}M=0,(I(dA​dB)2vec⁡(M)vec(M)†1)⪰0,\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}I_{(d_{A}d_{B})^{2}}&\operatorname{vec}(M)\\
\operatorname{vec}(M)^{\dagger}&1\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0,M[n,n]⪯t​ISymn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symn⁡(ℋB),\displaystyle M^{[n,n]}\preceq tI_{\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{B})},

with variablesM∈Herm⁡(ℋA⊗ℋB)M\in\operatorname{Herm}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})andt∈ℝt\in\mathbb{R},
wherevec⁡(M)\operatorname{vec}(M)denotes the vectorization ofMM.
The first matrix inequality is equivalent to‖M‖2≤1\left\lVert M\right\rVert_{2}\leq 1by the
Schur complement, while the second is equivalent tot≥hBEXTn,n​(M)t\geq h_{\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}}(M). Hence the SDP has valueD^nHS​(ρ)\widehat{D}_{n}^{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho).

For the approximation algorithm, taken:=1+⌈min⁡{2​min⁡{dA,dB}−1ε,512ε2}⌉.n:=1+\left\lceil\min\left\{\frac{2\sqrt{\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}-1}}{\varepsilon},\frac{512}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\}\right\rceil.

ThenδnHS​(dA,dB)≤ε/2\delta_{n}^{\mathrm{HS}}(d_{A},d_{B})\leq\varepsilon/2.
Solving (7.11) to additive precisionε/2\varepsilon/2therefore gives an additive-ε\varepsilonapproximation by
(7.10).

The two semidefinite constraints have dimensions(dA​dB)2+1(d_{A}d_{B})^{2}+1andLdA,dB,n=(dA+n−1n)​(dB+n−1n),L_{d_{A},d_{B},n}=\binom{d_{A}+n-1}{n}\binom{d_{B}+n-1}{n},

respectively.
Standard algorithms for semidefinite programs, together with the
binomial estimate, give (7.7).

For the promise problem, increasennby a constant factor so thatδnHS​(dA,dB)≤ε/4\delta_{n}^{\mathrm{HS}}(d_{A},d_{B})\leq\varepsilon/4and solve to precisionε/8\varepsilon/8. The relaxation value is zero whenρ\rhois separable
and is at least3​ε/43\varepsilon/4whenDHS​(ρ,SEP⁡(dA,dB))≥εD_{\mathrm{HS}}(\rho,\operatorname{SEP}(d_{A},d_{B}))\geq\varepsilon. Thus the numerical output is
at mostε/8\varepsilon/8in the separable case and at least5​ε/85\varepsilon/8in the far case. Comparing it withε/2\varepsilon/2distinguishes the two
cases.
∎

For fixed additive accuracy, this improves the quasipolynomial
Hilbert–Schmidt weak-membership guarantee of Brandão, Christandl,
and Yard[BCY11b]to polynomial time in the local
dimensions. These algorithms take explicit matrices as input and do
not give a copy-efficient test for an unknown quantum state. Since
Hilbert–Schmidt distance is not contractive under general quantum
channels, they do not replace trace- or LOCC-norm separability testing.

estimate is the complex pure-state form of the argmax argument
underlying our argmax-SoS analysis of Best Separable
State[JWX26, Sec. 3].

LetℋA\mathcal{H}_{A}andℋB\mathcal{H}_{B}be finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, and use the

Subsubsection3.2.1.

Section2.4.

The vectorsx⊗n⊗y⊗mx^{\otimes n}\otimes y^{\otimes m}spanSymn⁡(ℋA)⊗Symm⁡(ℋB)\operatorname{Sym}^{n}(\mathcal{H}_{A})\otimes\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(\mathcal{H}_{B}). Fix integersn,m≥1n,m\geq 1and a unit vectorψ\psiin this space. Choose unit vectorsx,yx,ymaximizing|⟨x⊗n⊗y⊗m,ψ⟩|\left\lvert\langle x^{\otimes n}\otimes y^{\otimes m},\psi\rangle\right\rvert. After adjusting the
phase ofxx, setc:=⟨x⊗n⊗y⊗m,ψ⟩>0,c:=\langle x^{\otimes n}\otimes y^{\otimes m},\psi\rangle>0,(A.1)

and contract all but one tensor factor in each block:ηx,y:=[(⟨x|⊗(n−1)⊗IℋA)⊗(⟨y|⊗(m−1)⊗IℋB)]ψ∈ℋA⊗ℋB.\eta_{x,y}:=\Bigl[(\langle x\rvert^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes I_{\mathcal{H}_{A}})\otimes(\langle y\rvert^{\otimes(m-1)}\otimes I_{\mathcal{H}_{B}})\Bigr]\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}.(A.2)

## Proof of Proposition3.10.

B_{x,y}&\in x^{\perp}\otimes y^{\perp}.\end{aligned}(A.7)

Combining (A.6),

## Appendix BLower bounds for the de Finetti theorems

## B.1Optimality of thett-site bounds

The two-site rectangular Werner examples of Christandl, König,
Mitchison, and Renner, together with their symmetric purifications,
already show that the dimension dependence of our bounds is
optimal[CKMR07, Lemma II.5 and Corollary III.9]. By
contractivity under partial trace, the same conclusion holds for every
fixed marginal ordertt. We use their rectangular families below
only to prove the new point: the linear dependence onttis also
necessary.

FixN=a​rN=ar. Letωa​r,(ar)\omega_{ar,(a^{r})}be the rectangular Werner
state: the normalized projector onto the(ar)(a^{r})Schur–Weyl block of(ℂr)⊗a​r(\mathbb{C}^{r})^{\otimes ar}. Letψa,r\psi_{a,r}be its canonical symmetric
purification inSyma​r⁡(ℂr⊗ℂr)\operatorname{Sym}^{ar}(\mathbb{C}^{r}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{r}), equivalently the unit vector in the
one-dimensional Cauchy summandS(ar)​(ℂr)⊗S(ar)​(ℂr).S_{(a^{r})}(\mathbb{C}^{r})\otimes S_{(a^{r})}(\mathbb{C}^{r}).

We writeγa,r(t)\gamma_{a,r}^{(t)}andωa​r,(ar)(t)\omega_{ar,(a^{r})}^{(t)}for their respectivett-site marginals.
These are the rectangular families analyzed in[CKMR07]; the
calculation below extracts their dependence ontt.

## B.1.1Exact Schur-label distances

The same rectangular families also detect the dependence on the
marginal order. The cleanest formulation uses the Schur label of thett-site marginal. For a partitionμ⊢t\mu\vdash t, letfμf^{\mu}be the

for the content and hook length of a box□=(i,j)∈μ\square=(i,j)\in\mu. We use(x)t=x​(x−1)​⋯​(x−t+1)(x)_{t}=x(x-1)\cdots(x-t+1)

for the falling factorial.

A partition withμ1>a\mu_{1}>ahas zero weight in

## Lemma B.1(Rectangular branching weights).

Under the Cauchy decompositionSymt⁡(ℂr⊗ℂr)=⨁μ⊢tℓ​(μ)≤rSμ​(ℂr)⊗Sμ​(ℂr),\operatorname{Sym}^{t}(\mathbb{C}^{r}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{r})=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\vdash t\\
\ell(\mu)\leq r\end{subarray}}S_{\mu}(\mathbb{C}^{r})\otimes S_{\mu}(\mathbb{C}^{r}),

the block weights ofγa,r(t)\gamma_{a,r}^{(t)}arepa,r,t​(μ)p_{a,r,t}(\mu). Under the Schur–Weyl decomposition of(ℂr)⊗t(\mathbb{C}^{r})^{\otimes t}, the block weights ofωa​r,(ar)(t)\omega_{ar,(a^{r})}^{(t)}are the same numbers. Moreover,pa,r,t​(μ)=dimSμ​(ℂr)​dimSμ′​(ℂa)(a​rt).p_{a,r,t}(\mu)=\frac{\dim S_{\mu}(\mathbb{C}^{r})\,\dim S_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbb{C}^{a})}{\binom{ar}{t}}.(B.4)

In particular, the numbers in
(B.1) form a probability distribution.

## Proof.

Both restrictions are governed by the coherent down-transition measure
from the rectangular Specht moduleV(ar)V_{(a^{r})}:pa,r,t​(μ)=fμ​f(ar)/μf(ar).p_{a,r,t}(\mu)=\frac{f^{\mu}f^{(a^{r})/\mu}}{f^{(a^{r})}}.(B.5)

For the Werner state, this follows by restricting the normalized
identity onV(ar)V_{(a^{r})}.

We give the corresponding argument forψa,r\psi_{a,r}.
Setλ=(ar)\lambda=(a^{r})and choose a real orthonormal model of the Specht
moduleVλV_{\lambda}. In the Schur–Weyl realization of the Cauchy
summand, the Specht factor ofψa,r\psi_{a,r}is the
normalized diagonal invariantΩλ:=1fλ​∑j=1fλej⊗ej∈Vλ⊗Vλ.\Omega_{\lambda}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{f^{\lambda}}}\sum_{j=1}^{f^{\lambda}}e_{j}\otimes e_{j}\in V_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\lambda}.

Upon restriction toStS_{t},Vλ↓St≅⨁μ⊢tμ⊆λVμ⊗Mλ/μ,dimMλ/μ=fλ/μ.V_{\lambda}\downarrow_{S_{t}}\cong\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\vdash t\\
\mu\subseteq\lambda\end{subarray}}V_{\mu}\otimes M_{\lambda/\mu},\qquad\dim M_{\lambda/\mu}=f^{\lambda/\mu}.

Choose the basis(ej)(e_{j})adapted to this orthogonal decomposition and
letQμQ_{\mu}be the projection ontoVμ⊗Mλ/μV_{\mu}\otimes M_{\lambda/\mu}. Then‖(Qμ⊗Qμ)​Ωλ‖2=rank⁡Qμfλ=fμ​fλ/μfλ.\left\lVert(Q_{\mu}\otimes Q_{\mu})\Omega_{\lambda}\right\rVert^{2}=\frac{\operatorname{rank}Q_{\mu}}{f^{\lambda}}=\frac{f^{\mu}f^{\lambda/\mu}}{f^{\lambda}}.

BecauseΩλ\Omega_{\lambda}is invariant under the diagonal actiong⊗gg\otimes gof𝔖t\mathfrak{S}_{t}andQμQ_{\mu}commutes with that action,(Qμ⊗Qμ)​Ωλ(Q_{\mu}\otimes Q_{\mu})\Omega_{\lambda}is itself diagonal invariant. On
theμ⊗μ\mu\otimes\muisotypic component, the Cauchy-block projector for
the firstttpaired factors is the projection onto(Vμ⊗Vμ)diag⁡(𝔖t)(V_{\mu}\otimes V_{\mu})^{\operatorname{diag}(\mathfrak{S}_{t})}, tensored
with the identity on the multiplicity spaces. It therefore acts as

(B.5) forγa,r(t)\gamma_{a,r}^{(t)}as well.

The shifted-Schur skew-dimension formula, specialized to a rectangle,
gives[OO97, Theorem 8.1]f(ar)/μf(ar)=1(a​r)t​∏□∈μ(a−c​(□))​(r+c​(□))hμ​(□).\frac{f^{(a^{r})/\mu}}{f^{(a^{r})}}=\frac{1}{(ar)_{t}}\prod_{\square\in\mu}\frac{(a-c(\square))(r+c(\square))}{h_{\mu}(\square)}.

For a probability vectorx=(x1,…,xr)x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}), writeSWt,x⁡(μ):=fμ​sμ​(x).\operatorname{SW}_{t,x}(\mu):=f^{\mu}s_{\mu}(x).(B.6)

## Proposition B.2(Exact higher-marginal distance).

For everya,r∈ℕa,r\in\mathbb{N},r≥2r\geq 2, and2≤t≤a​r2\leq t\leq ar,Dtr​(γa,r(t),SEPH(t)⁡(r2))\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\gamma_{a,r}^{(t)},\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(r^{2})\bigr)=12​∑μ⊢tℓ​(μ)≤rqr,t​(μ)​|Ra,r,t​(μ)−1|,\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\vdash t\\
\ell(\mu)\leq r\end{subarray}}q_{r,t}(\mu)\left\lvert R_{a,r,t}(\mu)-1\right\rvert,(B.7)Dtr​(ωa​r,(ar)(t),SEPP(t)⁡(r))\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\omega_{ar,(a^{r})}^{(t)},\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(r)\bigr)=12​∑μ⊢tℓ​(μ)≤rqr,t​(μ)​|Ra,r,t​(μ)−1|.\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\vdash t\\
\ell(\mu)\leq r\end{subarray}}q_{r,t}(\mu)\left\lvert R_{a,r,t}(\mu)-1\right\rvert.(B.8)

A closest invariant Hartree mixture in the first line is the twirl of|Φr⟩⟨Φr|⊗t\lvert\Phi_{r}\rangle\langle\Phi_{r}\rvert^{\otimes t}, whereΦr=r−1/2​∑iei⊗ei\Phi_{r}=r^{-1/2}\sum_{i}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}; a closest invariant state of the
formτ⊗t\tau^{\otimes t}in the second line is(I/r)⊗t(I/r)^{\otimes t}. An optimal
separating operator0⪯A⪯I0\preceq A\preceq Iis
the sum of the Schur projectors indexed byℒa,r,t:={μ:Ra,r,t​(μ)≥1}.\mathcal{L}_{a,r,t}:=\{\mu:R_{a,r,t}(\mu)\geq 1\}.(B.9)

## Proof.

Twirling is trace-norm contractive, fixes the two target states, and
preserves the respective approximation sets. On the Cauchy side, theU​(r)×U​(r)U(r)\times U(r)decomposition is multiplicity free. On the Werner
side, the target and every twirled approximant commute with both theU​(r)U(r)andStS_{t}actions and are therefore scalar on each joint
Schur–Weyl blockSμ​(ℂr)⊗VμS_{\mu}(\mathbb{C}^{r})\otimes V_{\mu}. Consequently, trace distance
after twirling is the total-variation distance between the normalized
block weights. It remains to show thatqr,tq_{r,t}is closest among
convex combinations ofSWt,x\operatorname{SW}_{t,x}.

Order partitions by dominance, withν⊵μ\nu\unrhd\mumeaning thatν\nuis more dominant. The likelihood ratio in
(B.3) is decreasing in this order.
Indeed, a covering move transfers one box from a lower row to a higher
row and strictly increases its content; the corresponding factora−c​(□)a-c(\square)decreases, while all other factors are unchanged. If
the move leaves thea×ra\times rrectangle, the new likelihood ratio is
zero. Consequently,ℒa,r,t\mathcal{L}_{a,r,t}is a lower ideal in
dominance order.

Every probability vectorxxmajorizesx¯\bar{x}. The Schur–Weyl
majorization coupling states that ifxxmajorizesyy, one may coupleμx∼SWt,x\mu_{x}\sim\operatorname{SW}_{t,x}andμy∼SWt,y\mu_{y}\sim\operatorname{SW}_{t,y}so thatμx⊵μy\mu_{x}\unrhd\mu_{y}almost surely[OW16, Thm. 1.11]. Therefore
every lower idealℒ\mathcal{L}satisfiesSWt,x⁡(ℒ)≤qr,t​(ℒ).\operatorname{SW}_{t,x}(\mathcal{L})\leq q_{r,t}(\mathcal{L}).(B.10)

The same inequality holds for every convex combination of Schur–Weyl
distributions.

Letp=pa,r,tp=p_{a,r,t}andq=qr,tq=q_{r,t}. The setℒa,r,t={p≥q}\mathcal{L}_{a,r,t}=\{p\geq q\}is the Neyman–Pearson set forppversusqq. For every feasible invariant approximant with block
distributionss,DTV​(p,s)\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{TV}}(p,s)≥p​(ℒa,r,t)−s​(ℒa,r,t)\displaystyle\geq p(\mathcal{L}_{a,r,t})-s(\mathcal{L}_{a,r,t})≥p​(ℒa,r,t)−q​(ℒa,r,t)=DTV​(p,q).\displaystyle\geq p(\mathcal{L}_{a,r,t})-q(\mathcal{L}_{a,r,t})=D_{\mathrm{TV}}(p,q).

Sinceqqitself is feasible in both problems, equality follows, as
does the optimal separating operator.
∎

The exact formula reduces optimality to a scalar Schur-label
calculation. We next extract a lower bound showing that the
linear dependence onttin Theorem4.1is
necessary. Letct​(μ):=∑□∈μc​(□),Mt:=(t2).\mathrm{ct}(\mu):=\sum_{\square\in\mu}c(\square),\qquad M_{t}:=\binom{t}{2}.(B.11)

The transposition class sum𝖳t=∑1≤i<j≤t(i​j)\mathsf{T}_{t}=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq t}(ij)acts onVμV_{\mu}as the scalarct​(μ)\mathrm{ct}(\mu).

## B.1.2Optimal dependence on the marginal order

## Lemma B.3(Plancherel content fluctuations).

Lett≥2t\geq 2, and letμ\muhave Plancherel lawPlt⁡(μ)=(fμ)2/t!\operatorname{Pl}_{t}(\mu)=(f^{\mu})^{2}/t!. There is a universal
constantc0>0c_{0}>0such thatc0​t≤𝔼Plt​|ct​(μ)|≤Mt.c_{0}t\leq\mathbb{E}_{\operatorname{Pl}_{t}}\left\lvert\mathrm{ct}(\mu)\right\rvert\leq\sqrt{M_{t}}.(B.12)

## Proof.

Use the normalized traceτreg\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}on the regular
representation of𝔖t\mathfrak{S}_{t}, and write‖X‖p,reg:=τreg​(|X|p)1/p.\left\lVert X\right\rVert_{p,\mathrm{reg}}:=\tau_{\mathrm{reg}}(\left\lvert X\right\rvert^{p})^{1/p}.

Distinct group elements are orthonormal in the associatedL2L_{2}inner
product, so𝔼​ct​(μ)=0,𝔼​ct​(μ)2=Mt.\mathbb{E}\,\mathrm{ct}(\mu)=0,\qquad\mathbb{E}\,\mathrm{ct}(\mu)^{2}=M_{t}.

Moreover,𝔼​ct​(μ)4\mathbb{E}\,\mathrm{ct}(\mu)^{4}is the number of ordered
quadruples of transpositions whose product is the identity. Associate
to such a quadruple the four-edge multigraph formed by its
transpositions. A vertex of degree one cannot occur in a word whose
product is the identity. Hence every nonisolated vertex has degree at
least two, so the graph uses at most four vertices. There are onlyO​(t4)O(t^{4})such ordered quadruples, and therefore𝔼​ct​(μ)4≤C0​t4\mathbb{E}\,\mathrm{ct}(\mu)^{4}\leq C_{0}t^{4}

for a universalC0C_{0}. Hölder’s inequality gives𝔼​|ct​(μ)|≥(𝔼​ct​(μ)2)3/2(𝔼​ct​(μ)4)1/2≥c0​t.\mathbb{E}\left\lvert\mathrm{ct}(\mu)\right\rvert\geq\frac{\bigl(\mathbb{E}\,\mathrm{ct}(\mu)^{2}\bigr)^{3/2}}{\bigl(\mathbb{E}\,\mathrm{ct}(\mu)^{4}\bigr)^{1/2}}\geq c_{0}t.

The upper bound is Cauchy–Schwarz.
∎

## Theorem B.5(Optimaltt-dependence of the rectangular examples).

There is a universal constantC>0C>0such that, for everya,r∈ℕa,r\in\mathbb{N}and2≤t≤a​r2\leq t\leq arsatisfyingr≥C​t2r\geq Ct^{2},Dtr​(γa,r(t),SEPH(t)⁡(r2))\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\gamma_{a,r}^{(t)},\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(t)}(r^{2})\bigr)=Ω​(min⁡{1,ta})=Ω​(min⁡{1,t​dN}),d=r2,\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\min\left\{1,\frac{t}{a}\right\}\right)=\Omega\left(\min\left\{1,\frac{t\sqrt{d}}{N}\right\}\right),\qquad d=r^{2},(B.19)Dtr​(ωa​r,(ar)(t),SEPP(t)⁡(r))\displaystyle D_{\mathrm{tr}}\bigl(\omega_{ar,(a^{r})}^{(t)},\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{P}}^{(t)}(r)\bigr)=Ω​(min⁡{1,ta})=Ω​(min⁡{1,t​dN}),d=r.\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\min\left\{1,\frac{t}{a}\right\}\right)=\Omega\left(\min\left\{1,\frac{td}{N}\right\}\right),\qquad d=r.(B.20)

Together with Theorem4.1, these estimates match up
to universal constants. In particular, the linear dependence on the
marginal order in (4.1) and
(4.2) cannot be improved in general.

## Proof.

By PropositionB.2, the two
distances are the same scalar quantity. Ifa≥C1​ta\geq C_{1}t, apply
LemmaB.4.

Supposea<C1​ta<C_{1}t. Distance to the relevant de Finetti set cannot
increase under partial trace, so thett-site distance is at least thess-site distance for every2≤s≤t2\leq s\leq t. Ifaais larger than a
sufficiently large universal constant, takes=⌊a2​C1⌋.s=\left\lfloor\frac{a}{2C_{1}}\right\rfloor.

Then2≤s≤t2\leq s\leq t,a≥C1​sa\geq C_{1}s, andr≥C​t2≥C1​s2r\geq Ct^{2}\geq C_{1}s^{2}after increasingCC. The perturbative lemma
gives a universal constant lower bound becauses/a≥1/(4​C1)s/a\geq 1/(4C_{1}). Ifaais bounded, use the two-site marginal
instead. PropositionB.2,
specialized to two sites, gives for both rectangular familiesDt≥D2=r2−12​r​(a​r−1)≥38​a,D_{t}\geq D_{2}=\frac{r^{2}-1}{2r(ar-1)}\geq\frac{3}{8a},

which is again bounded below by a universal constant. This proves the
lower bounds in the regimet/a≳1t/a\gtrsim 1and hence the displayed capped
estimates. The matching upper bounds follow from
Theorem4.1, together with the trace-distance
diameter bound, after substitutingN=a​rN=arand, respectively,d=r2d=r^{2}ord=rd=r.

## B.2Dimension-free bosonic Hilbert–Schmidt lower bounds

## Theorem B.6(Dimension-free bosonic Hilbert–Schmidt lower bound).

AsN→∞N\to\infty,supd≥2DHHS​(BOSN(2)⁡(d),SEPH(2)⁡(d))=Ω​(N−1/2).\sup_{d\geq 2}D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(2)}(d),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)\Bigr)=\Omega(N^{-1/2}).

The lower bound is included for completeness. It adapts the
Jiang–Tacla–Caves bosonic pair construction, based on symmetrized powers of a
two-site vector[JTC17]. We give the short calculation in
symmetric-tensor notation.

## Proof of TheoremB.6.

Fixm≥1m\geq 1, letℋm=ℂm+1\mathcal{H}_{m}=\mathbb{C}^{m+1}with orthonormal basise0,e1,…,eme_{0},e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}, and definebm:=1m∑i=1mei⊗2,Am:=|e0⊗2⟩⟨bm|+|bm⟩⟨e0⊗2|.b_{m}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i}^{\otimes 2},\qquad A_{m}:=\lvert e_{0}^{\otimes 2}\rangle\langle b_{m}\rvert+\lvert b_{m}\rangle\langle e_{0}^{\otimes 2}\rvert.(B.21)

The vectorse0⊗2e_{0}^{\otimes 2}andbmb_{m}are orthonormal, so‖Am‖2=2.\left\lVert A_{m}\right\rVert_{2}=\sqrt{2}.(B.22)

Ifu=α​e0+vu=\alpha e_{0}+vwithv⟂e0v\perp e_{0}, then|⟨u⊗2|Am|u⊗2⟩|≤2m|α|2∥v∥2≤12​m.\left\lvert\langle u^{\otimes 2}\rvert A_{m}\lvert u^{\otimes 2}\rangle\right\rvert\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{m}}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert^{2}\left\lVert v\right\rVert^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}.

Equality is attained atu=(e0+e1)/2u=(e_{0}+e_{1})/\sqrt{2}, and thereforeh​(Am)=12​m.h(A_{m})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}.(B.23)

Thus the Hartree value vanishes asm→∞m\to\infty.

Forq≥0q\geq 0, setxq(m):=Πsym(2​q)​bm⊗q,χq(m):=xq(m)‖xq(m)‖,x_{q}^{(m)}:=\Pi_{\mathrm{sym}}^{(2q)}b_{m}^{\otimes q},\qquad\chi_{q}^{(m)}:=\frac{x_{q}^{(m)}}{\left\lVert x_{q}^{(m)}\right\rVert},(B.24)

withx0(m)=χ0(m)=1x_{0}^{(m)}=\chi_{0}^{(m)}=1. Expanding in the orthonormal
monomial basis ofSym2​q⁡ℂm\operatorname{Sym}^{2q}\mathbb{C}^{m}gives‖xq(m)‖2=∏j=0q−1m+2​jm​(2​j+1).\left\lVert x_{q}^{(m)}\right\rVert^{2}=\prod_{j=0}^{q-1}\frac{m+2j}{m(2j+1)}.(B.25)

LetCbmξ:=(⟨bm|⊗I)ξC_{b_{m}}\xi:=(\langle b_{m}\rvert\otimes I)\xidenote the partial inner product
withbmb_{m}on two tensor factors. Bothbmb_{m}andχq+1(m)\chi_{q+1}^{(m)}are invariant under the naturalO​(m)O(m)action, andCbmC_{b_{m}}isO​(m)O(m)-equivariant. HenceCbm​χq+1(m)C_{b_{m}}\chi_{q+1}^{(m)}lies in(Sym2​q⁡ℂm)O​(m)(\operatorname{Sym}^{2q}\mathbb{C}^{m})^{O(m)}. This invariant subspace is one-dimensional,
spanned byχq(m)\chi_{q}^{(m)}; equivalently, everyO​(m)O(m)-invariant
homogeneous polynomial of degree2​q2qis a scalar multiple of(z12+⋯+zm2)q(z_{1}^{2}+\cdots+z_{m}^{2})^{q}. ThusCbm​χq+1(m)=κq​χq(m).C_{b_{m}}\chi_{q+1}^{(m)}=\kappa_{q}\chi_{q}^{(m)}.

On symmetric tensors, the adjoint ofCbmC_{b_{m}}isξ↦Πsym(2​q+2)​(bm⊗ξ)\xi\mapsto\Pi_{\mathrm{sym}}^{(2q+2)}(b_{m}\otimes\xi). Taking the inner
product withχq+1(m)\chi_{q+1}^{(m)}and using
(B.25) givesκq=‖xq+1(m)‖‖xq(m)‖=m+2​qm​(2​q+1),Cbm​χq+1(m)=m+2​qm​(2​q+1)​χq(m).\kappa_{q}=\frac{\left\lVert x_{q+1}^{(m)}\right\rVert}{\left\lVert x_{q}^{(m)}\right\rVert}=\sqrt{\frac{m+2q}{m(2q+1)}},\qquad C_{b_{m}}\chi_{q+1}^{(m)}=\sqrt{\frac{m+2q}{m(2q+1)}}\,\chi_{q}^{(m)}.(B.26)

For0≤q≤⌊N/2⌋0\leq q\leq\lfloor N/2\rfloor, define the normalized symmetric
tensorψq(m,N):=N!(N−2​q)!​(2​q)!​Πsym(N)​(e0⊗(N−2​q)⊗χq(m))∈SymN⁡ℋm.\psi_{q}^{(m,N)}:=\sqrt{\frac{N!}{(N-2q)!(2q)!}}\,\Pi_{\mathrm{sym}}^{(N)}\left(e_{0}^{\otimes(N-2q)}\otimes\chi_{q}^{(m)}\right)\in\operatorname{Sym}^{N}\mathcal{H}_{m}.(B.27)

These vectors are mutually orthogonal. Fix0≤q≤⌊(N−2)/2⌋0\leq q\leq\lfloor(N-2)/2\rfloorand putr=N−2​qr=N-2q. After
contracting eithere0⊗2e_{0}^{\otimes 2}fromψq(m,N)\psi_{q}^{(m,N)}orbmb_{m}fromψq+1(m,N)\psi_{q+1}^{(m,N)}, the normalized remaining tensor is the same;
denote it byθq(m,N)\theta_{q}^{(m,N)}. Directly from
(B.27) and
(B.26),(⟨e0⊗2|⊗I)ψq(m,N)\displaystyle(\langle e_{0}^{\otimes 2}\rvert\otimes I)\psi_{q}^{(m,N)}=r​(r−1)N​(N−1)​θq(m,N),\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{r(r-1)}{N(N-1)}}\,\theta_{q}^{(m,N)},(B.28)(⟨bm|⊗I)ψq+1(m,N)\displaystyle(\langle b_{m}\rvert\otimes I)\psi_{q+1}^{(m,N)}=2​(q+1)​(q+m/2)m​N​(N−1)​θq(m,N).\displaystyle=\frac{2\sqrt{(q+1)(q+m/2)}}{\sqrt{mN(N-1)}}\,\theta_{q}^{(m,N)}.(B.29)

Since the vectors in (B.27) are
symmetric, every summand in the normalized lift has the same
matrix element. Equations (B.28) and
(B.29) therefore give⟨ψq+1(m,N)|Am[N]|ψq(m,N)⟩=tq,m,N,\langle\psi_{q+1}^{(m,N)}\rvert A_{m}^{[N]}\lvert\psi_{q}^{(m,N)}\rangle=t_{q,m,N},(B.30)

wheretq,m,N:=2N​(N−1)​m​(q+1)​(q+m/2)​(N−2​q)​(N−2​q−1).t_{q,m,N}:=\frac{2}{N(N-1)\sqrt{m}}\sqrt{(q+1)(q+m/2)}\sqrt{(N-2q)(N-2q-1)}.(B.31)

The diagonal matrix elements vanish. Hence the bosonic stateρq,m,N:=12|ψq(m,N)+ψq+1(m,N)⟩⟨ψq(m,N)+ψq+1(m,N)|\rho_{q,m,N}:=\frac{1}{2}\lvert\psi_{q}^{(m,N)}+\psi_{q+1}^{(m,N)}\rangle\langle\psi_{q}^{(m,N)}+\psi_{q+1}^{(m,N)}\rvert

satisfiesTr⁡(Am​ρq,m,N(2))=tq,m,N\operatorname{Tr}(A_{m}\rho_{q,m,N}^{(2)})=t_{q,m,N}.

Now letτ∈SEPH(2)⁡(m+1)\tau\in\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(m+1). Hilbert–Schmidt
Cauchy–Schwarz, (B.22), andTr⁡(Am​τ)≤h​(Am)\operatorname{Tr}(A_{m}\tau)\leq h(A_{m})give‖ρq,m,N(2)−τ‖2≥Tr⁡(Am​ρq,m,N(2))−Tr⁡(Am​τ)2≥tq,m,N2−12​2​m.\left\lVert\rho_{q,m,N}^{(2)}-\tau\right\rVert_{2}\geq\frac{\operatorname{Tr}(A_{m}\rho_{q,m,N}^{(2)})-\operatorname{Tr}(A_{m}\tau)}{\sqrt{2}}\geq\frac{t_{q,m,N}}{\sqrt{2}}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2m}}.

Taking the infimum overτ\tauprovesDHHS​(BOSN(2)⁡(m+1),SEPH(2)⁡(m+1))≥tq,m,N2−12​2​m.D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(2)}(m+1),\,\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(m+1)\Bigr)\geq\frac{t_{q,m,N}}{\sqrt{2}}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2m}}.(B.32)

For fixedNNandqq,limm→∞tq,m,N2=(q+1)​(N−2​q)​(N−2​q−1)N​(N−1).\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{t_{q,m,N}}{\sqrt{2}}=\frac{\sqrt{(q+1)(N-2q)(N-2q-1)}}{N(N-1)}.

Since the second term in (B.32) tends to
zero, taking the supremum overd=m+1d=m+1gives, for every admissibleqq,supd≥2DHHS​(BOSN(2)⁡(d),SEPH(2)⁡(d))≥(q+1)​(N−2​q)​(N−2​q−1)N​(N−1).\sup_{d\geq 2}D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BOS}_{N}^{(2)}(d),\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(d)\Bigr)\geq\frac{\sqrt{(q+1)(N-2q)(N-2q-1)}}{N(N-1)}.

ForN≥6N\geq 6, chooseq=⌊N/6⌋q=\lfloor N/6\rfloor. Thenq+1≥N6,N−2​q≥2​N3,N−2​q−1≥N2.q+1\geq\frac{N}{6},\qquad N-2q\geq\frac{2N}{3},\qquad N-2q-1\geq\frac{N}{2}.

Substitution into the preceding display gives the claimedΩ​(N−1/2)\Omega(N^{-1/2})lower bound.
∎

## Theorem B.7(Hilbert–Schmidt lower bound for two-sided
Bose-symmetric extendible states).

Asn→∞n\to\infty,supd≥2DHHS​(BEXTn,n⁡(d,d),SEP⁡(d,d))=Ω​(n−1/2).\sup_{d\geq 2}D_{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{HS}}\Bigl(\operatorname{BEXT}_{n,n}(d,d),\,\operatorname{SEP}(d,d)\Bigr)=\Omega(n^{-1/2}).

## Proof.

Use the witnessAmA_{m}from
(B.21). Ifx=α​e0+vx=\alpha e_{0}+vandy=β​e0+wy=\beta e_{0}+w, then|⟨x⊗y|Am|x⊗y⟩|≤2m|α|∥v∥|β|∥w∥≤12​m.\left\lvert\langle x\otimes y\rvert A_{m}\lvert x\otimes y\rangle\right\rvert\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{m}}\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert\left\lVert v\right\rVert\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\left\lVert w\right\rVert\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}.

Equality holds forx=y=(e0+e1)/2x=y=(e_{0}+e_{1})/\sqrt{2}, and hencehSEP​(Am)=12​m=h​(Am).h_{\operatorname{SEP}}(A_{m})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{m}}=h(A_{m}).(B.33)

Setq=⌊n/3⌋q=\lfloor n/3\rfloorand take the bosonic2​n2n-site stateρq,m,2​n\rho_{q,m,2n}from the proof of TheoremB.6. Grouping
its first and lastnnsites gives a two-sided Bose-symmetric extension
whose cross-block marginal isρq,m,2​n(2)\rho_{q,m,2n}^{(2)}. The
Hilbert–Schmidt-normalized witnessAm/2A_{m}/\sqrt{2}and
(B.33) give the
finite-mmestimate (B.32), now withN=2​nN=2nand the separable set in place ofSEPH(2)⁡(m+1)\operatorname{SEP}_{\mathrm{H}}^{(2)}(m+1).
Withq=⌊n/3⌋q=\lfloor n/3\rfloor, the right-hand side tends, asm→∞m\to\infty, to at least1/(6​n)1/(6\sqrt{n})by the calculation in the
proof of TheoremB.6. For eachn≥3n\geq 3, choose a
sufficiently large finitemmand setdn=m+1d_{n}=m+1. The resulting
distance is at least1/(12​n)1/(12\sqrt{n}), and taking the supremum over
finite dimensions proves the claim.
∎
